
---
## IL-ARBITRATION-ACT.md
---

# Indian Consumer & Misc Law: Arbitration & Conciliation

## Purpose
Comprehensive guidance on Arbitration and Conciliation Act 1996 covering arbitration agreements, arbitral tribunal constitution, arbitral procedure, awards, enforcement, setting aside awards, international commercial arbitration, and mediation/conciliation.

## Activation Triggers
Use when: Commercial dispute with arbitration clause | Appointing arbitrator | Challenging arbitrator | Interim measures in arbitration | Arbitral award enforcement | Setting aside award | International arbitration (seat in India) | Conciliation/mediation | Emergency arbitrator | Court intervention in arbitration.

## Core Principles

### Section 7: Arbitration Agreement

**"Agreement to submit present/future disputes to arbitration"**

**Forms:**
1. **Arbitration Clause** - In main contract ("Disputes resolved by arbitration")
2. **Separate Agreement** - Standalone submission agreement

**Requirements:**
- **In Writing** (signed document OR exchange of letters/emails/fax)
- **Clear Intent** to arbitrate
- **Defined Disputes** (present OR future)

**Example Clause:**
```
"Any dispute arising out of or in connection with this contract shall be 
finally resolved by arbitration under Arbitration and Conciliation Act 1996. 
The seat of arbitration shall be New Delhi. The language shall be English. 
The arbitral tribunal shall consist of [one/three] arbitrator(s)."
```

**Validity:** Arbitration agreement survives termination of main contract (Section 16)

### Section 8: Power to Refer to Arbitration

**If arbitration agreement exists:**
- Party can apply to court to refer dispute to arbitration
- Court SHALL refer (mandatory - no discretion) if:
  - Valid arbitration agreement exists
  - Dispute arbitrable

**Timeline:** Apply BEFORE first statement on substance of dispute

**Effect:** Court proceedings stayed, matter referred to arbitration

---

## Arbitrability (What Can Be Arbitrated)

**Arbitrable Disputes (Section 2(3)):**
- Commercial disputes (contracts, partnerships, joint ventures)
- Civil disputes

**NON-Arbitrable:**
- Criminal offenses
- Matrimonial disputes (divorce, custody)
- Insolvency/winding up
- Disputes affecting third-party rights (tenancy eviction)
- Frauds (if complex - may require court investigation)

---

## Arbitral Tribunal Constitution

### Number of Arbitrators (Section 10)

**Parties' Choice:**
- 1 arbitrator (sole arbitrator) OR
- 3 arbitrators (usual for large disputes) OR
- Panel (odd number)

**Default:** 1 arbitrator (if parties do not agree)

### Appointment (Section 11)

**By Agreement:**
- Parties agree on appointment procedure

**If No Agreement:**

**Sole Arbitrator:**
- Parties jointly appoint OR
- If fail, application to court (High Court/Supreme Court)

**3 Arbitrators:**
- Each party appoints 1 arbitrator
- 2 arbitrators appoint 3rd (presiding arbitrator)
- If fail, court appoints

**Court Appointment:**
- High Court (for domestic arbitration seated in India)
- Supreme Court (for international commercial arbitration)
- Arbitral institution (if parties agree - e.g., MCIA, Delhi SIAC)

**Timeline:** Court appoints within 60 days

### Qualifications (Section 11(8) - for certain arbitrations)

**For disputes > Rs. 1 crore (if parties opt):**
- Experience as High Court Judge OR
- Senior advocate (15+ years) OR
- Specialized expertise in subject matter

### Challenge to Arbitrator (Section 12-13)

**Grounds:**
- **Bias/Partiality** - Justifiable doubts about independence/impartiality
- **Lack of Qualifications** - Does not meet agreed qualifications

**Disclosure:** Arbitrator must disclose any circumstances likely to give rise to doubts (Section 12)

**Procedure:**
- Challenge within 15 days of becoming aware
- If arbitrator does not withdraw → Arbitral tribunal decides
- If challenge rejected → Party can apply to court (within 30 days)

---

## Arbitral Procedure

### Seat vs Venue

**Seat:** Legal place of arbitration (determines applicable law, court jurisdiction)

**Venue:** Physical place of hearings (can be different from seat)

**Example:** Seat: New Delhi (Indian law applies); Venue: Mumbai (hearings conducted in Mumbai for convenience)

### Procedure (Section 18-27)

**Party Autonomy (Section 19):**
- Parties free to agree on procedure
- Subject to Part I of Act (mandatory provisions)

**Default Procedure:**
- Statement of Claim (Claimant)
- Statement of Defense (Respondent)
- Documentary evidence
- Oral hearings OR written submissions (tribunal's discretion)
- Witness examination

**Timeline:**
- Award within **12 MONTHS** from tribunal constitution (extendable by 6 months by consent OR court)

**Section 29A - Fast Track Arbitration:**
- If parties agree: 6-month timeline
- Sole arbitrator
- Documents-only (no oral hearings unless parties request)

### Interim Measures (Section 9, 17)

**Section 9 - Court's Power (Before/During Arbitration):**
- Interim injunction
- Appointment of receiver
- Preservation of subject matter
- Securing amount in dispute

**Section 17 - Tribunal's Power (After constitution):**
- Tribunal can order interim measures (same as court under Section 9)
- 2015 Amendment: Tribunal's interim orders enforceable like court orders

---

## Arbitral Award

### Types of Awards (Section 31)

**1. Final Award:** Deciding all issues

**2. Interim Award:** Partial award on some issues

**3. Award on Agreed Terms (Consent Award):** Parties settle, tribunal records settlement as award

### Contents (Section 31)

**Award Must:**
- Be in writing
- Signed by arbitrators (majority if 3 arbitrators)
- State reasons (unless parties waive)
- State date and place (seat)
- Be delivered to each party

### Costs (Section 31A - 2015 Amendment)

**Costs Follow the Event:**
- Loser pays winner's costs (unless unreasonable to do so)
- Costs = Arbitrator fees + legal fees + administrative costs

**Factors:**
- Conduct of parties
- Whether party's claim/defense was frivolous
- Whether party offered settlement

---

## Challenge to Award (Section 34)

**Setting Aside Award (Within 3 MONTHS + 30 days condonable):**

**Grounds (Narrow):**

**A. Party Proves (Section 34(2)(a)):**
- **Incapacity** of party
- **Invalid arbitration agreement**
- **Not given proper notice** or unable to present case
- **Award beyond scope** of submission to arbitration
- **Tribunal not constituted** as per agreement/Act

**B. Court Finds (Section 34(2)(b)):**
- **Subject matter not arbitrable** under Indian law
- **Award contrary to public policy** of India

**Public Policy (Section 34(2A) - Narrow):**
- Fraud/corruption
- Violation of fundamental policy of Indian law (not just legal error)
- Conflict with basic notions of morality/justice
- Patently illegal award (post-2015 amendment)

**NOT Grounds for Setting Aside:**
- Errors of fact (arbitrator's findings binding)
- Errors of law (unless patently illegal)
- Different interpretation

**Timeline:**
- Application within 3 months of award (extendable by 30 days)
- Court disposes within 1 year (2015 amendment - expedited)

---

## Enforcement of Award (Section 36)

**Domestic Awards:**

**After 3 Months (from Award):**
- If no setting aside application → Award enforceable like court decree
- Execute under CPC (attachment, sale)

**If Setting Aside Application Pending:**
- Court may stay enforcement (if prima facie case)
- But party must deposit entire award amount OR furnish security (2015 amendment)

**Foreign Awards (Section 44-60):**

**New York Convention (1958):**
- India signatory
- Foreign arbitral awards (from Convention countries) enforceable in India

**Procedure:**
- File application to enforce foreign award (in High Court)
- Court may refuse enforcement ONLY if grounds under Article V of Convention (similar to Section 34)

**Geneva Convention Awards (Section 53-60):**
- Older regime (pre-New York Convention)
- Rarely invoked

---

## International Commercial Arbitration (Section 2(1)(f), Part II)

**Definition:** Arbitration between parties having place of business in different countries

**Seat in India:**
- Part I applies (Indian Arbitration Act governs procedure)
- Indian courts have supervisory jurisdiction

**Seat Outside India:**
- Part II applies (limited court intervention)
- Only enforcement of foreign awards (not supervision)

**UNCITRAL Model Law:**
- Act based on UNCITRAL Model Law on International Commercial Arbitration
- Harmonized with international standards

---

## Conciliation (Part III - Section 61-81)

**Alternative to Arbitration:**

**Procedure:**
- Party sends written request to conciliate
- Other party accepts → Conciliation commenced
- Conciliator appointed (1 OR 2-3 conciliators)
- Conciliator assists parties to reach settlement (not binding)

**Conciliator's Role:**
- Neutral facilitator (not arbitrator)
- Hears both sides, suggests settlement terms
- Non-binding recommendations

**Settlement:**
- If settlement reached → Settlement agreement signed
- Enforceable like arbitral award (if parties request)

**Confidentiality:**
- Conciliation proceedings confidential
- Cannot be used as evidence in arbitration/court

**Termination:**
- Settlement reached OR
- Conciliator/party terminates (if settlement not possible) OR
- 180 days elapsed without settlement

---

## Institutional Arbitration

**Ad Hoc vs Institutional:**

**Ad Hoc:**
- Parties appoint arbitrators directly
- No administering institution
- Flexible but may face procedural issues

**Institutional:**
- Arbitration administered by institution (ICC, SIAC, MCIA, DIAC, LCIA)
- Institution appoints arbitrators (if parties don't agree)
- Institutional rules apply (unless modified)
- Administrative support, case management

**Indian Institutions:**
- **Mumbai Centre for International Arbitration (MCIA)**
- **Delhi International Arbitration Centre (DIAC)**
- **Arbitration centres under state laws**

**International Institutions:**
- **ICC (International Chamber of Commerce - Paris)**
- **SIAC (Singapore International Arbitration Centre)**
- **LCIA (London Court of International Arbitration)**
- **HKIAC (Hong Kong International Arbitration Centre)**

---

## Advantages of Arbitration vs Litigation

| Aspect | Arbitration | Litigation |
|--------|-------------|------------|
| **Confidentiality** | Private (confidential) | Public (open court) |
| **Party Autonomy** | Parties choose arbitrators, procedure, seat | Court, procedure statutory |
| **Expertise** | Technical experts as arbitrators | Generalist judges |
| **Speed** | Usually faster (12 months target) | Slow (3-10 years) |
| **Flexibility** | Flexible procedure | Rigid procedure (CPC) |
| **Finality** | Limited appeal (setting aside - narrow grounds) | Multiple appeals |
| **Enforceability** | International (New York Convention) | Domestic only |
| **Cost** | Arbitrator fees (high for large disputes) | Court fees (lower) |

---

## Common Scenarios

**Scenario 1: Appointing Arbitrator When Other Party Non-Cooperative**

**Facts:**
- Contract has arbitration clause: "Disputes resolved by 3 arbitrators"
- Dispute arises, Party A appoints arbitrator
- Party B refuses to appoint

**Remedy:**
- Party A applies to High Court under Section 11
- Court appoints arbitrator on behalf of Party B
- Timeline: 60 days

**Scenario 2: Award Challenged on Ground of Legal Error**

**Facts:**
- Arbitrator passes award
- Party claims arbitrator made error of law

**Analysis:**
- Error of law NOT ground for setting aside (unless patently illegal)
- Section 34 grounds narrow
- If interpretation reasonable (even if debatable) → Award upheld

**Likely Outcome:** Challenge dismissed

**Scenario 3: Emergency Arbitrator for Urgent Interim Relief**

**Facts:**
- Dispute arises, arbitration to be commenced
- Party needs urgent interim relief (prevent asset disposal)
- Tribunal not yet constituted

**Remedy:**
- Apply for emergency arbitrator (if institutional rules provide - SIAC, ICC)
- Emergency arbitrator appointed within 24-48 hours
- Passes interim order (valid until tribunal constituted)

**Alternative:** Court under Section 9

---

## Common Mistakes

**1. Poorly Drafted Arbitration Clause** → Disputes over seat, number of arbitrators, procedure

**2. Not Appointing Arbitrator Timely** → Delay (court appointment takes months)

**3. Not Raising Jurisdictional Objection Early (Section 16)** → Waived (must raise before statement on substance)

**4. Seeking Setting Aside for Legal Errors** → Narrow grounds (patently illegal standard high)

**5. Not Depositing Award Amount When Challenging** → Award enforceable despite challenge

**6. Not Choosing Institutional Arbitration** → Ad hoc difficulties (appointment, administration)

**7. Ignoring Fast Track Option** → Slower timeline (12 months vs 6 months)

**8. Not Agreeing on Costs Consequences** → Winner may not recover full costs

**9. Delay in Filing Enforcement Application** → Limitation may bar

**10. Not Specifying Seat Clearly** → Disputes over applicable law, court jurisdiction

---

**END OF PROTOCOL**

**Related Protocols:** IL-CONTRACT-LAW.md | IL-CPC-EXECUTION.md

**Legislation:** Arbitration and Conciliation Act 1996 (as amended 2015, 2019, 2021) | UNCITRAL Model Law | New York Convention 1958

**Version:** 1.0 | **Last Updated:** December 2025 | **Jurisdiction:** India


---
## IL-COMPETITION-ACT.md
---

# Indian Consumer & Misc Law: Competition Law (Anti-Trust)

## Purpose
Comprehensive guidance on Competition Act 2002 covering anti-competitive agreements (cartels, vertical restraints), abuse of dominant position, merger control (combinations), CCI procedures, leniency program, remedies, and competition compliance for businesses.

## Activation Triggers
Use when: Cartel investigation | Price-fixing allegations | Abuse of dominant position | Merger/acquisition (above thresholds) | Exclusive dealing arrangements | Resale price maintenance | Predatory pricing | Bid-rigging | Leniency application | CCI investigation response | Competition compliance audit.

## Core Principles

### Section 3: Anti-Competitive Agreements (PROHIBITED)

**Two Types:**

#### A. Horizontal Agreements (Section 3(3)) - PRESUMED to have AAEC (Adverse Effect on Competition)

**Between competitors at same level:**

**(a) Price-fixing** - Directly/indirectly determining purchase/sale prices

**Example:** 5 cement companies agree to sell cement at Rs. 400/bag (cartel) → Prohibited

**(b) Bid-rigging/Collusive Bidding** - Manipulating tender process

**Example:** 3 contractors agree not to bid against each other in government tenders → Prohibited

**(c) Output Limitation** - Limiting/controlling production/supply

**Example:** Steel manufacturers agree to reduce production to increase prices → Prohibited

**(d) Market Allocation** - Sharing markets by territory/customers

**Example:** 2 FMCG companies agree "Company A sells in North, Company B in South" → Prohibited

**PRESUMPTION:** These agreements presumed to have AAEC (burden on parties to prove otherwise)

#### B. Vertical Agreements (Section 3(4)) - Rule of Reason Analysis

**Between parties at different levels (manufacturer-distributor-retailer):**

**(a) Tie-in Arrangement** - Condition that buyer must purchase other goods

**Example:** Printer manufacturer requires buyers to purchase only its ink cartridges → May be prohibited (if AAEC)

**(b) Exclusive Supply** - Buyer must buy only from one supplier

**Example:** Retailer agrees to stock only Coca-Cola products (not Pepsi) → Analyzed under rule of reason

**(c) Exclusive Distribution** - Supplier sells only to one distributor in territory

**Example:** Car manufacturer appoints single dealer in city → Analyzed under rule of reason

**(d) Refusal to Deal** - Refusing to supply goods/services

**Example:** Dominant telecom company refuses interconnection to new entrant → May be prohibited

**(e) Resale Price Maintenance (RPM)** - Fixing resale price

**Example:** Manufacturer mandates retailers sell product at Rs. 1,000 (no discounting) → Prohibited if AAEC

**Test:** Does agreement cause AAEC? (Analyzed case-by-case, not presumed)

**AAEC Factors (Section 19(3)):**
- Creation of barriers to entry
- Denial of market access
- Foreclosure of competition
- Accrual of benefits to consumers
- Improvements in production/distribution

### Section 4: Abuse of Dominant Position (PROHIBITED)

**Dominant Position (Section 4 + Section 19(4)):**
- Position of strength enabling independent action
- Affecting competitors/consumers
- Market share >50% (rebuttable presumption of dominance)

**Abuse Examples:**

**(a) Unfair/Discriminatory Prices** - Predatory pricing, excessive pricing

**Example:** Telecom company prices below cost to eliminate competitors (predatory) → Prohibited

**(b) Limiting Production/Services** - Reducing output to increase prices

**(c) Denial of Market Access** - Refusing to deal with competitors

**Example:** Dominant OS manufacturer refuses to license to hardware makers → May be abuse

**(d) Leveraging** - Using dominance in one market to enter/protect position in another

**Example:** Dominant search engine ties its browser (leveraging search dominance to browser market) → May be abuse

**(e) Exclusive Supply/Refusal to Deal**

**Test:** Does conduct amount to abuse? (Dominance itself not illegal; abuse is)

**Relevant Market Definition (Section 2(r), (s), (t)):**
- **Product Market** - Substitutable products
- **Geographic Market** - Area where conditions of competition homogeneous
- **Relevant Market** = Product Market × Geographic Market

**Example:**
- Product market: Smartphones (not all mobile phones)
- Geographic market: India
- Relevant market: Smartphone market in India

---

## Section 5-6: Combinations (Merger Control)

**Combination = Merger, Amalgamation, Acquisition**

**Thresholds (Assets/Turnover - Section 5):**

**Domestic Threshold:**

**Assets:**
- Acquirer + Target combined assets in India > Rs. 2,000 crores OR
- Acquirer + Target combined assets worldwide > USD 1 billion (with India assets > Rs. 550 crores)

**Turnover:**
- Acquirer + Target combined turnover in India > Rs. 6,000 crores OR
- Acquirer + Target combined turnover worldwide > USD 3 billion (with India turnover > Rs. 1,650 crores)

**Group Assets/Turnover Aggregated**

**Exemptions (Section 6):**
- Acquisition of shares <25% (if no control)
- Intra-group transactions
- Banking/insurance sector consolidation (if approved by regulator)

**Notification:**
- **Within 30 days** of trigger event (execution of agreement OR Board approval)
- Form I (short form) OR Form II (detailed)
- Fee: Rs. 10-50 lakhs (depending on deal value)

**CCI Review:**
- Phase I: 30 working days (can approve OR refer to Phase II)
- Phase II: 180 days (detailed investigation)

**Approval:**
- Unconditional approval OR
- Conditional approval (with modifications/commitments) OR
- Disapproval (if AAEC)

**Gun-Jumping:** Implementing combination before CCI approval → Penalty (up to 1% of assets/turnover)

---

## CCI (Competition Commission of India) - Procedures

### Investigation (Section 26)

**Initiation:**
- **Information** filed by any person/consumer/association OR
- **Suo motu** (CCI's own motion) OR
- **Reference** by statutory authority

**Director General (DG) Investigation:**
- CCI refers matter to DG
- DG investigates (60-90 days, extendable)
- DG Report submitted to CCI

**Search and Seizure (Section 41):**
- DG can search premises
- Seize documents, computers

**Leniency/Settlement (Section 46):**
- Cartel member can apply for leniency (immunity/reduction in penalty)
- **First applicant:** 100% penalty waiver (if full cooperation)
- **Subsequent applicants:** Up to 50% reduction

**CCI Order (Section 27):**
- After DG report + hearing
- **If violation found:**
  - Cease and desist order
  - Penalty: Up to 10% of average turnover for 3 preceding years
  - Structural remedies (divestiture - rare)

**Appeal:**
- To NCLAT (National Company Law Appellate Tribunal) within 60 days
- Further appeal to Supreme Court

---

## Penalties

**Section 27 - Penalty for Anti-Competitive Agreements/Abuse:**
- **Enterprises:** Up to 10% of average turnover for 3 preceding years
- **Individuals (cartels):** Up to Rs. 1 crore OR 3 times profit (whichever higher)

**Section 43A - Penalty for Gun-Jumping (Combinations):**
- Up to 1% of total assets OR turnover (whichever higher)

**Section 42 - Penalty for Non-Compliance:**
- Failure to comply with CCI orders/DG investigation
- Rs. 1 crore OR imprisonment up to 3 years OR both

---

## Competition Compliance

### Compliance Program Elements:

1. **Policy:** Written competition law policy
2. **Training:** Regular training for employees (sales, procurement, pricing)
3. **Due Diligence:** Pre-M&A competition assessment
4. **Red Flags:** Price discussions with competitors, market allocation
5. **Reporting:** Whistleblower mechanism
6. **Monitoring:** Regular audits

### Do's and Don'ts:

**DON'T:**
- Discuss prices with competitors
- Participate in trade association meetings discussing prices/output
- Engage in bid-rigging
- Impose RPM without justification
- Abuse dominant position (if dominant)

**DO:**
- Compete independently
- Unilateral pricing decisions
- Notify combinations above thresholds
- Seek legal advice for vertical restraints
- Apply for leniency if cartel involvement discovered

---

## Key Cases

**Shamsher Kataria v. Honda Siel (2014):** RPM by Honda (fixed spare parts prices) → CCI found abuse of dominance

**CCI v. Coordination Committee of Artists (2017):** Film producers' association boycotted multiplex (collective refusal to deal) → Anti-competitive agreement

**Matrimony.com v. Google (2018):** Google alleged to abuse dominance in search market by favoring own services

---

## Common Mistakes

**1. Not Notifying Combination Above Thresholds** → Gun-jumping penalty

**2. Implementing Combination Before CCI Approval** → Violation, penalty

**3. Price Discussions with Competitors** → Cartel evidence (even informal)

**4. RPM Without Economic Justification** → Vertical restraint violation

**5. Not Applying for Leniency (If Cartel Participant)** → Miss immunity opportunity

---

**END OF PROTOCOL**

**Legislation:** Competition Act 2002 (as amended 2007, 2009, 2023) | Competition Commission of India (General) Regulations 2009 | Combination Regulations 2011

**Version:** 1.0 | **Last Updated:** December 2025 | **Jurisdiction:** India


---
## IL-CONSUMER-PROTECTION-ACT.md
---

# Indian Consumer & Misc Law: Consumer Protection

## Purpose
Comprehensive guidance on Consumer Protection Act 2019, covering consumer rights, unfair trade practices, defective goods/deficient services, product liability, e-commerce regulations, consumer dispute forums (District/State/National Commission), remedies, and complaint procedures.

## Activation Triggers
Use when: Defective product purchased | Deficient service received | Unfair trade practice (misleading advertisement, false representation) | Product liability claim (manufacturing defect, design defect) | E-commerce dispute | Consumer complaint filing | Forum jurisdiction determination | Product recall | Class action by consumers | Mediation/settlement in consumer disputes.

## Core Principles

### Section 2(7): "Consumer" Defined

**"Consumer means any person who:**
**(i) buys any goods for consideration (paid/promised/part-paid/deferred/any mode);**
**(ii) hires/avails any service for consideration;**

**Excludes person obtaining goods for resale OR commercial purpose"**

**Essential Elements:**
1. **GOODS/SERVICES** - Purchased/hired
2. **CONSIDERATION** - Paid (not free/gratuitous)
3. **NOT FOR RESALE** - For personal use (not commercial)

**Examples:**

**Consumer ✓:**
- Individual buying mobile phone for personal use
- Hiring cab service for personal travel
- Patient availing medical services

**NOT Consumer ✗:**
- Wholesaler buying goods for resale
- Company purchasing office equipment (commercial purpose)
- Free goods/services (no consideration)

**Important - Commercial Purpose Exclusion:**
- Laxmi Engineering v. PSG College (SC 1995): Goods for commercial purpose ≠ Consumer
- But if earning livelihood (sole source of income) → May be consumer (liberal interpretation)

### Section 2(42): "Service" Defined

**Broad Definition:** Any service made available to potential users (banking, insurance, transport, medical, education, etc.)

**Excludes:**
- Free services
- Service under contract of personal service (employment)

---

## Consumer Rights (Section 2(9))

**Six Fundamental Rights:**

1. **Right to Safety** - Protection against hazardous goods/services
2. **Right to Information** - Informed choice (quality, quantity, price, ingredients)
3. **Right to Choose** - Access to variety at competitive prices
4. **Right to be Heard** - Voice in consumer policy making
5. **Right to Redress** - Remedies for unfair trade practices, defective goods
6. **Right to Consumer Education** - Awareness of rights

---

## Unfair Trade Practices (Section 2(47))

**Prohibited Practices:**

**1. False/Misleading Representation:**
- Standard/quality/grade
- Ingredients/contents
- Sponsorship/approval
- Place of origin

**Example:** Labeling product "Made in Switzerland" when manufactured in India

**2. Misleading Advertisement:**
- Exaggerated claims
- False testimonials
- Bait advertising (advertise low price, refuse to sell)

**Example:** Ad claims "Cures cancer" (unsubstantiated medical claim)

**3. Bargain Sale (False Discount):**
- "50% off" when original price inflated

**4. Defective Goods Sold as New:**
- Refurbished sold as brand new

**5. Hoarding/Destruction to Raise Prices**

**Remedies:**
- Cease practice
- Refund
- Damages
- Penalty (up to Rs. 10 lakhs + Rs. 1 lakh per day for continuing offense)

---

## Defective Goods / Deficient Services (Section 2(10), (11))

**Defect (Section 2(10)):**
- Fault, imperfection, shortcoming in quality, quantity, purity, standard
- Goods not conforming to claims/representations

**Deficiency (Section 2(11)):**
- Shortfall in quality, nature, manner of performance of service
- Service not as per standard/contract

**Examples:**

**Defective Goods:**
- Mobile phone not working (manufacturing defect)
- Food item contaminated
- Medicine expired

**Deficient Services:**
- Flight delayed without justification
- Hospital negligence
- Bank charging unauthorized fees

**Remedies:**
- Replacement
- Refund
- Repair
- Compensation for loss/injury

---

## Product Liability (Section 82-87 - NEW in 2019 Act)

**Strict Liability Regime:**

**Section 84: Product Liability Action**

**When manufacturer/seller liable (NO FAULT required - strict liability):**
- Product has manufacturing defect OR
- Product design defective OR
- Deviation from manufacturing specifications OR
- Product not conforming to express warranty OR
- Failure to provide adequate instructions/warnings

**Defendants:**
- Product manufacturer
- Product service provider
- Product seller (in certain cases)

**Defenses (Section 85):**

1. **Compliance with Law** - Product conforms to legal standards
2. **No Defect** - Defect did not exist when sold
3. **Not Engaged in Business** - Occasional sale (not commercial)

**Damages (Section 87):**
- Compensatory damages (actual loss)
- Punitive damages (if malice/gross negligence)

**Example:**
- Pressure cooker explodes due to design defect → Manufacturer strictly liable (even if no negligence)

---

## E-Commerce Regulations (Section 2(16) + Rules 2020)

**E-Commerce Entity (Section 2(16)):**
- Online platform (marketplace OR inventory-based)

**Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules 2020:**

**Obligations of E-Commerce Entities:**

1. **Display Information:**
   - Legal name, address, contact
   - Terms of use, return/refund policy
   - Country of origin (products)
   - Grievance officer details

2. **No Misleading Ads/Unfair Trade Practices**

3. **Protect Consumer Data** - Privacy policy, consent for data use

4. **Cancellation/Return Rights:**
   - Clear return/refund/exchange policy

5. **Grievance Redressal:**
   - Appoint Grievance Officer
   - Acknowledge complaint within 48 hours
   - Redress within 1 month

**Flash Sales:**
- Ensure adequate inventory (no back-to-back cancellations)

**Marketplace vs Inventory Model:**

| Aspect | Marketplace | Inventory |
|--------|-------------|-----------|
| **Role** | Platform (connects sellers-buyers) | Seller (owns inventory) |
| **Liability** | Generally NOT liable for seller's products (unless unfair practices) | LIABLE as seller |
| **Example** | Amazon, Flipkart (marketplace) | Amazon (own brands), Myntra |

---

## Consumer Dispute Forums (Three-Tier System)

### District Consumer Disputes Redressal Commission (Section 34)

**Jurisdiction:**
- **Pecuniary:** Goods/services value up to Rs. 1 CRORE

**Composition:**
- President (District Judge OR qualified to be District Judge)
- 2-4 members

**Procedure:**
- File complaint (Form CPA-1)
- Fee: Nil (for complaints up to Rs. 5 lakhs) OR Rs. 200-5,000 (above Rs. 5 lakhs)
- Timeline: Dispose within 3 MONTHS (can extend to 5 months)

### State Consumer Disputes Redressal Commission (Section 45)

**Jurisdiction:**
- **Pecuniary:** Goods/services value > Rs. 1 crore, up to Rs. 10 CRORES
- **Appellate:** Appeals from District Commission

**Composition:**
- President (High Court Judge OR qualified to be HC Judge)
- 4-8 members

**Procedure:**
- Complaint OR Appeal (from District Commission order)
- Appeal: Within 30 days (extendable to 45 days)

### National Consumer Disputes Redressal Commission (Section 58)

**Jurisdiction:**
- **Pecuniary:** Goods/services value > Rs. 10 CRORES
- **Appellate:** Appeals from State Commission

**Composition:**
- President (Supreme Court Judge OR qualified to be SC Judge)
- 4+ members

**Procedure:**
- Complaint OR Appeal (from State Commission order)
- Appeal: Within 30 days (extendable to 45 days)

**Further Appeal:**
- To Supreme Court (within 30 days from National Commission order)

---

## Complaint Procedure

### Filing Complaint (Section 35)

**Who Can File:**
- Consumer OR
- Registered voluntary consumer association OR
- Central/State Government OR
- One or more consumers (class action) OR
- Legal heir/representative (if consumer deceased/incapacitated)

**Against Whom:**
- Manufacturer, seller, service provider

**Form:** CPA-1 (online OR offline)

**Mode:**
- E-filing (preferred - https://edaakhil.nic.in)
- Physical filing at Commission office

**Contents:**
1. Complainant details
2. Opposite party details (manufacturer, seller, trader)
3. Facts (when, where goods purchased/service availed)
4. Defect/deficiency details
5. Relief sought (refund, replacement, compensation)
6. Valuation (for jurisdiction)
7. Affidavit verifying complaint

**Documents:**
- Purchase invoice/bill
- Warranty card
- Correspondence with seller/manufacturer
- Medical reports (if injury)
- Expert opinion (if technical product)

**Limitation:**
- **2 YEARS** from cause of action (date of purchase/service/defect discovered)
- **Exception:** Condonable if sufficient cause (up to additional 2 years in State/National Commission)

### Admission and Notice (Section 37)

**Commission:**
- Admits complaint OR rejects (if not maintainable/frivolous)
- Sends notice to opposite party (20 days to file response)

**Opposite Party Response:**
- File written version (defense)
- 30 days from notice

### Evidence and Hearing

**Not Bound by CPC/Evidence Act:**
- Informal procedure (but principles of natural justice)
- Oral/documentary evidence
- Expert evidence (if required)

**Mediation (Section 37A - NEW):**
- Parties can opt for mediation
- Appointed mediator (from panel)
- Settlement within 60 days (extendable to 90 days)
- If settled → Order as per settlement
- If not settled → Proceed to hearing

### Order (Section 38)

**Remedies Commission Can Grant:**

1. **Remove Defect** - Repair goods

2. **Replace Goods** - With new goods of similar description (free from defect)

3. **Refund Price Paid** - Plus interest + costs

4. **Compensation** - For loss/injury suffered (physical, mental, financial)

5. **Punitive Damages** - If malicious/gross negligence

6. **Cease Unfair Trade Practice** - Discontinue practice

7. **Withdrawal of Hazardous Goods** - Not to offer for sale

8. **Corrective Advertisement** - At opposite party's cost

**Timeline:**
- Order within 3-5 months (District Commission)

**Non-Compliance:**
- Penalty: Imprisonment up to 1 year OR fine up to Rs. 10,000 OR both
- Execution as decree (CPC provisions apply)

---

## Class Action / Representative Complaints (Section 35(1)(a))

**One or More Consumers on Behalf of Class:**

**Conditions:**
1. Numerous consumers having same interest
2. Common question of law/fact
3. Representative adequate

**Example:**
- 1000 customers bought defective batch of product → 10 customers file on behalf of all 1000

**Benefits:**
- Efficiency (one proceeding instead of 1000)
- Access to justice (small individual claims)

**Procedure:**
- File complaint stating class action
- Commission gives wide publicity (notice to other class members)
- Class members can opt in/out

**Order:**
- Binds all class members (who did not opt out)

---

## Key Judgments and Principles

**Indian Medical Association v. V.P. Shantha (SC 1995):**
- Medical services are "service" under Consumer Protection Act
- Doctors/hospitals liable for medical negligence as service providers

**Lucknow Development Authority v. M.K. Gupta (SC 1994):**
- Housing construction service falls under Consumer Protection Act
- Delay in delivery/defects in construction = Deficiency in service

**Narmada Bachao Andolan v. Union of India (SC 2011):**
- Public interest litigation possible in consumer matters
- NGOs can file on behalf of affected consumers

---

## Common Scenarios

**Scenario 1: E-Commerce Purchase - Product Not Delivered**

**Facts:**
- Consumer orders phone from e-commerce site
- Payment made (Rs. 20,000)
- Product not delivered for 3 months
- E-commerce company not responding

**Remedy:**
1. File complaint with District Commission (value < Rs. 1 crore)
2. Against: E-commerce company (if inventory model) OR Seller (if marketplace) + E-commerce (if unfair practice)
3. Relief: Refund Rs. 20,000 + interest + compensation for mental agony

**Likely Outcome:**
- District Commission orders refund + interest (12% p.a.) + Rs. 10,000 compensation

**Scenario 2: Medical Negligence**

**Facts:**
- Patient undergoes surgery
- Doctor's negligence → Complications
- Patient suffers permanent disability

**Remedy:**
1. File complaint (valuation based on compensation sought)
2. Against: Doctor + Hospital
3. Relief: Compensation for medical expenses + loss of income + pain/suffering

**Expert Evidence:**
- Medical expert opinion (establish negligence, causation)

**Likely Outcome:**
- Compensation Rs. 10-50 lakhs (depending on injury severity, earning capacity)

**Scenario 3: Misleading Advertisement - False Claims**

**Facts:**
- Advertis

ement claims product cures disease
- No scientific basis
- Consumer purchases, no benefit

**Remedy:**
1. File complaint for unfair trade practice
2. Against: Manufacturer + Advertiser
3. Relief: Refund + compensation + penalty

**Regulatory Action:**
- CCPA (Central Consumer Protection Authority) can initiate suo motu proceedings
- Penalty on manufacturer

---

## Central Consumer Protection Authority (CCPA) - NEW (Section 10)

**Regulatory Body (Established 2020):**

**Powers:**
1. **Investigate** unfair trade practices, misleading ads
2. **Recall Products** (unsafe/hazardous)
3. **Refund** to consumers (if product withdrawn)
4. **Impose Penalty** (up to Rs. 10 lakhs + Rs. 1 lakh per day for continuing violation)
5. **File Class Action** on behalf of consumers

**Complaint to CCPA:**
- Any consumer OR consumer association
- CCPA investigates
- Penalty proceedings (separate from consumer dispute redressal)

---

## Common Mistakes

**1. Filing in Wrong Forum** → Dismissed for lack of pecuniary jurisdiction

**2. Filing Beyond Limitation** → Barred by time (2 years + condonation)

**3. Not Preserving Evidence** → Weak case (no invoice, no proof)

**4. Commercial Purchase Claimed as Consumer** → Not maintainable

**5. Not Attempting Settlement/Mediation** → Prolonged litigation

**6. Insufficient Compensation Claim** → Under-compensation

**7. Not Following Up on E-Commerce Grievance Officer** → Direct forum approach (should exhaust internal grievance)

**8. Not Joining Necessary Parties** → Manufacturer, seller, service provider (all may be liable)

**9. Not Providing Expert Evidence (Technical/Medical Cases)** → Difficult to prove defect/negligence

**10. Ignoring Execution** → Order passed but not enforced (follow up on compliance)

---

**END OF PROTOCOL**

**Related Protocols:**
- IL-CONTRACT-LAW.md
- IL-CPC-EXECUTION.md
- IL-TORT-LAW.md (when available)

**Legislation:**
- Consumer Protection Act 2019
- Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules 2020
- Consumer Protection Rules 2021

**Version:** 1.0  
**Last Updated:** December 2025  
**Jurisdiction:** India


---
## IL-ENVIRONMENTAL-LAW.md
---

# Indian Consumer & Misc Law: Environmental Law and Pollution Control

## Purpose
Comprehensive guidance on Indian environmental law framework covering Environmental Protection Act 1986, Pollution Control Acts (Air, Water, Noise), National Green Tribunal (NGT), environmental clearances, EIA requirements, pollution penalties, public interest litigation, and remedies for environmental violations.

## Activation Triggers
Use when: Industrial pollution (air/water/noise) | Environmental clearance required for project | NGT petition filing | River/groundwater contamination | Illegal construction in eco-sensitive zone | Forest clearance (diversion) | E-waste/hazardous waste disposal | Public interest environmental litigation | Violation of environmental norms | Sustainable development disputes.

## Core Principles

### Constitutional Foundation

**Article 21: Right to Life Includes Right to Healthy Environment**

**M.C. Mehta v. Union of India (1987):** "Right to clean environment is fundamental right under Article 21"

**Article 48A (DPSP):** State shall endeavor to protect and improve environment

**Article 51A(g) (Fundamental Duty):** Duty of every citizen to protect and improve natural environment

### Precautionary Principle

**"When activity raises threats of harm to environment/human health, precautionary measures taken even if cause-effect not fully established scientifically"**

**Vellore Citizens' Welfare Forum v. Union of India (SC 1996):**
- Precautionary principle = part of Indian environmental law
- Burden of proof on activity proponent (to prove NO harm)
- Applied when scientific uncertainty exists

### Polluter Pays Principle

**"Polluter must bear cost of pollution control measures and compensation for damage"**

**Indian Council for Enviro-Legal Action v. Union of India (1996):**
- Polluting industries liable to pay restoration cost
- Remediation of contaminated land
- Compensation to affected persons

**Application:**
- Pollution fines/penalties
- Remediation costs
- Compensation to victims

### Sustainable Development

**"Development that meets present needs without compromising future generations' ability to meet their needs"**

**Balancing Test:**
- Economic development vs environmental protection
- NOT absolute preservation (balanced approach)
- Environmental concerns integrated into development projects

---

## Environmental Protection Act 1986

### Section 3: Power to Take Measures for Environmental Protection

**Central Government can:**
1. Coordinate actions of State Governments, authorities
2. Plan and execute environmental programs
3. Lay down standards for emissions/discharges (air, water, soil)
4. Restrict areas for industries/operations
5. Lay down procedures/safeguards for hazardous substances

### Section 6: Rules for Standards

**Emission/Discharge Standards:**
- Air pollutants (PM10, PM2.5, SO2, NOx)
- Water pollutants (BOD, COD, heavy metals)
- Noise levels (residential, commercial, industrial zones)

**Notified Standards:**
- Environment (Protection) Rules 1986
- Hazardous Waste Management Rules 2016
- E-Waste Management Rules 2016
- Plastic Waste Management Rules 2016

### Section 15: Penalties

**Violation of EPA provisions:**
- Imprisonment: Up to 5 YEARS
- Fine: Up to Rs. 1 LAKH
- Continuing violation: Additional Rs. 5,000 per day

**Subsequent Offense:**
- Imprisonment: Up to 7 YEARS

**Section 17: Offences by Companies:**
- Directors, managers personally liable (if violation with consent/connivance)

---

## Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act 1974

### Section 24: Prohibition of Discharge Without Consent

**"No person shall discharge pollutants into water sources without consent from State Pollution Control Board"**

**Consent (Section 25-26):**
- **Consent to Establish** (before project setup)
- **Consent to Operate** (renewable periodically - 1-5 years)

**Application:**
- Details of effluent (quantity, quality, composition)
- Treatment methodology
- Discharge point

**Conditions:**
- Meet prescribed standards
- Install effluent treatment plant (ETP)
- Periodically monitor and report
- Zero Liquid Discharge (ZLD) for certain industries

### Section 41-42: Inspection and Samples

**Pollution Control Board Officers Can:**
- Enter premises (with notice)
- Inspect treatment plants
- Collect samples (water, effluent)
- Analyze in approved laboratories

### Section 43-45: Penalties

**Discharge without consent OR exceeding standards:**
- Imprisonment: Up to 6 YEARS
- Fine: Rs. 10,000-1,00,000
- Continuing violation: Rs. 5,000 per day

**Failure to Comply with Board Directions:**
- Imprisonment: Up to 3 months
- Fine: Rs. 10,000

**Closure Order (Section 33A):**
- Board can order closure of polluting industry
- Supreme Court/High Court can direct closure (writ jurisdiction)

---

## Air (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act 1981

### Section 21: Prohibition of Air Polluting Industries Without Consent

**Similar Structure to Water Act:**
- Consent to Establish
- Consent to Operate

**Air Pollutants:**
- Suspended Particulate Matter (SPM)
- PM10, PM2.5
- Sulfur dioxide (SO2)
- Nitrogen oxides (NOx)
- Carbon monoxide (CO)

### Section 22A: Power to Give Directions

**Central/State Government can:**
- Direct closure of industry
- Stop electricity/water supply
- Prohibit use of fuel

**Example - Delhi Air Quality:**
- GRAP (Graded Response Action Plan) implemented during winter
- Stage IV: Construction ban, truck entry ban, work-from-home advisories

### Section 37: Penalties

**Operating without consent OR exceeding standards:**
- Imprisonment: Up to 3 months to 5 YEARS (depending on severity)
- Fine: Rs. 10,000 to Rs. 1,00,000

---

## Noise Pollution (Regulation and Control) Rules 2000

### Ambient Noise Standards

**Decibel Limits (dB):**

| Area Type | Day (6 AM - 10 PM) | Night (10 PM - 6 AM) |
|-----------|-------------------|----------------------|
| Industrial | 75 dB | 70 dB |
| Commercial | 65 dB | 55 dB |
| Residential | 55 dB | 45 dB |
| Silence Zone | 50 dB | 40 dB |

**Silence Zones:** Within 100 meters of hospitals, educational institutions, courts

### Loudspeakers/Public Address Systems

**Permission Required:**
- From District Magistrate OR authorized officer
- Not permissible in silence zones
- Time restriction: 10 PM - 6 AM (no loudspeakers in public)

**Exceptions:**
- Emergency (police, fire, ambulance)

**Penalty:**
- Fine: Up to Rs. 1 lakh OR imprisonment OR both (under EPA Section 15)

---

## Environmental Clearance (EIA Notification 2006)

### EIA Requirement

**Environmental Impact Assessment Mandatory for:**
- Mining (>5 hectares)
- Thermal power plants (>25 MW)
- Highways/expressways
- Dams/hydro projects
- Ports/harbors
- Industrial estates (>50 hectares)
- Real estate (>20,000 sq.m. built-up area)
- Chemical/pharmaceutical/textile industries

### Category A vs Category B

**Category A (Central Level - MoEF&CC):**
- Larger projects (e.g., mining >50 hectares, power plants >500 MW)
- EAC (Expert Appraisal Committee) reviews
- Public hearing mandatory

**Category B (State Level - SEIAA):**
- Smaller projects
- Screened by SEAC (State EAC)
- Category B1: Public hearing required
- Category B2: No public hearing

### EIA Process

**Step 1: Screening (Category B only)**
- Determine if project requires EIA
- Categorize as B1 (EIA) or B2 (no EIA)

**Step 2: Scoping**
- Define terms of reference (ToR)
- Identify environmental concerns to be studied

**Step 3: EIA Study**
- Baseline data collection (air, water, soil, biodiversity)
- Impact prediction (construction phase, operation phase)
- Mitigation measures
- Environmental Management Plan (EMP)

**Step 4: Public Hearing**
- Local community consultation
- Concerns recorded
- Project proponent responds

**Step 5: Appraisal**
- EAC/SEAC examines EIA report + public hearing minutes
- Recommends approval/rejection/modifications

**Step 6: Decision**
- MoEF&CC/SEIAA grants/rejects Environmental Clearance
- Validity: Project-specific (usually tied to project completion + 5 years)

**Conditions:**
- Compliance with EMP
- Half-yearly monitoring reports
- Statutory clearances (forest, CRZ)

### Violation of EC Conditions

**Project Proponent:**
- Show-cause notice
- Suspension/revocation of EC
- Bank guarantee forfeiture
- Prosecution under EPA

---

## Forest (Conservation) Act 1980

### Section 2: Prior Approval for Forest Land Diversion

**"No forest land shall be diverted for non-forest purpose without Central Government approval"**

**Non-Forest Purpose:**
- Mining
- Infrastructure (roads, dams, transmission lines)
- Real estate

**Approval Process:**
1. State Government proposes diversion
2. Central Government (MoEF&CC) examines
3. Clearance granted with conditions:
   - Compensatory afforestation (2× area OR ₹NPV)
   - Net Present Value (NPV) payment
   - Wildlife clearance (if in wildlife area)

**NPV (Net Present Value of Forest):**
- Monetary value of ecosystem services lost
- Varies by state, forest type (Rs. 4-15 lakhs per hectare)

**Compensatory Afforestation:**
- Afforest double the diverted area
- OR pay cost of afforestation

**Penalty (Section 3A - Amendment 2023):**
- Fine: Up to Rs. 1 crore
- Restoration cost: On violator

---

## Coastal Regulation Zone (CRZ) Notification 2019

### CRZ Classification

**CRZ-I (Ecologically Sensitive):**
- Mangroves, coral reefs, sand dunes, national parks
- NO development (except eco-tourism, renewable energy)

**CRZ-II (Built-Up Areas):**
- Areas already developed up to or close to shoreline
- No-Development Zone (NDZ): 20 meters from HTL (High Tide Line)

**CRZ-III (Rural/Undeveloped):**
- NDZ: 50 meters from HTL (general)
- NDZ: 200 meters for ecologically sensitive areas

**CRZ-IV (Water Area and Tidal Influenced):**
- Water area up to territorial waters
- Tidal influenced water bodies

**Clearance Required:**
- From MoEF&CC for CRZ activities
- Violation: Demolition + penalty

---

## National Green Tribunal (NGT) Act 2010

### Section 14: Jurisdiction

**NGT has jurisdiction over:**
- All civil cases relating to environmental protection
- Substantial question relating to environment
- Arising under:
  - Water Act 1974
  - Air Act 1981
  - Environment Protection Act 1986
  - Forest Act 1980
  - Biological Diversity Act 2002
  - Public Liability Insurance Act 1991

**Excludes:**
- Wildlife Protection Act 1972 (High Court jurisdiction)

### Section 15: Powers

**NGT Can:**
- Grant relief, compensation
- Order restoration of environment
- Impose costs (polluter pays)
- Issue directions (cease pollution, close factory)

**Not Bound by CPC:**
- Simplified procedure
- Expedited hearings

### Section 16: Applications

**Who Can File:**
- Person affected by pollution
- NGO (not required to be registered)
- Central/State Government
- Local authority

**Against Whom:**
- Polluting industry
- Government authority (for inaction, granting illegal clearances)

**Limitation:** Within 6 MONTHS from knowledge of violation

**Forum:**
- NGT Principal Bench (Delhi)
- Regional Benches (Bhopal, Pune, Kolkata, Chennai)

### Section 17: Procedure

**Filing:**
- Application with facts, relief sought
- Documents, evidence
- Fee: Rs. 1,000

**Admission:**
- NGT admits OR rejects (if frivolous)

**Hearing:**
- Oral arguments, documentary evidence
- Site inspection (if required)

**Order:**
- Within 6 MONTHS (expedited)
- Compensation awarded
- Directions issued

**Execution:**
- Order executed as decree (CPC provisions apply)

### Section 22: Appeal

**Appeal to Supreme Court:**
- Within 90 days from NGT order
- On question of law

---

## Hazardous Waste Management

### Hazardous Waste Management Rules 2016

**Hazardous Waste Includes:**
- Chemical waste (acids, heavy metals, solvents)
- Pharmaceutical waste
- Waste from healthcare (biomedical waste - separate rules)
- E-waste (electronic waste - separate rules)

**Obligations:**
- **Generator (Occupier):** Store, transport, dispose through authorized agencies only
- **Treatment/Storage/Disposal Facility (TSDF):** Authorized by SPCB
- **Transporter:** Registered with SPCB

**Manifest System:**
- Document tracking waste from generation to disposal

**Penalty:**
- Unauthorized disposal: Imprisonment up to 5 years + fine

### E-Waste Management Rules 2016

**Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR):**
- Manufacturer/importer responsible for collection and recycling
- Targets: 70% collection by weight (7th year onward)

**Channelization:**
- E-waste collected through authorized recyclers only

**Penalty:**
- Violation of EPR: Compensation + prosecution under EPA

---

## Common Environmental Violations and Remedies

### Scenario 1: Factory Discharging Untreated Effluent into River

**Facts:**
- Textile factory
- No effluent treatment plant (ETP)
- Discharging untreated wastewater into river
- Fish mortality, contaminated drinking water

**Applicable Law:**
- Water Act Section 24-25 (no consent to discharge)
- EPA Section 7 (violation of effluent standards)

**Remedy:**

**Option 1: Complaint to SPCB**
- SPCB inspects, samples effluent
- Show-cause notice
- Closure directions + penalty

**Option 2: NGT Petition**
- File application before NGT
- Relief: Closure of factory, compensation to affected persons, restoration of river

**Option 3: Criminal Complaint**
- Under Water Act Section 43
- Imprisonment + fine

**Typical NGT Order:**
- Immediate closure until ETP installed
- Compensation: Rs. 50 lakhs-10 crores (depending on damage)
- Restoration plan (river rejuvenation)

### Scenario 2: Construction in CRZ Without Clearance

**Facts:**
- Hotel constructed 30 meters from High Tide Line
- CRZ-III area (NDZ 50 meters)
- No CRZ clearance obtained

**Violation:**
- CRZ Notification 2019
- EPA Section 5 (violation of notification)

**Remedy:**

**NGT Petition:**
- Application by local residents/NGO
- NGT orders:
  - Demolition of unauthorized construction
  - Restoration of coastal area
  - Penalty on builder/approving authority

**Example - T.N. Godavarman v. Union of India (ongoing):**
- Continuous monitoring by Supreme Court
- Demolitions ordered for CRZ violations

### Scenario 3: Illegal Mining Without Environmental Clearance

**Facts:**
- Mining lease granted by State Government
- No Environmental Clearance (EC) from MoEF&CC
- Forest land diverted without FC Act approval
- Mining > 5 hectares (requires EC)

**Violations:**
- EIA Notification 2006 (no EC)
- Forest Conservation Act 1980 (no approval)

**Remedy:**

**NGT Petition:**
- File application
- NGT orders:
  - Immediate suspension of mining
  - Penalty on lease holder
  - Restoration of mined area (reafforestation)

**Goa Foundation v. Sesa Sterlite (NGT/SC):**
- Illegal mining in Goa without renewals
- Mining suspended, penalties imposed

### Scenario 4: Air Pollution from Stubble Burning

**Facts:**
- Farmers burning crop residue (paddy stubble)
- Severe air pollution (PM2.5 > 500)
- Delhi air quality crisis

**Applicable Law:**
- Air Act Section 19 (prohibition on air pollution)
- EPA (violation of air quality standards)

**Remedy:**

**NGT Directions:**
- State governments directed to penalize farmers
- In-situ crop residue management incentives
- Enforcement of ban on stubble burning

**Environmental Compensation Charges (ECC):**
- Rs. 2,500 per acre (on violating farmers) - controversial implementation

### Scenario 5: Noise Pollution from Religious Place

**Facts:**
- Temple/mosque using loudspeakers beyond 10 PM
- Residential area (limit 45 dB at night)
- Measured 80 dB

**Violation:**
- Noise Pollution Rules 2000
- EPA Section 15

**Remedy:**

**Option 1: Complaint to Local Police/District Magistrate**
- Seizure of loudspeakers
- Fine

**Option 2: NGT Petition**
- Directions to comply with noise limits
- Penalty

**Church of God (Full Gospel) v. K.K.R. Majestic Colony (SC 2000):**
- Fundamental right to worship does NOT include right to use loudspeakers
- Noise pollution violates Article 21 (right to silence)

---

## Public Interest Litigation (PIL) for Environment

### Article 32 (SC) and Article 226 (HC): Writ Jurisdiction

**Locus Standi Relaxed:**
- Any concerned citizen can file PIL
- NGOs (environmental groups) frequently file
- Not necessary to be personally affected

**Landmark Environmental PILs:**

**1. M.C. Mehta v. Union of India (Oleum Gas Leak - 1987):**
- Absolute liability rule for hazardous industries
- No defenses (not even Act of God)

**2. M.C. Mehta v. Union of India (Taj Trapezium - 1996):**
- 292 polluting industries around Taj Mahal ordered to close/relocate
- Use CNG instead of coal/coke

**3. M.C. Mehta v. Kamal Nath (Span Resorts - 1997):**
- Public Trust Doctrine applied
- Rivers, forests, seashores held in trust for public
- Government cannot alienate to private parties

**4. Vellore Citizens' Welfare Forum v. Union of India (1996):**
- Tanneries in Tamil Nadu polluting river
- Polluter pays principle, precautionary principle applied
- Industries ordered to pay compensation + close

**5. T.N. Godavarman Thirumulpad v. Union of India (1997-ongoing):**
- Forest conservation monitoring
- Nationwide ban on tree felling (until sustainable forest management)
- CEC (Central Empowered Committee) appointed to monitor

---

## Key Environmental Principles from Case Law

### 1. Absolute Liability (M.C. Mehta v. Union of India - 1987)

**Rule:**
- Enterprise engaged in hazardous/inherently dangerous activity
- Strictly and absolutely liable for harm
- No defenses (not even Act of God, third-party negligence)
- Liability proportionate to magnitude of enterprise

**Application:**
- Chemical plants, nuclear facilities, gas storage

### 2. Public Trust Doctrine (M.C. Mehta v. Kamal Nath - 1997)

**Rule:**
- Natural resources (air, water, forests, seashores) held by State in trust for public
- State as trustee cannot alienate to private parties
- State owes duty to protect for present and future generations

**Application:**
- Government cannot lease forests, rivers to private parties without ensuring public use

### 3. Intergenerational Equity (Vellore Citizens - 1996)

**Rule:**
- Present generation obligated to preserve environment for future generations
- Sustainable use (not depletion)

### 4. Doctrine of Proportionality

**Balancing Test:**
- Environmental protection vs economic development
- Neither absolute (balanced approach)
- Development permitted if:
  - Mitigation measures adequate
  - Compensatory measures (afforestation, payment)
  - Benefits outweigh environmental costs

---

## Common Mistakes

**1. Starting Project Without Environmental Clearance** → Violation, project halted, penalties

**2. Not Maintaining Consent Validity (Water/Air Act)** → Illegal operation, closure risk

**3. Not Installing Pollution Control Equipment** → Exceeding standards, penalties

**4. Diverting Forest Land Without FC Act Approval** → Illegal diversion, restoration cost

**5. Construction in CRZ/Eco-Sensitive Zone Without Clearance** → Demolition risk

**6. Not Filing NGT Application Within 6 Months** → Barred by limitation

**7. Ignoring SPCB Directions/Show-Cause Notices** → Deemed admission, closure orders

**8. Not Conducting EIA Study Properly** → EC challenge, cancellation

**9. Not Complying with EC Conditions (Post-Approval)** → EC revocation, bank guarantee forfeiture

**10. Assuming Pollution Fines are One-Time Cost** → Continuing daily penalties add up significantly

---

**END OF PROTOCOL**

**Related Protocols:**
- IL-PIL-ENVIRONMENTAL (India civic law - environmental litigation)
- IL-CONSTITUTIONAL-REMEDIES.md (Article 32, 226 writs for environmental violations)
- IL-CONSUMER-PROTECTION-ACT.md (Product liability for environmental harm)

**Key Legislation:**
- Environment (Protection) Act 1986
- Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act 1974
- Air (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act 1981
- Forest (Conservation) Act 1980
- National Green Tribunal Act 2010
- Wildlife Protection Act 1972
- Biological Diversity Act 2002
- EIA Notification 2006
- CRZ Notification 2019

**Regulatory Bodies:**
- Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEF&CC)
- Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB)
- State Pollution Control Boards (SPCBs)
- National Green Tribunal (NGT)
- Expert Appraisal Committees (EAC)

**Version:** 1.0  
**Last Updated:** December 2025  
**Jurisdiction:** India


---
## IL-IT-ACT.md
---

# Indian Consumer & Misc Law: Information Technology Act & Cyber Law

## Purpose
Comprehensive guidance on IT Act 2000 covering digital signatures, electronic records, cyber crimes (hacking, identity theft, data breach), intermediary liability, data protection, cybersecurity obligations, and remedies for cyber offenses.

## Activation Triggers
Use when: Cyber crime (hacking, phishing, identity theft) | Data breach | Unauthorized access to computer system | Website defacement | Online fraud | Intermediary liability (ISP, social media) | Digital signature validity | E-commerce dispute | Data protection compliance | Cybersecurity incident | Privacy violation | Revenge porn/morphed images.

## Core Principles

### Section 4: Legal Recognition of Electronic Records

**Electronic records = Paper records** (legal equivalence)

**"Information shall not be denied legal effect solely on ground it is in electronic form"**

**Application:**
- Contracts (e-commerce, online agreements)
- Government filings (e-filing of taxes, forms)
- Banking (online transactions)

**Exception - Documents Requiring Physical Presence:**
- Negotiable instruments (cheques - but moving to electronic)
- Powers of attorney (some states)
- Wills, trusts
- Sale/transfer of immovable property (registration required)

### Section 5: Digital Signatures

**Digital Signature = Electronic authentication**

**Valid if:**
- Affixed using asymmetric crypto system + hash function
- Issued by Certifying Authority (licensed by CCA - Controller of Certifying Authorities)

**Legal Effect:** Deemed authenticated (Section 3A Evidence Act)

**Use Cases:**
- E-filing (income tax, company filings)
- E-tendering (government procurement)
- Electronic contracts

**Certifying Authority:** Licensed by CCA (government body under IT Act)

---

## Cyber Crimes (Section 43-74)

### Section 43: Unauthorized Access/Damage (Civil Penalty)

**Acts Constituting Offense (Damages up to Rs. 1 crore):**

**(a) Unauthorized Access** - Accessing computer/network without permission

**(b) Downloading/Copying Data** - Without authorization

**(c) Introducing Virus** - Computer contaminant

**(d) Damaging Computer System** - Destroying/deleting/altering data

**(e) Denial of Service** - Disrupting computer access

**(f) Facilitating Unauthorized Access** - Providing means (password, security code)

**Remedy:** Compensation up to Rs. 1 crore (adjudication by Adjudicating Officer)

### Section 66: Hacking (Criminal Offense)

**"Whoever with intent to cause wrongful loss/gain, destroys/deletes/alters information, or diminishes value/utility, or affects injuriously"**

**Punishment:** Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine up to Rs. 5 lakhs

**Example:** Hacker accesses company server, deletes database → Section 66

### Section 66A: Offensive Messages (STRUCK DOWN by Supreme Court 2015)

**Was:** Sending offensive/menacing messages via communication service

**Supreme Court (Shreya Singhal v. Union of India):** Unconstitutional (violates free speech - Article 19(1)(a))

**Now:** Section 66A NOT enforceable

### Section 66B: Dishonestly Receiving Stolen Computer Resource

**Punishment:** Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine up to Rs. 1 lakh

**Example:** Knowingly receiving stolen credit card data

### Section 66C: Identity Theft

**"Using electronic signature/password/unique identification of another person fraudulently/dishonestly"**

**Punishment:** Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine up to Rs. 1 lakh

**Example:** Using someone's Aadhaar/PAN for fraudulent transactions

### Section 66D: Cheating by Personation Using Computer Resource

**"Cheating by personation by using computer resource"**

**Punishment:** Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine up to Rs. 1 lakh

**Example:** Creating fake profile impersonating person, defrauding others

### Section 66E: Privacy Violation (Publishing Private Images)

**"Capturing/publishing/transmitting image of private area without consent"**

**Punishment:** Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine up to Rs. 2 lakhs (First offense) | Up to 5 years + Fine up to Rs. 5 lakhs (Subsequent offense)

**Application:** Revenge porn, morphed images

**Private Area:** Genitals, pubic area, buttocks, breasts (female)

### Section 66F: Cyber Terrorism

**"Threatens unity, integrity, security, sovereignty of India by denying access to computer resource"**

**Punishment:** Imprisonment up to life

**Example:** Hacking critical infrastructure (power grid, banks, defense systems)

### Section 67: Publishing Obscene Material

**Publishing/transmitting obscene material in electronic form**

**Punishment:** Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine up to Rs. 5 lakhs (First offense) | Up to 5 years + Fine up to Rs. 10 lakhs (Subsequent offense)

### Section 67A: Sexually Explicit Material

**Publishing sexually explicit acts/conduct**

**Punishment:** Imprisonment up to 5 years + Fine up to Rs. 10 lakhs (First offense) | Up to 7 years + Fine up to Rs. 10 lakhs (Subsequent offense)

### Section 67B: Child Pornography

**Publishing/transmitting material depicting children in sexually explicit act**

**Punishment:** Imprisonment up to 5 years + Fine up to Rs. 10 lakhs (First offense) | Up to 7 years + Fine up to Rs. 10 lakhs (Subsequent offense)

**Also:** POCSO Act 2012 (stricter punishment - up to life imprisonment)

---

## Intermediary Liability (Section 79 + IT Rules 2021)

### Section 79: Safe Harbor for Intermediaries

**Intermediary:** Person providing telecom/internet service (ISP, hosting, search engine, social media)

**Safe Harbor (NO liability) if:**
1. Function = Intermediary (not originating/modifying information)
2. No actual knowledge of illegal content OR
3. On obtaining knowledge, expeditiously removes/disables access
4. Observes due diligence (IT Rules 2021)

**Loses Safe Harbor if:**
- Actual knowledge of illegal content + fails to remove
- Fails to comply with due diligence (IT Rules)

### IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules 2021

**Due Diligence Obligations:**

**All Intermediaries:**
1. **Terms of Use** - Prohibit hosting illegal content
2. **Privacy Policy** - Data collection, usage
3. **Grievance Officer** - Appoint within India, acknowledge complaint (24 hours), resolve (15 days)
4. **Takedown** - Remove illegal content within 24 hours of court order/government notification

**Significant Social Media Intermediaries (Users > 50 lakhs):**
1. **Chief Compliance Officer** - Ensure compliance
2. **Nodal Contact Person** - 24/7 contact for law enforcement
3. **Resident Grievance Officer** - Based in India
4. **Monthly Report** - Proactive monitoring, content removal
5. **Traceability** - Enable identification of first originator (encrypted messaging - controversial)

**Illegal Content (Must Not Host):**
- Violates law (defamation, obscenity, child porn, IP infringement)
- Threatens unity/integrity of India
- Incites violence
- Invades privacy

**Content Moderation:**
- User complaints → Intermediary reviews → Remove/retain (with reasons)
- Automated tools + human moderation

**Criticism:** Concerns over censorship, privacy (traceability of encrypted messages)

---

## Data Protection (No Comprehensive Law Yet - Bills Pending)

**Current Framework:**

**IT Act Section 43A + IT (Reasonable Security Practices) Rules 2011:**

**"Body corporate possessing sensitive personal data must implement reasonable security practices"**

**Sensitive Personal Data:**
- Passwords
- Financial information (bank, credit card)
- Health records
- Biometric information
- Sexual orientation

**Reasonable Security Practices:**
- IS/ISO/IEC 27001 certification OR
- Comprehensive documented information security program

**Data Breach:**
- If breach due to negligence → Compensation (up to Rs. 5 crores)

**Consent Required:**
- Collecting sensitive personal data requires consent
- Purpose limitation (collect only for stated purpose)

**Future:** Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 (passed but not yet notified - will replace current framework)

---

## Cybersecurity (Section 70B + CERT-In)

**CERT-In (Indian Computer Emergency Response Team):**
- National nodal agency for cybersecurity incidents
- Collects, analyzes, disseminates info on cyber incidents

**Cybersecurity Directions (Section 70B(6)):**

**Mandatory Reporting (CERT-In Directions 2022):**
- Service providers, intermediaries, data centers must report cyber incidents to CERT-In within 6 HOURS
- Incidents: Data breach, unauthorized access, malware, DDoS

**Log Maintenance:**
- ICT systems must maintain logs for 180 days (for investigation)

**VPN/Cloud Service Providers:**
- Maintain customer data (name, address, email, IP, purpose) for 5 years (controversial - privacy concerns)

---

## Adjudication and Appeals

### Adjudicating Officer (Section 46)

**Appointed by Central/State Government**

**Powers:**
- Adjudicate claims under Section 43 (unauthorized access, damage)
- Compensation up to Rs. 1 crore

**Procedure:**
- Complaint filed
- Notice to respondent
- Hearing (oral/written submissions)
- Order (within 6 months)

**Appeal:** To Cyber Appellate Tribunal (within 45 days)

### Cyber Appellate Tribunal (Section 48)

**Appeals from:**
- Adjudicating Officer orders
- Controller of Certifying Authorities orders

**Further Appeal:** To High Court (within 60 days)

---

## Investigation (Section 78-81)

**Police Investigation:**
- Cyber crimes cognizable (police can arrest without warrant)
- Specialized cyber crime cells in states

**Section 78: Power to Investigate**
- Police Officer (rank Inspector and above) can investigate

**Section 80: Power of Police to Enter/Search**
- With warrant from Magistrate
- Seize computers, devices, evidence

**Jurisdiction (Section 75):**
- Offense committed anywhere (if victim/accused in India)
- Extraterritorial application

---

## Common Cyber Crimes - Remedies

**Phishing/Online Fraud:**
- Sections: 66C (identity theft), 66D (cheating by personation), 420 IPC (cheating)
- Report to cyber crime portal (https://cybercrime.gov.in)
- File FIR with cyber cell

**Hacking:**
- Section 66 (hacking), Section 43 (unauthorized access)
- Civil compensation + Criminal prosecution

**Data Breach:**
- Section 43A (compensation if negligence)
- Report to CERT-In (within 6 hours)

**Defamation Online:**
- Section 499/500 IPC (defamation - criminal)
- Civil defamation suit
- Section 79 notice to intermediary (takedown)

**Revenge Porn/Morphed Images:**
- Section 66E (privacy violation), Section 67/67A (obscene material)
- Section 354C IPC (voyeurism), Section 509 IPC (insult to modesty)
- File FIR, seek takedown from platform

---

## Common Mistakes

**1. Not Reporting Cyber Crime Timely** → Evidence lost (digital evidence volatile)

**2. Tampering with Evidence** → Destroying device/data (obstruction of justice)

**3. Not Preserving Digital Evidence** → Screenshots, emails, URLs (with timestamp)

**4. Assuming Anonymity Online** → IP addresses traceable, digital footprint

**5. Not Complying with CERT-In Reporting** → Penalty, regulatory action

**6. Intermediaries Not Appointing Grievance Officer** → Lose safe harbor, liability

**7. Not Implementing Data Security (Section 43A)** → Compensation liability for breach

**8. Not Obtaining Consent for Sensitive Data** → Violation of IT Rules

**9. Delay in Takedown (Intermediaries)** → Liability for illegal content

**10. Not Updating Cybersecurity Measures** → Vulnerable to attacks, compliance gaps

---

**END OF PROTOCOL**

**Related Protocols:** IL-CONSUMER-PROTECTION-ACT.md | IL-COPYRIGHT-ACT.md (ISP safe harbor overlap) | IPC Cyber Crimes

**Legislation:** IT Act 2000 (as amended 2008) | IT Rules 2011 (Reasonable Security Practices) | IT Rules 2021 (Intermediary Guidelines) | CERT-In Directions 2022 | Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 (pending notification)

**Version:** 1.0 | **Last Updated:** December 2025 | **Jurisdiction:** India


---
## IL-LABOUR-LAW.md
---

# Indian Consumer & Misc Law: Labour Law and Industrial Relations

## Purpose
Comprehensive guidance on Indian labour law covering Industrial Disputes Act 1947, termination/retrenchment, layoff, closure, strikes/lockouts, labour courts/tribunals, Payment of Wages Act, Provident Fund (PF), Employees' State Insurance (ESI), Gratuity, maternity benefits, contract labour, and remedies for wrongful termination.

## Activation Triggers
Use when: Wrongful termination/dismissal | Retrenchment without compensation | Unpaid wages/salary | Illegal strike/lockout | Industrial dispute (workers vs management) | Labour court reference | Contract labour exploitation | PF/ESI non-compliance | Gratuity denial | Maternity leave denial | Standing orders violation | Domestic enquiry flaws | Unfair labour practice.

## Core Principles

### Constitutional Foundation

**Article 14:** Equality before law (no arbitrary termination)

**Article 16:** Equality of opportunity in employment

**Article 19(1)(c):** Right to form associations/unions

**Article 21:** Right to livelihood (cannot be deprived arbitrarily)

**Article 23-24:** Prohibition of forced labour, child labour

**Article 39(d), 42, 43:** DPSP - living wage, humane working conditions, worker participation

### Industrial Jurisprudence Principles

**1. Social Justice:**
- Labour laws are beneficial legislation
- Interpretation in favor of workers (liberal construction)

**2. Master-Servant Relationship:**
- Employer has control over employee
- Employee economically dependent
- Protection needed against arbitrary employer actions

**3. Industrial Peace:**
- Minimize disputes, maximize production
- Collective bargaining, conciliation, adjudication

---

## Industrial Disputes Act 1947

### Section 2(k): "Industrial Dispute" Defined

**"Dispute between employers and workmen OR between workmen and workmen, connected with employment/non-employment/terms of employment/conditions of labour"**

**Essential Elements:**
1. Dispute (not individual grievance - must be raised by union OR substantial body of workers)
2. Between employer and workmen (OR workmen inter se)
3. Connected with employment matters

**Individual Dispute vs Industrial Dispute:**

| Individual Dispute | Industrial Dispute |
|--------------------|-------------------|
| Single worker's complaint | Espoused by union OR substantial number of workers |
| Civil suit jurisdiction | Labour Court/Tribunal jurisdiction |
| Example: One worker's termination | Example: Union takes up termination, OR mass retrenchment |

**Bangalore Water Supply v. Rajappa (SC 1978):**
- Broad definition of "industry" (any systematic activity by cooperation between employer and employees)
- Includes cooperatives, societies, hospitals, educational institutions (if commercial element)

### Section 2(oo): "Workman" Defined

**Includes:**
- Skilled, unskilled, manual, operational, clerical workers
- Earning up to Rs. 10,000/month (as of 2017 - subject to periodic revision)

**Excludes:**
- Managerial/supervisory employees drawing > Rs. 10,000/month
- Employees in administrative capacity
- Armed forces, police

**Test:** Nature of work (skilled/unskilled manual/clerical) + wage threshold

---

## Termination of Employment

### Types of Termination

**1. Dismissal (Misconduct):**
- For proven misconduct (theft, insubordination, absenteeism, fraud)
- After domestic enquiry (principles of natural justice)
- No compensation

**2. Retrenchment (Section 2(oo)):**
- Termination for reasons other than misconduct (redundancy, efficiency, cost-cutting)
- Compensation mandatory (Section 25F)

**3. Layoff (Section 2(kkk)):**
- Temporary suspension of employment (not permanent termination)
- Due to shortage of coal, power, raw materials, breakdown, accumulation of stocks

**4. Closure (Section 25FFA-25FFC):**
- Permanent shutdown of establishment

**5. Voluntary Retirement/Resignation:**
- By employee's own will
- No compensation (unless VRS scheme)

### Section 25F: Conditions for Valid Retrenchment

**Three Conditions (ALL mandatory):**

**1. One Month Notice OR Notice Pay**
- Notice in writing
- OR payment equivalent to 1 month wages

**2. Retrenchment Compensation:**
- Formula: 15 DAYS average pay for each completed year of service
- OR as per standing orders/collective agreement (if higher)

**Example:**
- Service: 10 years
- Average pay: Rs. 30,000/month (Rs. 1,000/day)
- Compensation: 15 days × 10 years = 150 days' wages = Rs. 1,50,000

**3. Notice to Appropriate Government:**
- For establishments with >100 workers (Section 25N)
- Prior approval required (before retrenchment)

**Non-Compliance:**
- Retrenchment VOID
- Remedy: Reinstatement with back wages

### Section 25G-25H: Layoff Compensation

**If layoff > 45 days in year:**
- Compensation @ 50% of basic wages + DA
- For period of layoff

**Conditions:**
- Worker not on leave/strike
- Available for work

### Section 25O: Last Come, First Go (LIFO)

**Retrenchment Principle:**
- Last hired, first retrenched (seniority-based)

**Exception:** If agreement with union specifies different criteria

---

## Misconduct and Domestic Enquiry

### Standing Orders (Industrial Employment Standing Orders Act 1946)

**For establishments with >100 workers:**
- Must certify standing orders with Labour Commissioner
- Define:
  - Misconduct categories
  - Disciplinary procedure
  - Termination grounds

**Model Standing Orders (if not certified):**
- Default rules apply

### Misconduct Categories

**Major Misconduct (Dismissal):**
- Theft, fraud, embezzlement
- Wilful insubordination
- Habitual absence without leave
- Assault, violence
- Intoxication at work

**Minor Misconduct (Warning/Suspension):**
- Late attendance
- Minor negligence
- Discourteous behavior

### Domestic Enquiry Procedure (Principles of Natural Justice)

**Requirements (ALL mandatory):**

**1. Charge Sheet:**
- Specific allegations with dates, facts
- Not vague (must enable defense)

**2. Suspension Pending Enquiry (Optional):**
- If employee's presence prejudicial
- Subsistence allowance (50% wages) during suspension

**3. Enquiry Notice:**
- Date, time, venue
- Reasonable time to prepare defense (minimum 7 days)

**4. Right to Defense:**
- Inspect documents
- Cross-examine witnesses
- Lead defense evidence
- Engage representative (coworker/union representative - not advocate)

**5. Enquiry Officer (Unbiased):**
- Not complainant
- Independent assessment

**6. Enquiry Report:**
- Findings on each charge
- Evidence considered

**7. Second Show-Cause Notice:**
- On proposed punishment
- Opportunity to submit mitigation

**8. Final Order:**
- Reasoned order (dismissal/suspension/warning)

**Procedural Flaws = Bad Domestic Enquiry:**
- No opportunity to defend
- Denial of cross-examination
- Biased enquiry officer
- No charge sheet/vague charges

**Remedy:**
- Labour Court/Tribunal can reinstate if enquiry flawed (even if misconduct substantive)

### Quantum of Proof (Domestic Enquiry)

**Preponderance of Probabilities (Not Beyond Reasonable Doubt):**
- Standard lower than criminal trial
- Substantial evidence sufficient

---

## Labour Courts and Tribunals (Section 7-7A)

### Three-Tier Adjudication

**1. Conciliation Officer (Section 4):**
- Appointed by Government
- Attempts settlement before adjudication
- If settlement fails → Matter referred to Court/Tribunal

**2. Labour Court (Section 7):**
- One presiding officer (judicial background)
- Jurisdiction: Schedule II matters
  - Propriety/legality of employer's orders (termination, transfer)
  - Application/interpretation of standing orders
  - Illegal strikes/lockouts
  - Individual disputes

**3. Industrial Tribunal (Section 7A):**
- One presiding officer (judicial background)
- Jurisdiction: Schedule III matters (larger disputes)
  - Wages, hours of work
  - Bonus, profit-sharing
  - Retrenchment, closure, layoff
  - Collective bargaining agreements

**National Tribunal (Section 7B):**
- For disputes of national importance
- Appointed by Central Government

### Section 10: Reference to Labour Court/Tribunal

**By Appropriate Government (Central/State):**
- After conciliation fails
- Dispute referred for adjudication
- Compulsory (if government refers)

**Application by Workman (Section 10A):**
- Individual workman can apply directly (since 2010 amendment)
- If dispute individual and not espoused by union

### Procedure Before Labour Court (Similar to Civil Court)

**Pleadings:**
- Statement of claim (workman)
- Written statement (employer)

**Evidence:**
- Documentary (charge sheet, enquiry report, standing orders, wage slips)
- Oral (witnesses)

**Arguments:**
- Parties/counsel

**Award (Section 17):**
- Within 3 months (extended in practice)
- Binding on parties
- Published in official gazette

**Execution:**
- Award executed as decree (CPC provisions apply)

### Section 11A: Appeal

**Appeal to Labour Appellate Tribunal (if constituted) OR High Court (writ under Article 226/227):**
- Limited grounds (error of law apparent on record, perversity)
- Not re-appreciation of evidence

---

## Strikes and Lockouts

### Section 2(q): "Strike" Defined

**"Cessation of work by body of persons employed, acting in combination, OR concerted refusal to work"**

### Section 2(l): "Lockout" Defined

**"Closing of place of employment, OR suspension of work, OR refusal to continue employment by employer"**

### Section 22-23: Illegal Strikes and Lockouts

**Strike/Lockout ILLEGAL if:**

**1. Without Notice (Section 22):**
- 14 days notice required (in public utility services)
- 6 weeks after conciliation proceedings end (in other establishments)

**2. During Conciliation/Adjudication (Section 23):**
- Strike/lockout prohibited during pendency of proceedings + 7 days thereafter

**3. Contrary to Award/Settlement:**
- Strike against binding award/settlement

**Public Utility Services (Schedule I):**
- Railways, postal, telegraph
- Ports, airways
- Banks, electricity, water supply
- Hospitals

**Penalty (Section 26):**
- Illegal strike: Imprisonment up to 1 month OR fine up to Rs. 50 (per worker)
- Illegal lockout: Imprisonment up to 1 month OR fine up to Rs. 1,000

**Remedies:**
- Employer can dismiss workers participating in illegal strike (misconduct)
- Workers can challenge illegal lockout (wages for lockout period)

### Section 24: Go-Slow, Work-to-Rule

**Prohibited:**
- Deliberately slowing production
