# Local Self-Government - Panchayats and Municipalities

## Purpose
Understand local self-government structure (Panchayats and Municipalities), their powers, functions, and how citizens can engage with/seek redressal from local authorities.

---

## Constitutional Framework

### 73rd Amendment (1992): Panchayati Raj
**Articles 243-243O**: Established Panchayats (rural local self-government).

**Three-Tier Structure** (Article 243C):
1. **Gram Panchayat** (Village level)
2. **Panchayat Samiti / Block Panchayat** (Intermediate level)
3. **Zila Parishad** (District level)

**Key Features**:
- **Direct elections** every 5 years (Article 243E)
- **Reservations**: 1/3 seats for women, SC/ST proportional reservation (Article 243D)
- **State Election Commission** conducts elections (Article 243K)
- **State Finance Commission** recommends financial devolution (Article 243I)

**Functions** (11th Schedule - 29 items):
1. Agriculture, land improvement
2. Minor irrigation, water management
3. Animal husbandry, dairy, poultry
4. Fisheries
5. Social forestry, farm forestry
6. Minor forest produce
7. Small scale industries
8. Khadi, village, cottage industries
9. Rural housing
10. Drinking water
11. Fuel and fodder
12. Roads, culverts, bridges, ferries
13. Rural electrification
14. Non-conventional energy sources
15. Poverty alleviation programmes
16. Education (primary, secondary)
17. Technical training, vocational education
18. Adult and non-formal education
19. Libraries
20. Cultural activities
21. Markets and fairs
22. Health and sanitation
23. Family welfare
24. Women and child development
25. Social welfare
26. Welfare of weaker sections (SC/ST/OBC)
27. Public distribution system
28. Maintenance of community assets
29. **Any other subject** assigned by state law

---

### 74th Amendment (1992): Municipalities
**Articles 243P-243ZG**: Established municipalities (urban local self-government).

**Types of Municipalities** (Article 243Q):
1. **Nagar Panchayat** (Transitional area - village → town)
2. **Municipal Council** (Smaller urban area)
3. **Municipal Corporation** (Larger urban area)

**Key Features**:
- Direct elections every 5 years (Article 243U)
- Reservations for SC/ST, women (1/3), OBC (Article 243T)
- State Election Commission (Article 243K)
- Wards committees in cities >3 lakh population (Article 243S)

**Functions** (12th Schedule - 18 items):
1. Urban planning, town planning
2. Regulation of land use, construction of buildings
3. Planning for economic and social development
4. Roads and bridges
5. Water supply for domestic, industrial, commercial purposes
6. Public health, sanitation, solid waste management
7. Fire services
8. Urban forestry, environmental protection
9. Safeguarding interests of weaker sections
10. Slum improvement and upgradation
11. Urban poverty alleviation
12. Provision of urban amenities and facilities
13. Promotion of cultural, educational, aesthetic aspects
14. Burials and burial grounds, cremations, crematoria, electric crematori
15. Cattle pounds, prevention of cruelty to animals
16. Vital statistics (births, deaths registration)
17. Public amenities (street lighting, parking lots, bus stops, public conveniences)
18. Regulation of slaughter houses and tanneries

---

## Powers of Local Bodies

### Revenue Sources

**Panchayats** (Article 243H):
- Land revenue
- Taxes on professions, trades, callings
- Taxes on animals, boats
- Toll taxes
- Market fees
- Grants from state government
- Assigned revenues

**Municipalities** (Article 243X):
- **Property tax** (major source)
- Octroi (entry tax on goods)
- Advertisement tax
- Water/sewerage charges
- Building plan approval fees
- Trade license fees
- Market fees
- Parking fees
- Grants from state/central government

**Reality**: Most local bodies financially weak, depend heavily on state grants.

---

### Regulatory Powers

**1. Building Permissions** (Municipal Corporation Act):
- Citizen must obtain permission before construction
- Submit building plan
- Compliance with zoning regulations, setback rules, FAR (Floor Area Ratio)
- Timeline: 30-60 days for approval

**Citizen's Right**:
- If plan complies with rules, approval **mandatory** (not discretionary)
- If denied, appeal to municipal authority OR file writ (mandamus)

**2. Trade Licenses**:
- Shops, businesses must obtain trade license
- Health, safety conditions compliance
- Annual renewal

**3. Water/Electricity Connections**:
- Municipality supplies water (in some areas)
- Must provide connection if area has supply line

**4. Sanitation, Garbage Collection**:
- Municipality's **mandatory duty**
- Citizen can file complaint if not collecting garbage
- Writ petition possible if persistent violation

---

## Citizen Engagement with Local Government

### 1. Gram Sabha (Village Assembly)

**Article 243A**: Gram Sabha = Body consisting of all registered voters in village.

**Powers**:
- Approve plans, programmes, projects
- Identify beneficiaries for welfare schemes
- Monitor implementation
- Financial audit of Gram Panchayat accounts

**Meetings**: Minimum 2 per year (varies by state law)

**Citizen Participation**:
- **Any voter** can attend Gram Sabha
- Voice concerns, suggestions
- Hold Panchayat accountable

**Example**: Gram Sabha can question Panchayat on MGNREGA (rural employment scheme) implementation, fund utilization.

---

### 2. Ward Committees (Urban)

**Article 243S**: Cities >3 lakh population must have ward committees.

**Composition**:
- 1 or more wards
- Councillor of ward (chairperson)
- Community representatives

**Functions**:
- Local development planning
- Feedback on municipal services
- Grievance redressal

**Citizen Participation**: Attend ward committee meetings, raise local issues (roads, water supply, garbage).

---

### 3. RTI to Local Bodies

**Local bodies are "Public Authorities"** under RTI Act.

**Information You Can Seek**:
- Property tax assessment records
- Building plan approvals granted in your area
- Water supply timings, quality test reports
- Road repair budgets and contractors
- Waste management contracts
- Official's attendance, salaries

**Example RTI**: "Provide copy of building plan approval granted to plot no. [X] in [locality] on [date]."

---

## Common Grievances and Remedies

### Problem 1: Property Tax Dispute

**Issue**: Municipality levies excessive property tax / wrongly calculates.

**Remedy**:
1. **Appeal to Assessment Officer** (within 30 days of notice)
2. **Revision to Municipal Commissioner**
3. **Writ Petition** (if tax levy illegal or arbitrary - Article 226)

**Legal Ground**: Article 265 - "No tax shall be levied except by authority of law." If tax levy violates municipal act, unconstitutional.

---

### Problem 2: Building Permission Denied

**Issue**: Municipality refuses building permission despite plan complying with rules.

**Remedy**:
1. **Appeal within municipal hierarchy** (if provided in state municipal act)
2. **Writ Petition (Mandamus)**: Compel municipality to grant permission

**Legal Ground**: If plan complies with all rules, municipality has **mandatory duty** to approve (not discretionary).

**Case Law**: *Hardeep Singh v. Municipal Corporation* - If all statutory conditions met, permission cannot be arbitrarily denied.

---

### Problem 3: Water Supply Disruption

**Issue**: Municipality not providing water supply for weeks in your area.

**Remedy**:
1. **Written complaint to municipal office** (Executive Engineer, Water Supply Dept)
2. **RTI**: Ask for reasons for disruption, action plan to restore
3. **Public Grievance Portal** (state/central)
4. **Writ Petition (Mandamus)**: If municipality fails to act, compel water supply

**Legal Ground**: Right to water is part of Article 21 (right to life). Municipality has constitutional duty.

**Case Law**: *Subhash Kumar v. State of Bihar* (1991) - Right to potable water flows from Article 21.

---

### Problem 4: Garbage Not Collected

**Issue**: Municipal garbage van not coming to your area.

**Remedy**:
1. **Complaint to Sanitation Department**
2. **Social Media / Local Councillor**: Often effective
3. **Swachh Bharat Helpline** (1969 - national)
4. **Writ Petition** (if persistent non-compliance)

**Legal Ground**: Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016 (under Environment Protection Act) mandate municipal garbage collection.

---

### Problem 5: Road Repair Delays

**Issue**: Roads in your ward in poor condition, potholes not repaired.

**Remedy**:
1. **Ward Councillor**: Raise in ward committee / council meeting
2. **RTI**: Ask for road repair budget allocation, work orders issued
3. **Public Complaint on Municipal App/Portal**
4. **Writ Petition**: If deliberate negligence causes accidents (Article 21 - right to safe roads)

**Case Law**: *Hindustan Construction v. Union of India* - Government has duty to maintain public infrastructure.

---

## Practical Tips

### 1. Know Your Local Representatives

- **Gram Panchayat**: Sarpanch, Ward Members
- **Municipality**: Councillor (ward), Mayor, Municipal Commissioner

**Contact Details**: Available on municipal website, notice boards.

**Approach**: Before legal remedies, approach elected representative (often quickest resolution).

---

### 2. Use Mobile Apps

**Central Government**:
- **MyGov** (mygov.in): Citizen engagement, grievances
- **CPGRAMS** (pgportal.gov.in): Public grievance portal

**State/Municipal Apps**:
Most cities have mobile apps (e.g., MyBMC for Mumbai, MCD app for Delhi):
- Register complaints (garbage, streetlight, potholes)
- Track status
- Pay property tax online

---

### 3. Attend Meetings

**Gram Sabha** (rural): Participate actively, hold Panchayat accountable.

**Ward Committee** (urban): Raise local issues, demand action.

**Open House** (Mayor/Commissioner): Some cities have monthly open houses - citizens can directly meet officials.

---

### 4. RTI for Transparency

**Strategic RTI Queries**:
- "Provide list of contractors awarded contracts in [ward] in last 6 months with contract amounts."
  → Exposes corruption if same contractor gets all work.

- "Provide quality test reports of drinking water supplied in [area] in last 3 months."
  → Forces municipality to test water if not testing.

- "Provide attendance records of sanitation workers assigned to [ward] for last month."
  → Holds workers accountable.

---

## Protocol Cross-References

**Related**:
- **CIVIC-RTI-BASICS**: File RTI to local bodies for information
- **CIVIC-PUBLIC-GRIEVANCE**: Grievance portals for municipal complaints
- **CIVIC-WRIT-BASICS**: Mandamus to compel municipal duties

---

## Disclaimer

**This protocol provides information about local governance. It does NOT constitute legal advice.**

**Key Points**:
- Local government powers vary by state laws
- Municipal acts differ (Delhi Municipal Corporation Act vs. Maharashtra Municipal Corporations Act, etc.)
- Citizen participation is key to accountability
- Legal remedies available but local engagement often more effective

**Engage actively with your local government for better services.**

---

**Protocol Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-05
**Part of**: India Civic Law Knowledge Ecosystem
**Constitutional Basis**: 73rd & 74th Amendments

---

# National Green Tribunal (NGT) - Environmental Justice Forum

## Purpose
Understand NGT as specialized environmental tribunal, its jurisdiction, procedure for filing applications, types of cases, compensation principles, and strategic use for environmental enforcement.

## Activation Triggers
- Pollution violation (air, water, noise, soil)
- Illegal construction in eco-sensitive zone
- Forest clearance violation
- Wildlife protection violation
- Challenge to environment clearance
- Seeking compensation for environmental damage
- Appeal from Pollution Control Board order

---

## What is National Green Tribunal (NGT)?

### Establishment
**National Green Tribunal Act, 2010** - Enacted by Parliament.

**Operational**: October 18, 2010

**Purpose** (Preamble):
> "An Act to provide for the establishment of a National Green Tribunal for the effective and expeditious disposal of cases relating to environmental protection and conservation of forests and other natural resources..."

---

### Need for Specialized Tribunal

**Problems with Regular Courts**:
1. **Delays**: Environmental cases in HC/SC take 5-10 years
2. **Lack of Technical Expertise**: Judges not trained in environmental science
3. **Procedural Complexity**: CPC/Evidence Act formalities slow down proceedings

**NGT Solution**:
- **Faster Disposal**: Target 6 months (Section 18(3))
- **Expert Members**: Judicial + Environmental Scientists/Engineers
- **Simplified Procedure**: Less formalistic than regular courts
- **Specialized Focus**: Only environmental matters

---

### International Inspiration
**Similar Tribunals Globally**:
- Land and Environment Court (Australia)
- Environmental Court (New Zealand)
- Environmental Tribunal (South Africa)

**India's NGT**: One of the most comprehensive environmental tribunals globally.

---

## Jurisdiction of NGT

### Section 14: Civil Cases Relating to Environment

**NGT has jurisdiction over**:

**1. Substantial Questions Relating to Environment**:
Arising under 7 specified Acts:
1. Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1974
2. Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Cess Act, 1977
3. Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980
4. Air (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1981
5. **Environment (Protection) Act, 1986** (Umbrella law)
6. Public Liability Insurance Act, 1991
7. Biological Diversity Act, 2002

**Plus**: Any rules, notifications, regulations under these Acts (e.g., EIA Notification 2006, Solid Waste Rules 2016, etc.)

**2. Enforcement of Legal Rights Relating to Environment**

**3. Compensation and Relief**:
For damages to persons, property, environment.

**What NGT CANNOT Decide**:
- Pure criminal cases (criminal prosecution under EPA - goes to criminal courts)
- Wildlife offense prosecutions (goes to criminal courts)
- Cases NOT arising under the 7 specified Acts
- Disputes between private parties (unless involving environmental violation)

---

### Section 16: Appeals from Regulatory Authorities

**NGT is Appellate Authority** for orders/decisions/directions by:
- **Pollution Control Boards** (CPCB, SPCBs)
- **State Environment Impact Assessment Authority (SEIAA)**
- **District Environment Impact Assessment Authority (DEIAA)**
- **Any other authority** under the 7 Acts

**Example**:
- SPCB issues closure direction to factory → Factory appeals to NGT
- SEIAA denies Environment Clearance → Applicant appeals to NGT
- SPCB grants consent to polluting industry → Citizen appeals to NGT

**Timeline**: Appeal must be filed **within 30 days** of order (extendable by 60 days if sufficient cause).

---

## Who Can File in NGT?

### Section 15: Locus Standi

**Two Categories**:

**1. Aggrieved Person**:
Person whose legal right violated OR who suffered loss due to environmental violation.

**Example**:
- Farmer whose crops damaged by industrial pollution
- Resident suffering health issues from air pollution
- Landowner whose land encroached for illegal mining

**2. Any Person (PIL-Type Jurisdiction)**:
> "Any person... for a **substantial question** relating to environment..."

**Similar to PIL in HC/SC**: Public-spirited person, NGO, can file even if not personally affected.

**Example**:
- Environmental NGO files application on river pollution (even if NGO members not personally affected)
- Activist files on illegal mining affecting forest (even if activist lives elsewhere)

**Gatekeeping** (Section 19):
NGT may reject frivolous/vexatious applications + impose costs up to ₹10,000.

---

## NGT Bench Structure

### Principal Bench
**Location**: Delhi

**Jurisdiction**: All India (can hear cases from any state)

**Officers**:
- Chairperson (Judicial Member - retired SC/HC judge)
- Expert Members (Environmental scientists/engineers)

---

### Regional Benches

**1. Central Zonal Bench**: Bhopal
- Jurisdiction: Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh

**2. Eastern Zonal Bench**: Kolkata
- Jurisdiction: West Bengal, Bihar, Jharkhand, Odisha, Sikkim, Andaman & Nicobar Islands

**3. Southern Zonal Bench**: Chennai
- Jurisdiction: Tamil Nadu, Kerala, Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Puducherry, Lakshadweep

**4. Western Zonal Bench**: Pune
- Jurisdiction: Maharashtra, Goa, Gujarat, Daman & Diu, Dadra & Nagar Haveli

**Northern States**: No dedicated zonal bench → File in **Principal Bench, Delhi**

---

### Bench Composition

**Each Bench** has:
- **Judicial Members**: Retired HC/SC judges (minimum 2)
- **Expert Members**: Environmental scientists, engineers (minimum 2)

**Quorum**: 1 Judicial Member + 1 Expert Member (minimum for hearing).

**Advantage**: Technical environmental issues decided by experts, not just lawyers/judges.

---

## Types of Cases in NGT

### 1. Original Applications (OA)

**Fresh cases** filed directly in NGT for:
- **Environmental Violations**: Pollution, illegal construction in protected area, etc.
- **Compensation**: For environmental damage
- **Enforcement**: Compelling authority to enforce environmental laws

**Example**:
- OA seeking closure of polluting industry
- OA for compensation to villagers affected by industrial effluent
- OA challenging unauthorized construction in wetland

**Section 14(1)**: Original jurisdiction.

---

### 2. Appeals

**Against Orders of**:
- SPCBs (consent denial, closure direction, penalty)
- SEIAA/DEIAA (Environment Clearance grant/denial)
- Forest Clearance Authority
- Any authority under 7 Acts

**Example**:
- Factory appeals SPCB's closure order
- Citizen appeals SEIAA's grant of EC to project (claiming improper procedure)

**Section 16**: Appellate jurisdiction.

**Timeline**: Within **30 days** (extendable by 60 days).

---

### 3. Suo Moto Cases

**NGT can take cognizance** on its own based on:
- Newspaper reports
- Letters/complaints
- Media coverage

**Example**: NGT took suo moto cognizance of Delhi air pollution based on media reports, issued directions to government.

---

## Procedure for Filing in NGT

### Step 1: Determine Jurisdiction

**Check**:
- Does case arise under 7 specified Acts? (If not, NGT has no jurisdiction - approach HC)
- Is it within 30 days of impugned order (if appeal)?
- Which bench has territorial jurisdiction?

---

### Step 2: Draft Application

**Format** (NGT Practice & Procedure Rules, 2011):

```
BEFORE THE NATIONAL GREEN TRIBUNAL
[PRINCIPAL BENCH / ZONAL BENCH NAME]

ORIGINAL APPLICATION NO. _____ OF 2024
(OR: APPEAL NO. _____ OF 2024)

IN THE MATTER OF:
[Brief description - e.g., "Pollution of River X by Industrial Effluents"]

AND

IN THE MATTER OF:
Environment (Protection) Act, 1986
Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1974

[Applicant Name/Organization] ... Applicant

Versus

[Respondent Names - Polluter, SPCB, etc.] ... Respondents
```

**Contents**:
1. **Parties**: Applicant, Respondents
2. **Facts**: Chronological, numbered paragraphs
3. **Legal Violations**: Which Act/Rule violated
4. **Locus Standi**: Why applicant has right to file (aggrieved / substantial question)
5. **Relief Sought**: Prayers (closure, compensation, directions)
6. **Documents**: List of annexures
7. **Verification**: Applicant verifies facts on oath

---

### Step 3: Filing Fee

**Fee Structure** (NGT Rules):
- **Individuals**: ₹1,000
- **Association of Individuals**: ₹5,000
- **Other Applicants** (companies, etc.): ₹10,000

**Payment Mode**: Demand draft in favor of "Registrar, National Green Tribunal"

**Fee Waiver**: NGT may waive fee if applicant proves poverty (Section 18(2)).

---

### Step 4: Required Documents

**Mandatory**:
1. **Application** (typed, on A4 paper, double-spaced)
2. **Affidavit** verifying application
3. **Index** of annexures
4. **Vakalatnama** (if filing through advocate - optional, can file yourself)
5. **Annexures** (all supporting documents):
   - Pollution Control Board's order (if appeal)
   - RTI responses
   - Photos, videos (in CD/USB)
   - Expert reports (if available)
   - News clippings
6. **Demand Draft** (filing fee)
7. **Certified Copies**: 5 copies for respondents + 1 for NGT file

---

### Step 5: Filing

**Mode**:
- **In Person**: Visit NGT Registry (during office hours)
- **By Post**: Speed Post / Registered Post to Registrar, NGT (relevant bench)
- **Email**: ngt.filing@gov.in (some benches accept email filing)

**Acknowledgment**: Registry issues acknowledgment with diary number.

---

### Step 6: Scrutiny

**Registry Scrutiny** (3-5 days):
- Check if application complete
- Check if fee paid
- Check if jurisdiction exists

**If Defect**: Return application OR give time to rectify.

**If Complete**: Register application, allocate number.

---

### Step 7: Listing & Notice

**NGT issues notice** to respondents (SPCB, polluter, government).

**Respondents file reply** within specified time (usually 30 days).

**Listing**: Case listed for hearing.

---

### Step 8: Interim Relief (If Sought)

**If Urgent Relief Needed**:
File **application for interim directions** along with main application.

**Relief Sought**:
- Stay operation of impugned order
- Stop polluting activity immediately
- Prevent irreversible damage

**Test**:
- Prima facie case?
- Balance of convenience?
- Irreparable injury if relief not granted?
- Environmental emergency?

**Example**: River pollution causing health hazard → NGT may order immediate stoppage of effluent discharge pending final hearing.

---

### Step 9: Hearing

**Simplified Procedure**:
- **No CPC/Evidence Act formalities** (NGT not bound by these - Section 19(2))
- **Proceeds on affidavits** (usually no oral evidence)
- **Arguments by parties** (lawyers OR self-representation)
- **Questions by Expert Members** (on technical aspects)

**Timeline Target**: Dispose within **6 months** (Section 18(3)) - Often not met due to backlog, but faster than HC.

---

### Step 10: Judgment/Order

**NGT Passes**:
- **Reasoned Order**: Facts, legal analysis, findings
- **Directions**: To respondents (comply, pay compensation, restore, etc.)
- **Compensation** (if awarded)
- **Costs** (if frivolous application, costs imposed on applicant; if violation proved, costs on respondent)

**Execution**: NGT order is executable as decree of civil court (Section 26).

---

## Reliefs & Remedies in NGT

### 1. Closure / Stoppage Directions

**NGT can order**:
- Close polluting industry
- Stop illegal construction/mining
- Cease operations till compliance

**Example**: NGT ordered closure of 500+ illegal stone crushers in NCR for air pollution.

---

### 2. Compensation

**Section 17**: NGT can award compensation to victims of environmental damage.

**Principles Applied**:
- **Polluter Pays Principle**: Polluter bears cost of restoration + compensation
- **Restitution**: Restore environment to original state
- **Compensation to Affected Persons**: For health damage, property loss, livelihood loss

**Quantum**:
- Can be substantial (₹crores in some cases)
- Based on extent of damage, restoration cost, number of affected persons

**Example**: NGT ordered mining company to pay ₹100 crore for illegal mining + ecological damage in Goa.

---

### 3. Restoration Directions

**NGT can direct**:
- Afforestation
- Removal of encroachments
- Cleaning of rivers/water bodies
- Restoration of wetlands

**Example**: NGT directed Delhi government to remove all encroachments on Yamuna floodplain.

---

### 4. Compliance Monitoring

**NGT appoints**:
- **Monitoring Committees**: To oversee implementation (comprising government officials, experts, NGO representatives)
- **Expert Committees**: To study technical issues, submit reports

**Continuing Jurisdiction**: NGT monitors compliance till complete execution.

---

### 5. Costs

**On Frivolous Applicants**: Up to ₹10,000 (Section 19)

**On Violators**: Costs for wasting court time, causing environmental harm (no cap).

---

## Strategic Use of NGT

### Advantages Over HC/SC

**1. Speed**: Target 6 months (vs. 3-10 years in HC/SC)

**2. Lower Cost**:
- Filing fee: ₹1,000 (vs. ₹5,000+ in HC)
- No mandatory lawyer (can file yourself)

**3. Technical Expertise**: Expert members understand environmental science (don't need to educate judges on technicalities)

**4. Simplified Procedure**: Less formalistic

**5. Compensation**: NGT readily awards compensation on Polluter Pays Principle

---

### Disadvantages

**1. Limited Jurisdiction**: Only 7 Acts (doesn't cover all environmental issues)

**2. No Writ Powers**: Unlike HC (Article 226), NGT cannot issue writs against government for non-environmental matters

**3. Cannot Declare Law Unconstitutional**: Only SC/HC can declare law unconstitutional (if challenging validity of environmental law itself, must go to HC)

**4. Backlog**: Despite 6-month target, many cases take 1-3 years

**5. Appeal to SC**: If NGT decision adverse, must appeal to SC (expensive, time-consuming)

---

### When to Choose NGT vs. HC

**NGT**:
- Pure environmental violation (pollution, clearance violation)
- Seek compensation for environmental damage
- Appeal from SPCB/SEIAA order
- Cost-effective litigation
- Technical issue (need expert members)

**HC**:
- Constitutional question (Articles 14, 21)
- Challenge law itself (not just its application)
- Mixed issues (environmental + non-environmental)
- Need writ powers (mandamus, certiorari)
- Urgency (HC may grant stay faster in some cases)

**Both Allowed** (not mutually exclusive): Can file in both fora if grounds different. But usually, exhaust NGT first, then appeal to SC.

---

## Common Cases in NGT

### 1. Industrial Pollution

**Application**: Seek closure of polluting factory, compensation to affected residents.

**Evidence**: SPCB inspection reports (via RTI), pollution readings, medical reports of affected persons, photos/videos.

**Outcome**: NGT often orders closure till compliance + compensation.

---

### 2. Challenge to Environment Clearance

**Application** (Appeal under Section 16): SEIAA granted EC without proper public consultation OR based on flawed EIA.

**Grounds**: Violation of EIA Notification procedure, suppression of facts, non-consideration of environmental impact.

**Outcome**: NGT may quash EC, remand for fresh consideration, or uphold with additional conditions.

---

### 3. Illegal Construction in Eco-Sensitive Zone

**Application**: Seek demolition of illegal construction in forest/wetland/coastal regulation zone.

**Legal Basis**: Violation of Forest Act, Coastal Regulation Zone Notification, Wetland Rules.

**Outcome**: NGT orders demolition + restoration + penalty.

---

### 4. Waste Management Violations

**Application**: Municipality not collecting garbage / illegal landfill / hazardous waste dumping.

**Relief**: Directions to municipality to comply with Solid Waste Rules, appoint monitoring committee.

---

### 5. Wildlife Protection

**Application** (if civil relief sought, not criminal prosecution): Illegal clearing of habitat, encroachment in sanctuary.

**Outcome**: Restoration, compensation to Wildlife Department, penalty.

---

## Appeal from NGT

### Appeal to Supreme Court

**Section 22**: Any person aggrieved by NGT decision can appeal to SC.

**Timeline**: Within **90 days** (no extension provision - strictly 90 days).

**Grounds**: Legal error, perversity, non-consideration of material evidence.

**Note**: Appeal on facts difficult (SC generally doesn't reappreciate facts found by NGT's expert members).

---

## Practical Tips

### 1. Gather Strong Evidence

**NGT Proceeds on Evidence**:
- Pollution readings (from SPCB or independent testing)
- Photos/videos (timestamped, geotagged)
- Expert reports (environmental scientists, doctors)
- RTI responses (SPCB consent status, inspection reports)
- Affidavits of affected persons

**Weak Evidence = Weak Case.**

---

### 2. Use RTI Before Filing

**File RTI to**:
- SPCB: Industry's consent status, violations, inspection dates, actions taken
- SEIAA: EC application, EIA report, public hearing records
- Forest Department: Clearances, encroachment details

**RTI = Ammunition for NGT Application.**

---

### 3. Quantify Damage (For Compensation)

**For Compensation Claims**:
- Calculate restoration cost (afforestation, cleaning, etc.)
- Medical expenses of affected persons
- Loss of livelihood (if crops damaged, fish killed, etc.)
- Property damage

**Higher Compensation if Quantified Precisely.**

---

### 4. Seek Interim Relief

**If Environmental Emergency**:
File interim application immediately (stoppage of pollution, prevent irreversible damage).

**NGT Grants Interim Relief** if:
- Serious environmental degradation ongoing
- Irreversible damage likely
- Prima facie violation

---

### 5. Follow Up on Implementation

**NGT Order ≠ Automatic Compliance**:
- Monitor whether respondent complying
- If not, file **execution application** (Section 26)
- NGT can issue contempt proceedings for non-compliance

---

## Protocol Cross-References

**Prerequisites**:
- **CIVIC-ENVIRONMENTAL-LAW**: Understand environmental laws before approaching NGT

**Related**:
- **CIVIC-PIL-BASICS**: For PIL-type jurisdiction in NGT
- **CIVIC-WRIT-BASICS**: For alternative HC remedy
- **CIVIC-RTI-BASICS**: Use RTI for evidence gathering

---

## Disclaimer

**This protocol provides information about NGT. It does NOT constitute legal advice.**

**Key Points**:
- NGT is specialized forum for environmental disputes (faster, cheaper than HC)
- Jurisdiction limited to 7 specified Acts
- Simplified procedure, can file without lawyer
- Compensation awarded on Polluter Pays Principle
- Appeal to SC within 90 days (strictly enforced)

**NGT is powerful tool for environmental enforcement. Use it strategically with strong evidence and clear legal grounds.**

---

**Protocol Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-05
**Part of**: India Civic Law Knowledge Ecosystem
**Statutory Basis**: National Green Tribunal Act, 2010
**NGT Website**: https://greentribunal.gov.in

# Public Interest Litigation (PIL) - Strategic Citizen Action

## Purpose
Understand Public Interest Litigation as a constitutional remedy for enforcing diffused/collective rights, learn when PIL is appropriate vs. private remedy, understand locus standi liberalization, and strategic PIL filing for social justice.

## Activation Triggers
- Violation affects large section of public/marginalized groups
- Affected persons unable to approach court (poverty, illiteracy, fear)
- Environmental degradation affecting community
- Corruption in public projects affecting public funds
- Systemic violation of fundamental rights
- Government inaction on statutory duties affecting public

---

## What is PIL?

### Definition
**Public Interest Litigation (PIL)**: Strategic litigation filed for protection of public interest, especially for rights of marginalized/disadvantaged groups unable to approach courts themselves.

**Key Characteristics**:
1. **Not for private interest**: For collective/diffused rights
2. **Relaxed locus standi**: Any public-spirited person can file
3. **Pro-active judiciary**: Court takes active role in investigation, monitoring
4. **Continuing jurisdiction**: Court monitors implementation over years

**Dr. Justice V.R. Krishna Iyer**: "PIL is a strategic arm of the legal aid movement to reach social justice to the deprived millions."

---

## Evolution of PIL in India

### Pre-1980s: Traditional Standing Rule

**Classical Rule**: Only "aggrieved person" whose own legal right violated could file writ.

**Example**: If government demolished slum, only slum dweller could file writ (not NGO, not activist).

**Rationale**: Prevent busybodies from clogging courts.

---

### 1980s: PIL Revolution

**S.P. Gupta v. Union of India** (1982) - Watershed Case:

Justice Bhagwati held:
> "Public Interest Litigation... is a strategic arm of legal aid movement... Where a person or class of persons to whom legal injury is caused by reason of violation of a fundamental right is unable to approach the court for relief by reason of poverty or disability or socially or economically disadvantaged position, any member of the public acting bonafide can maintain an application in this Court."

**Liberalized Standing**:
- **Any public-spirited person** can file
- **Epistolary jurisdiction**: Letter to SC/HC treated as PIL
- Relaxed procedural requirements

---

### Landmark Early PILs

**1. Bandhua Mukti Morcha v. Union of India** (1984):
- **Issue**: Bonded laborers in stone quarries
- **Who filed**: Social organization (not laborers themselves)
- **Outcome**: SC liberated laborers, ordered rehabilitation
- **Principle**: PIL maintainable for rights of poorest who cannot access courts

**2. Sheela Barse v. State of Maharashtra** (1983):
- **Issue**: Women prisoners' rights
- **Who filed**: Journalist (based on letter)
- **Outcome**: SC issued directions for prison reforms
- **Principle**: Epistolary PIL (letter treated as writ petition)

**3. M.C. Mehta v. Union of India** (1987):
- **Issue**: Ganga river pollution
- **Who filed**: Environmental lawyer
- **Outcome**: SC directed closure of polluting industries
- **Principle**: Environmental PIL for diffused rights (clean water, air)

---

## Locus Standi in PIL

### Who Can File PIL?

**Eligible**:
1. **NGOs/Social Organizations**: Working in relevant field
2. **Public-Spirited Citizens**: Bonafide interest in issue
3. **Advocates**: Filing in public interest (not for professional gain)
4. **Journalists**: Exposing violations
5. **Affected Group Representative**: Filing on behalf of larger group

**Letter to CJI/Judges**: Can be treated as PIL (epistolary jurisdiction) if:
- Highlights serious violation
- Affects marginalized/vulnerable groups
- Requires judicial intervention

---

### Test for Maintainability

**Courts Apply Test** (*Common Cause v. Union of India*):

**1. Genuine Public Interest?**
- Does petition raise issue of public importance?
- Or is it private dispute in disguise?

**2. Affected Persons Unable to Approach Court?**
- Poverty, illiteracy, fear of retaliation?
- If affected persons can file themselves, why PIL?

**3. Bonafide Intent?**
- Is petitioner genuinely interested in social welfare?
- Or seeking publicity/political mileage/professional gain?

**4. Not Busybody/Publicity PIL?**
- Petitioner has credibility in field?
- Not using court as platform for extraneous purpose?

---

### When PIL NOT Maintainable

**SC/HCs have rejected PILs filed by**:

**1. Politicians for Political Mileage**:
*Example*: Politician files PIL against rival party's government scheme → Dismissed as politically motivated.

**2. Lawyers for Professional Gain**:
*Example*: Lawyer files PIL, then offers to withdraw if government hires him as consultant → Dismissed with costs.

**3. Busybodies**:
*Example*: Person with no connection to issue files PIL against municipal contractor → Dismissed ("not your business").

**4. Private Disputes Disguised as PIL**:
*Example*: Land dispute between two parties filed as "PIL for protecting public land" → Dismissed ("private interest").

**5. Premature PILs**:
*Example*: PIL challenging government policy before policy notified → Dismissed ("no cause of action yet").

---

## PIL vs. Private Writ Petition

### Comparison

| **PIL** | **Private Writ** |
|---------|------------------|
| Public/diffused rights (clean air, water, corruption-free governance) | Personal/individual rights (passport denial, property rights) |
| Any public-spirited person can file | Only aggrieved person can file |
| Court takes active role (suo moto investigation, monitoring) | Adversarial (petitioner vs. respondent) |
| No court fee (or nominal) | Court fee payable |
| Relaxed procedural rules | Strict procedure |
| Continuing jurisdiction (years of monitoring) | Disposed after final order |

---

### Examples

**PIL**:
- Government school lacks toilets for girl students → Violates Article 21A (right to education) → Any NGO can file PIL

**Private Writ**:
- Your passport application denied → Violates your right to travel → Only you can file writ

**PIL**:
- Municipal corporation not collecting garbage in slum area → Public health violation → Resident welfare association/NGO can file PIL

**Private Writ**:
- Municipality wrongly calculated your property tax → Personal grievance → Only you can file writ

---

## Subjects of PIL

### Classic PIL Domains

**1. Environmental Protection**:
- Pollution (air, water, noise)
- Forest encroachment
- Wildlife protection
- Illegal mining
- Waste management

**Example**: *M.C. Mehta PILs* - Dozens of environmental PILs by advocate M.C. Mehta led to:
- Closure of polluting industries
- CNG conversion of Delhi buses
- Yamuna cleaning
- Taj Mahal protection zone

---

**2. Rights of Marginalized Groups**:
- Bonded laborers
- Slum dwellers
- Prisoners
- Women and children
- Disabled persons
- Tribals

**Example**: *Bandhua Mukti Morcha* - Liberation of bonded laborers from quarries.

---

**3. Corruption in Public Projects**:
- Irregularities in tenders
- Misuse of public funds
- Illegal contracts

**Example**: PIL challenging award of contracts without tender → SC ordered CBI investigation.

---

**4. Right to Education**:
- School infrastructure (toilets, drinking water)
- Teacher attendance
- Mid-day meal quality
- Denial of admission

**Example**: *Unnikrishnan v. State of AP* (1993) - PIL on capitation fees in education.

---

**5. Right to Health**:
- Hospital negligence (systemic)
- Drug availability in government hospitals
- Public health hazards
- Epidemic response

**Example**: *CESC v. Subhash Chandra Bose* (1992) - PIL on dengue epidemic and public health measures.

---

**6. Police Reforms**:
- Custodial deaths
- Illegal detention
- Police brutality (systemic)
- Non-registration of FIRs

**Example**: *D.K. Basu v. State of West Bengal* (1997) - PIL led to SC guidelines on arrest and custodial interrogation.

---

**7. Access to Justice**:
- Legal aid for poor
- Speedy trial
- Prison overcrowding
- Judicial delays

**Example**: *Hussainara Khatoon v. State of Bihar* (1979) - PIL for undertrials languishing in jails beyond sentence period.

---

**8. Consumer Protection**:
- Adulterated food/drugs
- Defective goods (affecting large consumers)
- Misleading advertisements

**Example**: PIL challenging sale of misbranded drugs.

---

**9. Right to Information / Transparency**:
- Government secrecy violations
- Denial of public documents
- Opacity in governance

**Example**: PILs led to proactive disclosure requirements for government departments.

---

**10. Governance & Administrative Reforms**:
- Illegal demolitions
- Unauthorized constructions
- Encroachments on public land
- Non-implementation of welfare schemes

---

## Strategic Advantages of PIL

**1. Access to Justice for Poor**:
Persons who cannot afford lawyer/court fee can benefit from PIL filed by NGO/advocate.

**2. Systemic Change**:
PIL can lead to policy reforms, not just individual relief.

**Example**: *Vishaka v. State of Rajasthan* (1997) - PIL on sexual harassment led to Vishaka Guidelines (pre-cursor to Sexual Harassment Act 2013).

**3. Continuing Jurisdiction**:
SC/HC monitors implementation for years. Appoints monitoring committees, commissioners.

**Example**: *M.C. Mehta (Taj Mahal)* - SC monitored pollution around Taj Mahal for over 15 years.

**4. Judicial Activism**:
Court can issue innovative directions, not constrained by pleadings.

**Example**: SC ordered CNG conversion of buses (not specifically sought in petition).

---

## Limitations & Criticisms of PIL

### Judicial Overreach

**Criticism**: PIL leads to judiciary entering policy domain (separation of powers violation).

**Example**: SC ordering specific fuel type for buses (CNG) → Policy decision (should be executive's domain).

**Counter**: When executive fails to act, judiciary must step in to enforce fundamental rights.

---

### Delays in Regular Cases

**Criticism**: PIL cases consume judicial time, delaying regular cases.

**Reality**: Some PILs pending for 10-20 years.

---

### Publicity PILs

**Criticism**: Politicians, activists file PILs for media attention.

**Judicial Response**: HCs now impose costs on frivolous PILs.

**Example**: HC imposed ₹50,000 cost on politician who filed PIL for publicity, withdrew later.

---

### Weak Implementation

**Criticism**: SC/HC passes orders, but executive doesn't implement.

**Example**: Yamuna cleaning ordered decades ago, river still polluted.

---

## Procedure for Filing PIL

### Step 1: Identify Genuine Public Interest Issue

**Ask**:
- Does issue affect large section of public?
- Are affected persons unable to approach court themselves?
- Is there systemic violation of fundamental rights?
- Is PIL the appropriate remedy (or should grievance portal / RTI be used first)?

**Not PIL**: Your personal passport delay, property dispute, employment issue.

---

### Step 2: Gather Evidence

**Document**:
- Ground reality (photos, videos, affidavits of affected persons)
- Government data/RTI responses showing violation
- News reports, NGO reports
- Relevant laws/rules being violated

**Stronger Evidence = Higher Chance of Admission**

---

### Step 3: Draft PIL Petition

**Format** (varies by HC, but generally):

```
IN THE [HIGH COURT OF STATE / SUPREME COURT OF INDIA]
WRIT PETITION (CIVIL) NO. ____ OF 2024
(PUBLIC INTEREST LITIGATION)

IN THE MATTER OF:
[Issue - e.g., "Illegal mining in Protected Forest Area"]

AND

IN THE MATTER OF:
Article 21 (Right to Life and Clean Environment)

[Petitioner Name/Organization] ... Petitioner

Versus

[State Government / Union of India / Specific Authority] ... Respondents
```

**Contents**:
1. **Title & Parties**
2. **Nature of Petition** (Public Interest Litigation)
3. **Locus Standi** (Why petitioner has standing - bonafide interest, work in field)
4. **Facts** (Chronological, numbered paras)
5. **Public Interest Element** (How issue affects public/marginalized groups)
6. **Legal Violations** (Articles 14, 21, etc., or statutory violations)
7. **Respondents' Inaction** (What government should have done but didn't)
8. **Relief Sought** (Prayers)
9. **Verification & Affidavit**
10. **Annexures** (All documents, reports, photos)

---

### Step 4: File Petition

**Where**:
- **Supreme Court**: Article 32 (if fundamental rights violation of national importance)
- **High Court**: Article 226 (state-level issue)

**Court Fee**:
- Often waived for genuine PIL (or nominal - ₹50)
- If PIL frivolous, court may impose heavy costs

**Documents**:
- Petition with annexures
- Vakalatnama (if advocate filing)
- Affidavit verifying facts

---

### Step 5: Admission Hearing

**Threshold Test**:
- Is this genuine PIL or private interest?
- Does petitioner have locus standi?
- Is issue of public importance?
- Prima facie case?

**Outcomes**:
1. **Admitted**: Notice issued to respondents → Regular hearing
2. **Dismissed**: No public interest / frivolous / alternative remedy available
3. **Converted to Representation**: Court treats as letter, forwards to authority to take action

---

### Step 6: Interim Relief (If Sought)

**Prayers**:
- Stay illegal activity (e.g., stop illegal construction, mining)
- Preserve status quo
- Appoint fact-finding committee

**Test**: Same as regular writ (prima facie case, balance of convenience, irreparable injury).

---

### Step 7: Final Hearing & Directions

**Court's Active Role**:
- May appoint commissioners to investigate
- May summon authorities for personal appearance
- May issue wide-ranging directions beyond pleadings

**Remedies**:
- Declaratory relief (declare action unconstitutional)
- Mandamus (compel authority to act)
- Compensation to affected persons
- Structural reforms (policy changes)
- Monitoring committees (continuing jurisdiction)

---

## Strategic Tips for PIL Filing

### 1. Choose Right Forum

**Supreme Court** (Article 32):
- Issue of national importance (affects multiple states)
- Policy-level violation
- Precedent-setting matter

**High Court** (Article 226):
- State/local issue
- Faster disposal (SC backlog higher)
- Geographically accessible

---

### 2. Build Credibility

**Petitioner's Track Record**:
- If NGO: Show work in relevant field (education, environment, women's rights, etc.)
- If individual: Show bonafide interest (not first-time filer for publicity)

**Example**: M.C. Mehta filed dozens of environmental PILs → Courts trust his bonafides.

---

### 3. Use RTI First

**Strengthen PIL with Government's Own Data**:
- File RTI to get official statistics, files
- Cite RTI responses in PIL to show government's admission

**Example**: RTI revealed school lacks toilets despite funds allocated → PIL cites RTI response.

---

### 4. Collaborate with Experts

**Technical Issues** (environment, health, education):
- Attach expert reports (environmental scientists, doctors, engineers)
- Courts give weight to expert opinion

**Example**: PIL on air pollution supported by IIT study on pollution levels.

---

### 5. Media Strategy

**Responsible Use**:
- Use media to highlight issue (not for personal publicity)
- Public awareness → Pressure on government → Faster resolution

**Avoid**: Filing PIL just for press conference.

---

### 6. Be Prepared for Long Haul

**PIL = Marathon, Not Sprint**:
- Some PILs take 10-20 years
- Requires sustained engagement, follow-up
- Court may call for affidavits, status reports repeatedly

**Example**: Ganga pollution PIL (M.C. Mehta) - ongoing since 1985.

---

## Protocol Cross-References

**Prerequisites**:
- **CIVIC-WRIT-BASICS**: Understand writ jurisdiction before filing PIL

**Related**:
- **CIVIC-ENVIRONMENTAL-LAW**: For environmental PILs
- **CIVIC-WRIT-TYPES**: For selecting appropriate writ type (mandamus, etc.)
- **IL-CONST-ARTICLE-14**: Equality violations common ground for PIL
- **IL-CONST-ARTICLE-21**: Life & liberty violations central to PIL

---

## Disclaimer

**This protocol provides information about PIL. It does NOT constitute legal advice.**

**Key Points**:
- PIL is for public interest, not private disputes
- Courts increasingly vigilant against publicity/frivolous PILs
- Requires bonafide intent, credible petitioner, strong evidence
- Alternative remedies (RTI, grievance portal) may be more effective for some issues

**PIL is a powerful tool for social justice. Use it responsibly, not frivolously.**

---

**Protocol Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-05
**Part of**: India Civic Law Knowledge Ecosystem
**Constitutional Basis**: Articles 32, 226
**Inspiration**: Justice V.R. Krishna Iyer, Justice P.N. Bhagwati

# Public Grievance Redressal - Government Services

## Purpose
Navigate public grievance redressal mechanisms (CPGRAMS, state portals), understand Citizens Charter, and file complaints against government service delays/deficiencies.

---

## CPGRAMS (Centralized Public Grievance Redress and Monitoring System)

### What is CPGRAMS?
**Portal**: https://pgportal.gov.in

**Purpose**: Online platform for citizens to lodge complaints/grievances against Central Government departments/organizations.

**Coverage**:
- All Central Government Ministries/Departments
- Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs)
- Autonomous Bodies under Central Government

**NOT Covered**:
- State Government matters (use state portals)
- Private companies
- Judiciary (separate mechanism)
- Purely personal disputes

---

### How to File Grievance on CPGRAMS

**Step 1: Register**
- Visit pgportal.gov.in
- Click "Lodge Public Grievance"
- Register with mobile number / email (OTP verification)

**Step 2: Fill Grievance Details**
- **Select Department**: Choose relevant ministry/department from dropdown
- **Subject**: Brief description (50 characters)
- **Description**: Detailed grievance (max 1000 characters)
  - What service you sought
  - When you applied
  - What went wrong (delay/denial/poor quality)
  - Supporting documents (optional - upload scans)
- **Category**: Select category (pension, ration card, passport, etc.)

**Step 3: Submit**
- Get **unique registration number**
- Save this number for tracking

**Step 4: Track Status**
- Login to portal
- Enter registration number
- View status: Pending / Under Process / Resolved / Closed

**Timeline**: Department must respond within **60 days** (some departments have shorter timelines).

---

### Examples of Grievances

**Example 1: Passport Delay**
- Department: Ministry of External Affairs - Passport Services
- Grievance: "Applied for passport on [date] at [RPO]. Normal processing time is 30 days. 60 days elapsed, no update. Application no: [number]."

**Example 2: Pension Delay**
- Department: Ministry of Personnel (Department of Pension)
- Grievance: "Retired from [organization] on [date]. Submitted pension papers on [date]. No pension received for 6 months. PPO no: [number]."

**Example 3: EPFO Claim Delay**
- Department: Ministry of Labour - EPFO
- Grievance: "Filed EPF withdrawal claim on [date]. Claim no: [number]. Amount: ₹[amount]. Aadhaar-linked, all KYC complete. 45 days elapsed, no credit."

---

### After Filing on CPGRAMS

**Department Action**:
1. Grievance assigned to **Grievance Officer** of that department
2. Officer reviews complaint
3. Contacts concerned section/office
4. Takes corrective action (if valid)
5. Updates status on portal

**Escalation**:
If not resolved in 60 days:
- Grievance forwarded to **Appellate Authority** (senior officer)
- Can also approach **Director of Public Grievances** (PMO)

**Citizen Action**:
- Check status every week
- If marked "Resolved" but issue not resolved → Reopen grievance OR file fresh grievance with "Previous grievance no: [X] marked resolved without action"

---

## State Grievance Portals

### Common State Portals

- **Maharashtra**: Maha Jansampark (mahasampark.gov.in)
- **Delhi**: esamadhan.delhi.gov.in
- **Karnataka**: Sakala Services (sakala.karnataka.gov.in)
- **Tamil Nadu**: CM Helpline (cms.tn.gov.in)
- **Uttar Pradesh**: Jansunwai (jansunwai.up.nic.in)
- **Gujarat**: CM Window (cmogujarat.gov.in)

**Use State Portals For**:
- State government departments
- State PSUs
- Municipal corporations (some states)
- Revenue Department (land records, certificates)
- Police (non-emergency complaints)

---

## Citizens Charter

### What is Citizens Charter?
**Definition**: Document published by government department listing:
1. Services provided
2. **Timeline** for each service
3. Documents required
4. Fees
5. Grievance redressal officer contact

**Legal Status**: Not statutory (not law), but government policy. Violations can be challenged via grievance portal or writ petition (if systematic denial).

### Key Services with Timelines (Examples)

**Passport**:
- Normal: 30 days
- Tatkal: 3 days
- (Citizens Charter of Passport Seva)

**Ration Card**:
- 30 days from application
- (State Food & Civil Supplies Department)

**Driving License**:
- Learner's License: Same day (after test)
- Permanent License: 7-30 days (varies by state)
- (State Transport Department)

**Birth/Death Certificate**:
- If registered within 21 days: Immediately
- If late registration: 30 days
- (Municipal Corporation / Gram Panchayat)

**PAN Card**:
- Physical PAN: 15 days
- e-PAN: Immediate (online)
- (Income Tax Department)

**Police Clearance Certificate**:
- 30 days
- (State Police - varies by state)

---

### Using Citizens Charter

**Before Applying**:
- Check Citizens Charter of department
- Note timeline, required documents
- Ensure application complete (avoid rejection)

**After Applying**:
- If timeline expires without service delivered:
  - File grievance on CPGRAMS / State portal
  - Cite Citizens Charter commitment
  - Request escalation

**Example Grievance**:
"As per Citizens Charter of Passport Seva, passport must be issued within 30 days. I applied on [date]. 45 days elapsed. Charter violated. Request immediate processing."

---

## Sector-Specific Helplines

### Central Government

| Service | Helpline | Remarks |
|---------|----------|---------|
| **Railways** | 139 (enquiry), 182 (complaints) | Train delays, cleanliness, safety |
| **Banking** | Bank-specific (e.g., SBI: 1800 11 2211) | Account issues, fraud |
| **EPFO** | 1800 11 8005 | EPF withdrawal, claims |
| **Income Tax** | 1800 180 1961 | Tax refund, PAN, returns |
| **Electricity (State)** | Varies (e.g., BESCOM Bangalore: 1912) | Power cuts, meter issues |
| **Gas (LPG)** | Company-specific (Indane: 1800 233 3555) | Cylinder booking, gas leaks |
| **Telecom** | Operator-specific (Airtel: 121, Jio: 1991) | Billing, network issues |

**Use Helplines First**: Often faster than online grievance (immediate acknowledgment, faster resolution).

---

## Escalation Path

**Standard Escalation**:
1. **Service Provider** (Department/Office)
2. **Grievance Portal** (CPGRAMS / State portal - 60 days)
3. **Appellate Authority** (if provided in portal)
4. **RTI Application** (seek reasons for delay)
5. **Writ Petition** (if fundamental/legal right violated)

**Timeline**:
- Grievance: 60 days
- RTI: 30 days response
- Writ: 6 months - 3 years (court delay)

**Cost**:
- Grievance: Free
- RTI: ₹10
- Writ: Court fees + advocate fees (₹10,000 - ₹1,00,000+)

**Effectiveness**:
- Grievance: 60-70% resolved (simple service delays)
- RTI: 80% get information (disclosure, not service delivery)
- Writ: 40-50% success rate (court decides on merits)

---

## Common Grievance Categories

### 1. Service Delay (Most Common)

**Issue**: Department not delivering service within Citizens Charter timeline.

**Remedy**:
- File grievance citing timeline violation
- Provide application date, timeline as per charter, delay details
- Request expedited processing

**Success Rate**: High (if application complete)

---

### 2. Arbitrary Rejection

**Issue**: Application rejected citing vague/arbitrary reasons.

**Remedy**:
- File grievance questioning rejection
- Cite relevant rules showing application complies
- Request reconsideration + speaking order (reasoned rejection)

**Legal Ground**: Article 14 (arbitrary action violates equality)

---

### 3. Corruption / Bribery Demand

**Issue**: Official demands bribe for processing application.

**Remedy**:
1. **File complaint on Anti-Corruption Bureau portal / Lokayukta**
2. **Central Vigilance Commission** (cvc.gov.in) - for Central Government
3. **CBI Anti-Corruption Helpline**: 1800 110 180
4. **Do NOT pay bribe** - service can be obtained through grievance escalation

**Protection**: Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014 (protection for complainants)

---

### 4. Poor Service Quality

**Issue**: Service delivered but quality poor (e.g., poor road repair, unclean hospital).

**Remedy**:
- File grievance with photos/evidence
- Cite relevant quality standards (if applicable)
- Request re-work / improvement

**Example**: Municipal road repaired poorly (potholes reappear in 1 month) → Grievance to municipality + CC to CPGRAMS (if central scheme funds used).

---

## Strategic Tips

### 1. Document Everything
- Application acknowledgment / receipt
- Correspondence with department
- Citizens Charter timeline
- Photos (if quality issue)

**Attach to grievance**: Scanned documents strengthen case.

---

### 2. Be Specific, Factual
**Poor Grievance**: "XYZ office is useless, staff rude, don't care about citizens."
**Good Grievance**: "Applied for [service] on [date]. Application no: [X]. Timeline: 30 days as per Citizens Charter. 50 days elapsed. No response. Request immediate processing."

**Why**: Factual grievances get faster action (easy to verify, act upon).

---

### 3. Use Multiple Channels Simultaneously
File grievance + Call helpline + Tweet to department (if on Twitter) + Email to Grievance Officer.

**Pressure from multiple channels** → Faster resolution.

---

### 4. Follow Up Weekly
Check grievance status every 7 days. If status not updated, call helpline / email Grievance Officer.

**Squeaky wheel gets oil**: Persistent follow-up increases resolution chance.

---

### 5. Escalate if No Response in 60 Days
After 60 days:
- Reopen grievance (if marked resolved without action)
- File RTI asking reasons for delay in acting on grievance
- File fresh grievance addressed to **Secretary of Ministry** (top officer)

---

## Protocol Cross-References

**Related**:
- **CIVIC-RTI-BASICS**: Use RTI if grievance portal ineffective
- **CIVIC-LOCAL-GOVERNANCE**: Municipal grievances
- **CIVIC-WRIT-BASICS**: File writ if legal right violated

---

## Disclaimer

**This protocol provides information about public grievance mechanisms. It does NOT constitute legal advice.**

**Key Points**:
- Grievance portals are administrative remedies, not legal forums
- Resolution depends on department's response (not guaranteed)
- For serious violations, legal remedies (RTI, writ) may be needed
- Corruption complaints require special channels (ACB, CVC, CBI)

**Use grievance portals as first step for service delivery issues.**

---

**Last Updated**: 2025-12-05

**Critical Resource**: CPGRAMS (pgportal.gov.in)

# Right to Information Act 2005 - Fundamentals

## Purpose
Understand the basic framework of RTI Act 2005, who can file RTI applications, what information can be requested, and fundamental principles of transparency and accountability in government.

## Activation Triggers
Use this protocol when:
- Filing first RTI application
- Understanding scope of RTI Act
- Determining if information qualifies under RTI
- Assessing if body is "public authority"
- Educating about citizen's right to information

---

## Legal Framework

### Constitutional Foundation

**Article 19(1)(a) - Freedom of Speech and Expression**:
Supreme Court in *S.P. Gupta v. Union of India* (1982) held right to information is fundamental right flowing from Article 19(1)(a).

**Rationale**: Informed citizenry is prerequisite for participative democracy. Citizens cannot make informed choices without access to government information.

### Right to Information Act, 2005

**Enactment**: October 12, 2005 (effective)
**Objective**: Promote transparency and accountability in government functioning by providing citizens access to information.

---

## Key Definitions

### 1. Information (Section 2(f))

**Statutory Definition**:
> "Information means any material in any form, including records, documents, memos, e-mails, opinions, advises, press releases, circulars, orders, logbooks, contracts, reports, papers, samples, models, data material held in any electronic form and information relating to any private body which can be accessed by a public authority under any other law for the time being in force."

**Key Elements**:
- **Any material**: Physical or digital
- **Any form**: Documents, emails, data, models, samples
- **Electronic form included**: Databases, digital files
- **Private body information**: If accessible by public authority under law

**Examples of "Information"**:
- Government files, notings, minutes
- Tender documents, contracts
- Environmental clearance applications
- Police FIRs, investigation reports
- Municipal building plans, NOCs
- CCTV footage (if held by public authority)
- Emails between government officials
- Consultant reports commissioned by government

**NOT "Information"** (Exemptions - Section 8):
- Covered separately in CIVIC-RTI-PROCEDURE (exemptions)

---

### 2. Public Authority (Section 2(h))

**Statutory Definition**:
> "Public authority means any authority or body or institution of self-government established or constituted—
> (a) by or under the Constitution;
> (b) by any other law made by Parliament;
> (c) by any other law made by State Legislature;
> (d) by notification issued or order made by the appropriate Government,
> and includes any—
> (i) body owned, controlled or substantially financed;
> (ii) non-Government organisation substantially financed, directly or indirectly by funds provided by the appropriate Government."

**Categories**:

**Category A: Constitutional Bodies**
- Supreme Court, High Courts
- Election Commission
- Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG)
- Union Public Service Commission (UPSC)
- State Public Service Commissions

**Category B: Statutory Bodies**
- Central/State Government Departments
- Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs): ONGC, BHEL, Indian Railways
- Regulatory Authorities: SEBI, TRAI, RBI
- Universities (established by statute)

**Category C: Funded Bodies**
- NGOs receiving substantial government funding (>95% threshold per CIC rulings)
- Private schools receiving government grants
- Private hospitals empaneled under government schemes

**Category D: Self-Government Institutions**
- Gram Panchayats
- Municipal Corporations
- Cantonment Boards

**Important Rulings**:

*CBSE v. Aditya Bandopadhyay* (2011) 8 SCC 497:
CBSE is public authority. However, answer sheets are fiduciary relationship with student - not disclosed under RTI.

*Thalappalam Service Cooperative Bank v. State of Kerala* (2013) 16 SCC 82:
Cooperative societies NOT public authorities unless substantially funded by government.

---

### 3. Right to Information (Section 2(j))

**Statutory Definition**:
> "Right to information means the right to information accessible under this Act which is held by or under the control of any public authority and includes the right to—
> (i) inspection of work, documents, records;
> (ii) taking notes, extracts or certified copies of documents or records;
> (iii) taking certified samples of material;
> (iv) obtaining information in the form of diskettes, floppies, tapes, video cassettes or in any other electronic mode or through printouts where such information is stored in a computer or in any other device."

**Rights Included**:
1. **Inspection**: Visit office, view files physically
2. **Copies**: Certified/plain copies
3. **Notes/Extracts**: Handwritten notes from files
4. **Samples**: If information in material form (e.g., water quality sample)
5. **Electronic Format**: Data in digital form (CSV, PDF, etc.)

---

## Who Can File RTI?

### Section 3: Right to Information

**Statutory Text**:
> "Subject to the provisions of this Act, all citizens shall have the right to information."

**Eligibility**:
- **ANY citizen of India**
- No educational qualification required
- No age limit (minor can file through guardian)
- No need to show locus standi (personal interest)
- No need to give reasons for seeking information

**Case Law**:
*Institute of Chartered Accountants of India v. Shaunak H. Satya* (2011):
Applicant need NOT give reasons for seeking information. RTI Act does not require applicant to prove "need to know."

**Who CANNOT File RTI**:
- Non-citizens (foreigners, NRIs who renounced citizenship)
- Companies, LLPs, societies (only natural persons - citizens)

**Practical Note**: NRIs who retain Indian citizenship CAN file RTI.

---

## What Information Can Be Requested?

### Section 4: Obligations of Public Authorities

**Mandatory Disclosure** (Suo Moto):
Every public authority must publish 17 categories of information (Section 4(1)(b)):

1. **Particulars of organization, functions, duties**
2. **Powers and duties of officers and employees**
3. **Procedure for decision-making**
4. **Norms for discharge of functions**
5. **Rules, regulations, instructions, manuals, records for public**
6. **Documents held or under control**
7. **Policy formation process**
8. **Directory of officers**
9. **Monthly remuneration of officers**
10. **Budget allocation, plans, proposed expenditure**
11. **Subsidy programs (beneficiaries)**
12. **Concessions, permits, authorizations**
13. **Grant recipients**
14. **Tender information**
15. **Land/property allotment**
16. **Board, council, committee, body members**
17. **Any other information prescribed**

**Compliance**: Must be published on website, updated regularly.

**Benefit**: If already published (Section 4 compliance), citizen gets information without RTI application. If not published, can file RTI.

---

### Types of Information Requestable

**A. Government Decisions and Files**
- Cabinet decisions
- File notings (opinions of officers)
- Meeting minutes
- Policy drafts

**B. Financial Information**
- Budget documents
- Expenditure statements
- Audit reports
- Contractor payments
- Subsidy beneficiaries

**C. Project Information**
- Infrastructure project DPRs (Detailed Project Reports)
- Environmental clearances
- Land acquisition proceedings
- Tender evaluation reports

**D. Personal Information** (About Self):
- Service records (government employees)
- Medical records (government hospitals)
- Exam answer sheets (with caveats per CBSE case)

**E. Third-Party Information**:
- Contractor performance
- NGO utilization certificates
- University accreditation reports

---

### Exemptions (Section 8 - Brief Overview)

Information **CANNOT** be obtained if falls under exemptions:
1. National security, sovereignty
2. Cabinet papers, cabinet decisions (except post-decision)
3. Trade secrets, commercial confidence
4. Fiduciary relationship (e.g., banker-customer, doctor-patient)
5. Information from foreign governments in confidence
6. Endangers life or safety
7. Impedes investigation or prosecution
8. Legislature privilege
9. Contempt of court
10. Personal information (unrelated to public activity)

**Detailed Analysis**: CIVIC-RTI-PROCEDURE protocol

**Public Interest Override (Section 8(2))**:
Even if information falls under exemptions (except (1)), it MUST be disclosed if **public interest in disclosure outweighs harm** from disclosure.

**Example**: Corruption allegations against minister - personal information exemption overridden by public interest.

---

## Fundamental Principles

### 1. Maximum Disclosure, Minimum Exemptions

RTI Act embodies principle: **Government information is PUBLIC information**. Secrecy is exception, transparency is rule.

### 2. No Need for Reasons

Applicant need not:
- Justify why information is needed
- Show personal interest
- Prove public interest
- Explain use of information

**Rationale**: Right to information is fundamental right. Citizen does not need to justify exercising fundamental right.

### 3. Time-Bound Disposal

- **30 days**: Normal cases (Section 7(1))
- **48 hours**: Life or liberty matters (Section 7(1) proviso)
- **Additional 5 days**: If third party involved (Section 11)

Failure to respond within time = deemed refusal (Section 7(1))

### 4. Low Cost Access

- **Application fee**: ₹10 (Central), varies by state
- **Information fee**: ₹2 per page (A4/A3 size)
- **Inspection**: First hour free, ₹5 per additional 15 minutes
- **Samples**: Actual cost

**Below Poverty Line (BPL) applicants**: NO fees

### 5. Decentralized Mechanism

- **Public Information Officer (PIO)**: Receives applications, provides information
- **First Appellate Authority (FAA)**: Reviews PIO decisions
- **Central/State Information Commission (CIC/SIC)**: Final appellate authority

Every public authority must designate PIOs and FAAs (Section 5).

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Municipal Building Permission

**Question**: Can I get information about my neighbor's building plan approval?

**Analysis**:
- **Information**: Building plan, NOC, approval order (Section 2(f) - documents) ✓
- **Public Authority**: Municipal Corporation (Section 2(h) - self-government institution) ✓
- **Right**: Certified copy of building approval (Section 2(j)) ✓
- **Exemption Check**: No exemption (not personal information - relates to public activity of construction) ✓
- **Conclusion**: **YES**, RTI can be filed to Municipal Corporation PIO

### Example 2: Private School Fee Structure

**Question**: Can I get fee structure of private school (not aided)?

**Analysis**:
- **Public Authority Check**: Private unaided school - NOT public authority (no substantial government funding) ✗
- **Conclusion**: **NO**, RTI Act does not apply to private unaided schools

**Alternative**: If school affiliated to CBSE/ICSE, file RTI to Board asking for fee structure (as Board has regulatory power over affiliated schools).

### Example 3: Police Investigation Details

**Question**: Can I get copy of FIR filed by me?

**Analysis**:
- **Information**: FIR (Section 2(f) - document) ✓
- **Public Authority**: Police (Section 2(h) - government department) ✓
- **Right**: Certified copy (Section 2(j)) ✓
- **Exemption Check**: FIR is NOT exempt (it's public document). However, investigation details (chargesheet before filing) may be exempt under Section 8(1)(h) (impede investigation) ⚠
- **Conclusion**: **YES** for FIR copy. Investigation status MAY be provided if not impeding investigation.

**Practical Note**: Supreme Court has held FIR must be provided to complainant. RTI is alternative mechanism if police delay.

### Example 4: Government Tender Information

**Question**: Can I get details of tenders floated by PWD?

**Analysis**:
- **Information**: Tender documents, bidder names, evaluation reports (Section 2(f)) ✓
- **Public Authority**: Public Works Department (Section 2(h)) ✓
- **Exemption Check**:
  - **Pre-award**: Bidder identities, commercial bids MAY be exempt (Section 8(1)(d) - commercial confidence) ⚠
  - **Post-award**: Winning bid, contract terms, payment details - NOT exempt (public money involved) ✓
- **Conclusion**:
  - **Pre-award**: Partial disclosure (technical specs - yes, bidder details - may be refused)
  - **Post-award**: **FULL disclosure** (except trade secrets in contract)

---

## Strategic Tips for RTI Success

### 1. Be Specific

**Poor RTI**: "Give me all information about XYZ project"
**Good RTI**: "Provide copy of (1) DPR dated [date], (2) Environmental clearance letter, (3) Tender evaluation report for contract awarded to [contractor name]"

**Why**: Vague requests → PIO confusion → delay/rejection. Specific requests → clear response.

### 2. Identify Correct Public Authority

**Wrong**: File RTI about land records to Municipal Corporation
**Right**: File to Revenue Department (land records maintained by Tehsildar/Sub-Registrar)

**Research**: Check which department holds the information (visit office or check website)

### 3. Pay Correct Fee

**Fee varies**: Central (₹10), State-wise variation (Maharashtra ₹5, Delhi ₹10, etc.)
**Payment**: Cash/DD/IPO/Online (as per authority)
**BPL**: Attach BPL card copy, write "BPL applicant - no fee"

### 4. Use Section 6(3) Transfer Provision

If file RTI to **wrong** public authority:
- **PIO must transfer** to correct authority within 5 days (Section 6(3))
- Must inform applicant of transfer

**Benefit**: Even if wrong authority, application not rejected - transferred.

### 5. Cite Section 7(1) Proviso for Urgency

If information concerns **life or liberty**:
- **48-hour response** mandatory
- Mention in application: "This concerns life/liberty - response required within 48 hours u/s 7(1) proviso"

**Examples**: Medical emergency, illegal detention, food contamination

---

## Protocol Cross-References

**Next Steps**:
- **CIVIC-RTI-PROCEDURE**: How to file RTI application, format, timelines, appeals
- **CIVIC-RTI-PENALTIES**: Penalties for non-compliance, compensation, remedies

**Related**:
- **CIVIC-WRIT-BASICS**: If RTI denied, writ petition u/s Article 226 (alternative remedy)
- **CIVIC-PUBLIC-GRIEVANCE**: CPGRAMS for general government grievances (non-RTI)

---

## Limitations and Disclaimers

**This protocol provides information about RTI Act 2005. It does NOT constitute legal advice.**

**Key Points**:
1. **State Variations**: Some states have specific RTI rules (fee, procedure variations)
2. **Evolving Jurisprudence**: Courts continuously interpret RTI provisions
3. **PIO Discretion**: PIOs may refuse information citing exemptions - appeal available
4. **Third-Party Rights**: Some information may involve third-party privacy/commercial interests
5. **Professional Verification**: For complex RTI matters, consult RTI activists or lawyers

**RTI is citizen's tool for transparency. Use it responsibly.**

---
