- Part I (Sections 1-43): Domestic and international arbitrations seated in India
- Part II (Sections 44-60): Enforcement of foreign awards (New York Convention 1958, Geneva Convention 1927)
- Part III (Sections 61-66): Conciliation procedures

### 1.3 Major Amendments

**2015 Amendment (246th Law Commission Report)**:
- Section 2(2): Excluded Part I applicability to international commercial arbitrations seated outside India
- Section 11(6): Time limit for appointing arbitrator (30/60 days)
- Section 12: Mandatory disclosure of conflicts, Schedule V/VII grounds
- Section 17: Interim measures by tribunal enforceable as court orders
- Section 29-A: 12-month timeline for award (extendable 6 months)
- Section 34: Narrowed scope of challenge (patent illegality limited to domestic awards)
- Section 36: Automatic stay on award removed (need to apply separately)

**2019 Amendment**:
- Section 11(6-A): Arbitral institutions for appointment
- Section 42-A: Confidentiality obligation
- Section 43-J: Arbitration Council of India (ACI) establishment
- Eighth Schedule: Quality requirements for arbitral institutions

**2021 Amendment**:
- Section 36(3): Automatic stay if deposit waived for small value claims
- Section 43-J: ACI constitution and powers clarified
- Schedule: Qualifications and accreditation of arbitrators

---

## 2. SCOPE AND APPLICATION

### 2.1 Part I Application (Section 2(2))

**Applies to**:
- ✅ Domestic arbitrations (Indian parties, Indian seat)
- ✅ International commercial arbitrations **seated in India**
- ✅ Interim measures by Indian courts for arbitrations seated abroad (Section 9, if parties agree)

**Does NOT apply to**:
- ❌ International commercial arbitrations **seated outside India** (BALCO doctrine)
- ❌ Statutory arbitrations (unless parties agree - Section 2(3))

**Seat vs. Venue Distinction**:
- **Seat**: Legal/juridical home of arbitration, determines curial law (Part I applicable)
- **Venue**: Geographic location of hearings (may differ from seat)

**Determining Seat** (*BGS SGS SOMA JV v. NHPC Ltd.* 2020):
1. Express designation in agreement
2. Rules of arbitral institution chosen
3. Place of arbitration clause (rebuttable presumption)
4. Circumstances (location of cause of action, subject matter)

### 2.2 International Commercial Arbitration (Section 2(1)(f))

**Definition**: At least one party is:
- Individual who is national/resident of country other than India, OR
- Body corporate incorporated in country other than India, OR
- Company/association/body managed from outside India, OR
- Government of foreign country

**AND** dispute arises out of **commercial relationship** (Section 2(1)(c))

**Commercial Relationship** (broadly defined):
- Trade, commerce, supply of goods/services
- Investment, financing, banking
- Joint ventures, licensing
- Intellectual property
- Construction contracts
- Professional services
- Exploration/extraction of natural resources

### 2.3 Exclusions and Carve-Outs

**Non-Arbitrable Disputes** (Evolving Jurisprudence):

**Traditionally Non-Arbitrable**:
- ❌ **Criminal offenses** (prosecution, punishment)
- ❌ **Matrimonial disputes** (divorce, custody) - *A. Ayyasamy v. A. Paramasivam* (2016)
- ❌ **Guardianship** under Guardians and Wards Act
- ❌ **Insolvency and winding up** (corporate status questions) - BUT moratorium disputes arbitrable
- ❌ **Testamentary matters** (will validity, probate)
- ❌ **Tenancy governed by rent control laws** (eviction)

**Now Arbitrable (Expanded Scope)**:
- ✅ **Fraud allegations** if incidental to contract dispute (*Avitel Post Studioz v. HSBC PI Holdings* 2021 SC)
- ✅ **IP disputes** (infringement, licensing) - *Eros International v. Telemax* (2016)
- ✅ **Competition Act violations** if civil consequences of contract (*Vimal Kishor Shah v. Jayesh Dinesh Shah* 2016)
- ✅ **Shareholder disputes** (except oppression/mismanagement under Companies Act)
- ✅ **FEMA violations** (civil/contractual aspects)
- ✅ **Consumer disputes** (if bilateral arbitration clause, not adhesion contract)

**Test for Arbitrability** (*Booz Allen & Hamilton v. SBI Home Finance* 2011):
1. Does dispute involve **rights in personam** (between parties)? ✅ Arbitrable
2. Does dispute involve **rights in rem** (status/third-party rights)? ❌ Non-arbitrable

---

## 3. FOUNDATIONAL PRINCIPLES

### 3.1 Party Autonomy (Section 19(1))

**Freedom to determine**:
- Procedural rules (subject to Part I mandatory provisions)
- Rules of evidence
- Language of proceedings
- Seat and venue

**Mandatory Provisions (Cannot Be Contracted Out)**:
- Section 9: Interim measures by court
- Section 12: Independence and impartiality disclosure
- Section 16: Kompetenz-kompetenz (tribunal decides own jurisdiction)
- Section 17: Interim measures by tribunal
- Section 28: Tribunal to decide on equity if parties agree
- Section 29-A: 12-month timeline (extendable)
- Section 34: Challenge to award
- Section 36: Enforcement of award

### 3.2 Minimal Judicial Intervention (Section 5)

**"Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, in matters governed by this Part, no judicial authority shall intervene except where so provided in this Part."**

**Limited Grounds for Court Intervention**:
1. **Section 8**: Refer parties to arbitration if valid agreement exists
2. **Section 9**: Interim measures before/during arbitration
3. **Section 11**: Appointment of arbitrator if mechanism fails
4. **Section 14**: Termination of arbitrator mandate
5. **Section 27**: Court assistance in taking evidence
6. **Section 34**: Challenge to award (narrow grounds)
7. **Section 37**: Appeal from Section 34 order

**Landmark Case - *SBP & Co. v. Patel Engineering Ltd.* (2005)**:
- Chief Justice of India (CJI) not bound by Section 5 (inherent administrative powers)
- Judicial authority = courts, NOT tribunals
- Arbitration inherently involves "judicial authority" acting under statute

### 3.3 Kompetenz-Kompetenz (Section 16)

**Principle**: Arbitral tribunal can rule on its own jurisdiction, including objections to:
- Existence or validity of arbitration agreement
- Scope of arbitrable disputes

**Section 16(1)**: Tribunal may rule on own jurisdiction

**Section 16(2)**: Plea of no jurisdiction must be raised **no later than submission of statement of defence**

**Section 16(3)**: Tribunal decides jurisdiction plea as preliminary question OR in final award

**Section 16(5)**: Tribunal decision on jurisdiction **NOT appealable** (challenge only under Section 34 after final award)

**Severability/Separability Doctrine (Section 16(1)(a))**:
- Arbitration clause treated as **independent agreement**
- Invalidity of main contract does NOT automatically invalidate arbitration clause
- Tribunal can adjudicate validity of main contract

**Example**: Contract void for fraud. Arbitration clause survives—tribunal can decide if fraud vitiates contract.

### 3.4 Seat-Centric Framework (BALCO Doctrine)

**BALCO v. Kaiser Aluminum (2012) - Paradigm Shift**:

**Pre-BALCO (Bhatia International Regime)**:
- Part I applied to ALL arbitrations involving Indian parties (even if seated abroad)
- Indian courts had jurisdiction for interim measures, appointment, challenge

**Post-BALCO (Current Law)**:
- Part I applies ONLY if seat is in India
- Seat determines curial law (law governing arbitration)
- Foreign-seated arbitrations: Part II only (enforcement of foreign awards)

**Determining Factor**: **Seat, not nationality of parties**

**Practical Implication**:
- Singapore-seated arbitration (Indian parties): Singapore Arbitration Act governs, Indian courts NO jurisdiction under Part I
- Mumbai-seated arbitration (foreign parties): Indian A&C Act Part I governs, Indian courts have jurisdiction

---

## 4. KEY STATUTORY PROVISIONS

### 4.1 Section 7: Arbitration Agreement

**Definition (Section 7(1))**: Agreement to submit present or future disputes to arbitration

**Forms (Section 7(3))**:
- In writing (document signed by parties)
- Exchange of letters, fax, email, electronic communication
- Exchange of pleadings in which arbitration clause alleged and not denied
- Reference in contract to document containing arbitration clause

**Incorporation by Reference (Section 7(5))**:
- Valid if main contract refers to arbitration clause in another document (e.g., charter party incorporating bill of lading terms)

**Pathological Clauses**: Defective arbitration clauses (vague institutions, contradictory rules)
- Courts adopt **principle of effectiveness** (give effect to arbitration intent if possible)
- *Inox Renewables v. Jayesh Electricals* (2021): Court-assisted interpretation to save agreement

### 4.2 Section 8: Power to Refer Parties to Arbitration

**Mandatory Reference**:
"A judicial authority, before which an action is brought in a matter which is the subject of an arbitration agreement shall, if a party to the arbitration agreement or any person claiming through or under him, so applies not later than the date of submitting his first statement on the substance of the dispute, then, notwithstanding any judgment, decree or order of the Supreme Court or any Court, refer the parties to arbitration unless it finds that prima facie no valid arbitration agreement exists."

**Stage for Application**: **Before submitting first statement on substance of dispute** (first written statement in suit)

**Standard of Review**: **Prima facie** existence of arbitration agreement (NOT detailed inquiry)

**Effect**: Judicial proceedings stayed/terminated, parties referred to arbitration

**Non-Signatory Binding** (*Chloro Controls (I) P. Ltd. v. Severn Trent Water Purification* 2013):
- Group of companies doctrine
- Conduct implying consent
- Assignment/succession

### 4.3 Section 9: Interim Measures by Court

**Before or During Arbitral Proceedings**:
Court may grant interim measures:
- Preservation, interim custody, sale of goods
- Securing amount in dispute
- Detention, preservation, inspection of property/documents
- Interim injunction
- Appointment of receiver
- Other interim measures (Section 9(1)(ii)(e))

**Post-Award**: Section 9 NOT available after award made (use Section 36 enforcement)

**Conditions**:
- Prima facie arbitration agreement exists
- Urgency/balance of convenience
- Court satisfied measure necessary

**Enforcement**: Section 9 order executable as court decree

**Alternative**: Section 17 interim measures by tribunal (enforceable as court order under Section 17(2))

### 4.4 Section 11: Appointment of Arbitrators

**Appointment Mechanism**:
1. **Parties' agreement** (Section 11(2)): Follow agreed procedure
2. **Default procedure** (Section 11(3)):
   - Sole arbitrator: Joint appointment by parties (30 days)
   - Three arbitrators: Each party appoints one, two appoint third (presiding)
3. **Failure of mechanism** (Section 11(4)-(6)): Court/designated arbitral institution appoints

**Designated Authority** (Pre-2015: Chief Justice; Post-2015: Designated Judge/Arbitral Institution):
- Supreme Court: Justice nominated by CJI
- High Court: Justice nominated by Chief Justice of HC
- Arbitral institution (if parties agree under Section 11(6-A))

**Timelines** (Section 11(13)):
- Application for appointment: Dispose within **60 days**

**Qualifications** (Section 11(8)):
- Person of any nationality (unless parties agree otherwise)
- For international commercial arbitration: Parties free to determine qualifications

**Ineligibility** (Schedule VII added by 2015 Amendment):
- Employee, consultant, advisor to party
- Manager, director, shareholder of party (substantial interest)
- Relationship that creates reasonable doubts about independence

### 4.5 Section 12: Disclosure and Challenge

**Mandatory Disclosure (Section 12(1))**:
Arbitrator must disclose in writing:
- Any circumstances likely to give rise to **justifiable doubts** as to independence or impartiality
- Disclosure continues throughout arbitration

**Challenge Grounds (Section 12(3) + Schedule V/VII)**:

**Schedule V (Grounds for Ineligibility)**:
1. Relationship/interest (employee, shareholder, advisor to party)
2. Prior involvement as representative, expert in same dispute
3. Currently advising party on dispute

**Schedule VII (Grounds for Reasonable Doubts)**:
1. Financial interest in outcome
2. Business relationship with party
3. Regular advice to party (though not in this dispute)
4. Previous expression of views on merits
5. Arbitrator appointed unilaterally by one party if institution not involved

**Procedure for Challenge (Section 13)**:
- Written statement of reasons within **15 days** of becoming aware
- If arbitrator doesn't withdraw or other party doesn't agree, arbitral tribunal decides
- If challenge rejected, party can apply to court within **30 days** of tribunal decision

### 4.6 Section 29-A: Time Limit for Award

**12-Month Deadline** (from date of completion of pleadings):
- Arbitral tribunal shall make award within 12 months
- Extendable by **6 months** by consent of parties
- Beyond 18 months: Court may extend (sufficient cause)

**Consequences of Delay**:
- Arbitrator's mandate may be terminated (Section 14(1)(a)) if unreasonable delay
- Arbitrator's fees cease after 12 months (unless extended)

**Exclusions** (Time not counted):
- Proceedings under Section 17 (interim measures by tribunal)

**Rationale**: Prevent indefinite arbitration, maintain arbitration as speedy alternative

### 4.7 Section 34: Application for Setting Aside Award

**Grounds for Challenge (Section 34(2))**:

**Sub-Section (a) - Party Proving**:
1. **Incapacity** (Section 34(2)(a)(i))
2. **Arbitration agreement invalid** under applicable law (Section 34(2)(a)(i))
3. **Lack of notice** or unable to present case (Section 34(2)(a)(ii))
4. **Award beyond scope** of submission to arbitration (Section 34(2)(a)(iii))
5. **Composition or procedure not per agreement** (Section 34(2)(a)(iv))

**Sub-Section (b) - Court Finding**:
1. **Subject matter not arbitrable** (Section 34(2)(b)(i))
2. **Award in conflict with public policy of India** (Section 34(2)(b)(ii)):
   - **Patent illegality** appearing on face of award (domestic awards only - post-2015)
   - Award induced by fraud/corruption
   - Award in contravention with fundamental policy of Indian law
   - Award in conflict with basic notions of morality/justice

**Patent Illegality (Explanation 1 to Section 34(2)(b)(ii))**:
- Contravention of substantive law OR
- Contravention of Limitation Act 1963 OR
- Award is perverse/irrational

**Clarification**: Patent illegality NOT grounds for foreign awards (Section 48) or international commercial arbitrations seated in India (conflicting precedents)

**Procedure**:
- Application within **3 months** of receipt of award (extendable 30 days)
- Court may remand to tribunal OR set aside award

**Standard**: **Narrow judicial review** (not appeal on merits)

### 4.8 Section 36: Enforcement of Award

**Post-2015 Amendment**: Automatic stay removed

**Current Position**:
- Award enforceable as decree (Section 36(1))
- If Section 34 application filed: Automatic stay **removed**
- Need to apply separately for stay under Section 36(2)

**Conditions for Stay (Section 36(3))**:
- Prima facie case for setting aside award
- Balance of convenience/irreparable injury
- May require deposit/security

**Exception (2021 Amendment - Section 36(3))**:
- Automatic stay if court waives deposit for "reasons to be recorded"

**Effect**: Award creditor can execute during pendency of Section 34 (unless stay granted)

---

## 5. INSTITUTIONAL VS. AD HOC ARBITRATION

### 5.1 Institutional Arbitration

**Arbitral Institutions**:
- MCIA (Mumbai Centre for International Arbitration)
- DIAC (Delhi International Arbitration Centre)
- ICA (International Centre for ADR, ICADR)
- SIAC (Singapore International Arbitration Centre) - foreign, often chosen
- ICC (International Chamber of Commerce) - foreign
- LCIA (London Court of International Arbitration) - foreign

**Advantages**:
- Administered by institution (case management, timelines)
- Pre-established rules (MCIA Rules, SIAC Rules, ICC Rules)
- Panel of arbitrators (expertise, vetted)
- Scrutiny of awards (institutional review)
- Logistical support (hearing venues, secretariat)

**Costs**: Higher administrative fees + arbitrator fees

### 5.2 Ad Hoc Arbitration

**Definition**: Parties create own procedure, appoint arbitrators directly

**Applicable Rules** (if parties choose):
- UNCITRAL Arbitration Rules 2013 (commonly adopted)
- Bespoke rules agreed by parties

**Advantages**:
- Flexibility (tailor procedure to dispute)
- Lower administrative costs (no institutional fees)
- Direct control by parties

**Disadvantages**:
- No institutional support (parties manage logistics)
- Risk of procedural deadlock
- Arbitrator appointment delays if mechanism fails

**Hybrid**: Ad hoc with institutional appointment (e.g., ad hoc arbitration, MCIA appoints arbitrator under Section 11(6-A))

---

## 6. COSTS AND FEES

### 6.1 Arbitrator's Fees

**No Statutory Schedule** (unlike court fees)

**Determination**:
- Agreement between parties and arbitrator
- Institutional rules (if institutional arbitration)
- Court determination (if dispute arises)

**Section 31(8)**: Award to fix costs (including arbitrator fees)

**Section 31-A**: Tribunal may order costs based on frivolous/vexatious claims/defenses

**Fourth Schedule (2015 Amendment)**: Model fee schedule (not binding)
- Based on sum in dispute (sliding scale)
- Example: ₹1 crore dispute → ₹3-5 lakhs arbitrator fee (approx.)

### 6.2 Costs of Arbitration

**Recoverable Costs** (Section 31(8)):
- Arbitrator fees and expenses
- Legal fees and expenses
- Administrative fees (institutional arbitration)
- Expert witness fees
- Venue costs

**Costs Follow the Event**: Generally, losing party pays costs (tribunal has discretion)

**Frivolous Claims** (Section 31-A): Costs may include exemplary costs if claim/defense frivolous

---

## 7. ARBITRATION COUNCIL OF INDIA (ACI)

**Section 43-J (2019 Amendment)**: Establishment of ACI

**Functions**:
- Grade arbitral institutions (Section 43-K)
- Maintain panel of accredited arbitrators
- Establish norms for costs/fees
- Promote/facilitate ADR
- Maintain depository of arbitral awards (Section 42-B)

**Constitution** (2021 Amendment):
- Chairperson, 2 academics, 1 High Court judge (retired), 5 ex-officio members

**Status**: Notified in 2023, operational from 2024 (gradual rollout)

---

## 8. CRITICAL CASE LAW

### 8.1 Seat and Jurisdiction

**BALCO v. Kaiser Aluminum (2012) 9 SCC 552**:
- Overruled Bhatia International regime
- Part I applies ONLY to India-seated arbitrations
- Prospective overruling (contracts post-06.09.2012)

**BGS SGS SOMA JV v. NHPC Ltd. (2020) 4 SCC 234**:
- Seat vs. venue distinction
- Seat determines curial law
- Four-factor test for determining seat

### 8.2 Arbitrability

**Booz Allen & Hamilton v. SBI Home Finance (2011) 5 SCC 532**:
- Rights in personam arbitrable, rights in rem non-arbitrable

**Avitel Post Studioz v. HSBC PI Holdings (2021) 4 SCC 713**:
- Fraud allegations arbitrable if incidental to contract

**A. Ayyasamy v. A. Paramasivam (2016) 10 SCC 386**:
- Matrimonial disputes non-arbitrable
- Serious fraud allegations may oust arbitration (high threshold)

### 8.3 Judicial Intervention

**SBP & Co. v. Patel Engineering (2005) 8 SCC 618**:
- Section 11 appointment is administrative (not judicial), but post-2015 involves prima facie review

**Duro Felguera v. Gangavaram Port (2017) 9 SCC 729**:
- Limited review under Section 11 (existence of arbitration agreement, not detailed merits)

**Ssangyong Engineering v. NHAI (2019) 15 SCC 131**:
- Public policy confined to fundamental policy, justice, morality
- Patent illegality for domestic awards only
- Narrow scope of Section 34

### 8.4 Interim Measures

**Alka Chandewar v. Shamshul Ishrar Khan (2017) 16 SCC 119**:
- Section 9 not available after award made (pre-Section 36 enforcement)

**Board of Control for Cricket in India v. Kochhar (2018) 10 SCC 337**:
- Section 17 interim order enforceable as court order under Section 17(2)

---

## 9. MODERN DEVELOPMENTS

### 9.1 Emergency Arbitration

**Mechanism**: Urgent interim relief before tribunal constituted

**Institutional Rules**:
- SIAC, ICC, LCIA, MCIA have emergency arbitrator provisions
- Emergency arbitrator appointed within 24-48 hours
- Interim order within 7-14 days

**Indian Law Position**:
- **Amazon v. Future Retail (2020)**: Emergency arbitrator order enforceable under Section 17(2) (Delhi HC)
- **PASL v. GE Power (2021)**: Emergency arbitrator order enforceable (Supreme Court affirmed)

**Controversy**: Whether emergency arbitrator is "arbitral tribunal" under Section 2(1)(d) - generally accepted post-Amazon

### 9.2 Virtual Hearings (COVID-19 Impact)

**Section 19(2)** (amended 2020): Arbitral tribunal may hold oral hearings or conduct proceedings on basis of documents/written submissions

**Interpretation**: Includes video conferencing

**Supreme Court Guidelines** (Dr. Sharma v. Sri Sandeep (2020)):
- Consent of parties required for virtual cross-examination
- Technical safeguards necessary

**Institutional Rules**: MCIA, DIAC adopted specific virtual hearing protocols (2020-2021)

### 9.3 Third-Party Funding

**Definition**: Non-party finances arbitration in exchange for share of recovery

**Indian Position**:
- **Bar Council of India** rules prohibit champerty/maintenance by advocates
- **Third-party funders** (non-lawyers, international funders) - NO explicit prohibition
- Disclosure required under Section 12 (if creates reasonable doubt about arbitrator impartiality)

**Growing Practice**: Increased third-party funding in high-value international commercial arbitrations (India-seated)

---

## 10. PRACTICAL GUIDANCE

### 10.1 Drafting Arbitration Clauses

**Essential Elements**:
1. **Scope**: "All disputes arising out of or in connection with this contract"
2. **Number of arbitrators**: Sole OR panel of three (avoid even numbers)
3. **Seat**: "seated in Mumbai, India" (determines curial law)
4. **Governing law**: "governed by Indian law" (substantive law)
5. **Language**: "English" (if international parties)
6. **Institution/Rules** (optional): "administered by MCIA under MCIA Rules 2024"

**Example - Model Clause (Institutional)**:
"Any dispute arising out of or in connection with this contract, including any question regarding its existence, validity or termination, shall be referred to and finally resolved by arbitration administered by the Mumbai Centre for International Arbitration (MCIA) in accordance with the MCIA Arbitration Rules 2024. The seat of arbitration shall be Mumbai, India. The tribunal shall consist of three arbitrators. The language of arbitration shall be English."

**Example - Model Clause (Ad Hoc)**:
"Any dispute arising out of or in connection with this contract shall be referred to arbitration by a sole arbitrator appointed by mutual agreement of the parties. The arbitration shall be seated in Delhi, India and conducted in accordance with the UNCITRAL Arbitration Rules 2013. The language of arbitration shall be English. The substantive law governing the contract shall be Indian law."

### 10.2 Selecting Arbitrators

**Criteria**:
- Subject-matter expertise (construction, IP, banking, etc.)
- Experience (number of arbitrations conducted)
- Availability (time commitment)
- Independence (no conflicts under Schedule V/VII)
- Track record (awards upheld/set aside)

**Sources**:
- Arbitral institution panels (MCIA, DIAC)
- Chartered Institute of Arbitrators (CIArb)
- Professional bodies (ICAI for accounting disputes, Bar associations)

### 10.3 Choosing Between Court and Arbitration

**Prefer Arbitration When**:
- Confidentiality critical (arbitration confidential under Section 42-A)
- International parties (neutrality of forum)
- Technical expertise required (choose expert arbitrator)
- Speed important (12-month timeline under Section 29-A)

**Prefer Litigation When**:
- Need for precedent (arbitration awards not binding precedent)
- Interim relief critical and urgent (court Section 9 may be faster than constituting tribunal)
- Multiple parties/third-party implication (arbitration limited to signatories)
- Low-value disputes (arbitration costs may exceed court fees)

---

## 11. LIMITATIONS AND EXCEPTIONS

### 11.1 Non-Arbitrable Disputes
See Section 2.3 above (criminal, matrimonial, insolvency status, testamentary)

### 11.2 Statutory Arbitrations
**Section 2(3)**: A&C Act provisions apply to statutory arbitrations ONLY if parties agree

**Examples of Statutory Arbitrations**:
- Industrial Disputes Act 1947
- Payment of Gratuity Act 1972
- Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises Development Act 2006 (MSMED Act)

**MSMED Act Arbitration**: Mandatory reference to MSMED Facilitation Council (Section 18) - deemed arbitration, A&C Act applies with modifications

### 11.3 Public Procurement Contracts

**Arbitration Clause Validity**: Generally valid

**Challenges**:
- Government privilege clauses (unilateral termination)
- Sovereign immunity issues (enforcement against State)

**Board of Control for Cricket in India v. Kochhar (2018)**: Public bodies/PSUs can arbitrate; public policy not violated

---

## 12. INTEGRATION WITH OTHER PROTOCOLS

**Related Protocols**:
- **IL-ADR-002**: Arbitration Agreement and Arbitrability (detailed analysis)
- **IL-ADR-003**: Arbitral Tribunal Constitution and Procedure
- **IL-ADR-004**: Arbitral Award - Making, Challenge, Enforcement
- **IL-ADR-005**: International Commercial Arbitration (UNCITRAL, NY Convention)
- **IL-ADR-006**: Mediation Act 2023
- **IL-CPC-001**: Enforcement as decree (Section 36)
- **IL-LIMITATION-004**: Limitation for arbitration applications

**Cross-Domain**:
- **IL-CONTRACT-007** (Breach remedies): Arbitration as remedy forum
- **IL-COMMERCIAL-001**: Pre-institution mediation (Section 12-A) vs. arbitration

---

## LEGAL DISCLAIMERS

**NOT LEGAL ADVICE**: This protocol provides legal information about Indian arbitration law. It does NOT constitute legal advice. Specific disputes require consultation with licensed advocates familiar with current case law and amendments.

**NO ATTORNEY-CLIENT RELATIONSHIP**: Reading this protocol does not create an attorney-client relationship.

**SEEK PROFESSIONAL CONSULTATION**: Arbitration agreements, tribunal appointments, and award challenges involve significant legal rights and complex procedural rules. Always consult qualified arbitration counsel.

**EVOLVING LAW**: Arbitration law evolves through amendments and judicial interpretation. Verify current statutory position and recent Supreme Court/High Court decisions before relying on this protocol.

---

## REVISION HISTORY
- **v1.0 (December 2024)**: Initial comprehensive protocol covering A&C Act 1996 fundamentals, 2015/2019/2021 amendments, BALCO doctrine, emergency arbitration, virtual hearings, ACI establishment

---

**Word Count**: ~6,200 words
**Depth**: Comprehensive foundational coverage
**Target Audience**: Legal practitioners, in-house counsel, arbitration specialists, law students


---
## IL-ADR-002_arbitration_agreement.md
---

# IL-ADR-002: Arbitration Agreement and Arbitrability

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-ADR-002
- **Domain**: Indian Law - Alternative Dispute Resolution
- **Category**: Arbitration Agreement
- **Version**: 1.0
- **Last Updated**: December 2024
- **Scope**: Section 7 arbitration agreements, validity, pathological clauses, arbitrability analysis
- **Prerequisites**: IL-ADR-001 (Arbitration Fundamentals)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-ADR-003, IL-CONTRACT-001 (offer/acceptance), IL-CONTRACT-003 (consideration)

## Purpose
Comprehensive analysis of arbitration agreements under Section 7 A&C Act 1996, validity requirements, incorporation by reference, pathological clauses, multi-party/multi-contract scenarios, and detailed arbitrability assessment for all dispute categories.

---

## 1. ARBITRATION AGREEMENT - SECTION 7

### 1.1 Definition and Essentials

**Section 7(1)**: "arbitration agreement" means an agreement by the parties to submit to arbitration all or certain disputes which have arisen or which may arise between them in respect of a defined legal relationship, whether contractual or not.

**Essential Elements**:
1. **Agreement** (consensus ad idem - meeting of minds)
2. **To submit disputes** (present or future)
3. **Defined legal relationship** (contractual or non-contractual - e.g., tort, unjust enrichment)
4. **To arbitration** (not mediation, conciliation, or litigation)

**Contractual Nature**: Arbitration agreement is a contract - all contract law principles apply:
- Offer and acceptance (IL-CONTRACT-001)
- Consideration (IL-CONTRACT-003) - usually embedded in main contract
- Capacity to contract (Section 11 ICA)
- Free consent (Section 14 ICA)

### 1.2 Form Requirements (Section 7(3)-(5))

**Written Form Requirement** (Section 7(3)):
An arbitration agreement shall be in writing.

**Satisfies "In Writing"**:
- Document signed by parties
- Exchange of letters, telex, telegrams
- **Electronic communication** (email, WhatsApp, SMS) - Section 7(4)
- Exchange of **statement of claim and defense** in which existence of agreement alleged by one party and not denied by other (Section 7(4)(c))
- Reference in contract to document containing arbitration clause (Section 7(5))

**Electronic Communication** (Section 7(4-A) added 2015):
Includes communication by "data messages" (EDI, email, telegram, telex, telecopy)

**No Stamping Requirement for Validity** (*N.N. Global Mercantile v. Indo Unique Flame* 2021 SC):
- Unstamped arbitration agreement VALID (can be arbitrated)
- BUT not admissible in court proceedings (Section 34/36) until stamp duty paid
- Parties can pay stamp duty during enforcement

### 1.3 Incorporation by Reference (Section 7(5))

**Section 7(5)**: Reference in a contract to a document containing an arbitration clause constitutes an arbitration agreement if the contract is in writing and the reference is such as to make that arbitration clause part of the contract.

**Requirements**:
1. Main contract must be **in writing**
2. Main contract must **refer to** document containing arbitration clause
3. Reference must be **specific** enough to incorporate arbitration clause

**Degree of Incorporation** (Evolving Standards):

**General Reference - Sufficient** (*M.R. Engineers v. Som Datt Builders* 2009):
- "All terms and conditions as per standard form" → Incorporates arbitration clause

**Mere Signature - Insufficient** (*Inox Wind Ltd. v. Thermocables Ltd.* 2018):
- Signing purchase order referencing terms on website without explicit incorporation → NOT sufficient

**Best Practice**: Explicit incorporation language:
"This contract incorporates by reference all terms and conditions of the Master Agreement dated [date], including the arbitration clause contained in Clause [X]."

**Common Scenarios**:
- **Bill of Lading** incorporating charter party arbitration clause ✅
- **Purchase Order** incorporating vendor's standard terms ✅ (if specific)
- **Subcontract** incorporating main contract arbitration ✅ (if explicit)
- **Email** referring to quotation containing arbitration ⚠️ (case-by-case)

---

## 2. VALIDITY CHALLENGES

### 2.1 Grounds for Invalidity

**Section 16(1)(a)**: Arbitration clause can be invalid on grounds applicable to contracts generally (ICA 1872)

**Common Invalidity Grounds**:

**1. Void Agreement (Section 10 ICA)**:
- Lack of free consent (Section 14 ICA - coercion, undue influence, fraud, misrepresentation, mistake)
- Incapacity (minor, unsound mind, disqualified by law)
- Unlawful consideration/object (Section 23 ICA)
- Agreement opposed to public policy

**2. Voidable Agreement (Section 19 ICA)**:
- Consent obtained by coercion, undue influence, fraud, misrepresentation
- Party can choose to void (not automatically void)

**3. Uncertainty/Vagueness** (*R.M. Investment v. Boeing Co.* 1994):
- Arbitration clause so vague that disputes cannot be resolved
- Example: "Disputes resolved amicably" (not arbitration clause)
- BUT: Courts favor validity (*Inox Renewables v. Jayesh Electricals* 2021 - principle of effectiveness)

**4. One-Sided/Unconscionable Clause** (*Centrotrade Minerals v. Hindustan Copper* 2017 SC):
- Clause giving only one party right to arbitrate → Held VALID (party autonomy)
- BUT if agreement of adhesion + grossly unfair → May be unconscionable (rarely struck down)

**5. Unilateral Appointment Power** (*Perkins Eastman Architects v. HSCC* 2020):
- Employer/one party has sole right to appoint arbitrator → Schedule VII ineligibility
- Post-2015: Such clauses survive if arbitrator meets Schedule V/VII independence

**6. Non-Stamped Agreement** (*N.N. Global Mercantile* 2021):
- Valid but inadmissible until stamp duty paid (not void)

### 2.2 Severability Doctrine (Section 16(1)(a))

**Principle**: Arbitration clause treated as **independent agreement** separate from main contract

**Effect**:
- Invalidity of main contract does NOT automatically invalidate arbitration clause
- Tribunal can determine validity of main contract

**Example**:
- Contract alleged to be void for fraud
- Arbitration clause SURVIVES (unless fraud vitiates consent to arbitration itself)
- Tribunal has jurisdiction to decide fraud claim

**Limits**:
- If fraud/coercion goes to **arbitration clause itself** (not just main contract) → Arbitration clause may be invalid
- *A. Ayyasamy v. A. Paramasivam* (2016): Serious fraud allegations involving arbitration clause itself → Court may intervene

### 2.3 Pathological Clauses

**Definition**: Defective arbitration clauses (contradictory, vague, non-existent institutions)

**Examples**:
- "Arbitration under rules of XYZ Arbitration Centre" (XYZ does not exist)
- "Arbitration in London under Indian law administered by ICC" (contradictory seat/institution)
- "Three arbitrators, each party appoints two" (contradictory numbers)

**Judicial Approach - Principle of Effectiveness** (*Inox Renewables v. Jayesh Electricals* 2021):
- Courts should give effect to parties' intention to arbitrate
- Interpret clause to save it (if possible)
- Fill gaps using default provisions (Sections 11, 20, 21)

**Example Resolution**:
- Non-existent institution → Ad hoc arbitration, court appoints under Section 11
- Contradictory seat/venue → Determine intent from contract (seat governs)
- Odd number mismatch → Apply default Section 10(2) (three arbitrators, presiding appointed by party-appointees)

---

## 3. MULTI-PARTY AND MULTI-CONTRACT ARBITRATION

### 3.1 Non-Signatories to Arbitration Agreement

**General Rule**: Only parties to arbitration agreement bound (privity of contract)

**Exceptions - Non-Signatory Binding** (*Chloro Controls v. Severn Trent* 2013 SC):

**1. Group of Companies Doctrine**:
- Parent company signed arbitration agreement
- Subsidiary/affiliate involved in performance
- Single economic unit, mutual intent to bind group
- **Test** (*Cheran Properties v. Kasturi & Sons* 2018):
  - Direct relationship to party signatory
  - Direct commonality of subject matter
  - Composite transaction (performance interlinked)

**2. Alter Ego/Piercing Corporate Veil**:
- Company is mere façade/sham
- Used to evade legal obligations

**3. Agency**:
- Agent signs on behalf of undisclosed principal (Section 230 ICA)
- Ratification by principal

**4. Succession/Assignment**:
- Successor-in-interest to signatory party
- Merger/acquisition (automatic transfer of contract)
- Assignment of contract (with arbitration clause)

**5. Conduct Implying Consent**:
- Non-signatory actively participated in contract performance
- Invoked arbitration clause benefits
- Estoppel from denying arbitration

**6. Third-Party Beneficiary**:
- Contract for benefit of third party (Section 2(d) ICA - stranger to consideration not stranger to contract)

### 3.2 Multi-Contract Arbitration (Consolidation)

**Issue**: Multiple contracts with different (or same) arbitration clauses, related disputes

**No Statutory Provision for Consolidation** in A&C Act 1996 (unlike UNCITRAL Model Law 2006 amendment)

**Institutional Rules Allow Consolidation**:
- SIAC Rules 2016 (Article 8): Tribunal may consolidate if parties agree OR arbitrations arise from same transaction and compatible arbitration agreements
- ICC Rules 2021 (Article 10): Court of ICC may consolidate
- MCIA Rules 2024: Consolidation provisions

**Ad Hoc Arbitration - Consolidation Difficult**:
- Requires consent of all parties
- *Chloro Controls* (2013): Same group of contracts, overlapping issues, same parties → Single arbitration possible

**Common Scenarios**:
- **EPC Contract** + **Subcontracts**: Main contractor-employer arbitration + main contractor-subcontractor arbitration → Separate unless consent
- **Tripartite Agreements**: Bank-Developer-Contractor → May have single arbitration clause binding all three
- **Back-to-Back Contracts**: Supplier-Contractor-Employer chain → Typically separate arbitrations

---

## 4. ARBITRABILITY - COMPREHENSIVE ANALYSIS

### 4.1 Arbitrability Test

**Foundational Case - *Booz Allen & Hamilton v. SBI Home Finance* (2011)**:

**Rights In Personam → Arbitrable**:
- Disputes between specific parties based on their relationship
- Contractual rights, obligations
- Breach of trust, fiduciary duty (between specific parties)

**Rights In Rem → Non-Arbitrable**:
- Disputes affecting status, rights against world at large
- Matrimonial status, insolvency status, winding up (corporate existence)
- Validity of patents (affects public - BUT infringement between parties arbitrable)

**Public Policy Consideration**:
- Would arbitration contravene substantive law?
- Does subject matter require judicial oversight (e.g., criminal prosecution)?

### 4.2 Arbitrable Disputes

**Commercial Contracts** ✅:
- Breach of contract
- Payment disputes
- Specific performance (*Afcons Infrastructure v. Cherian Varkey Construction* 2010)
- Sale of goods
- Construction contracts
- Service agreements

**Tort Claims** ✅ (if parties agree post-dispute or if relationship exists):
- Negligence arising from contract performance
- Professional negligence
- Defamation (civil damages between parties)

**Intellectual Property** ✅:
- **Patent/Trademark/Copyright Infringement** between parties (*Eros International v. Telemax* 2016)
- Licensing disputes
- Royalty disputes
- **Validity of IP rights** ⚠️ (split view - Delhi HC favors arbitrability, Mumbai HC cautious)

**Shareholder Disputes** ✅:
- Breach of shareholders agreement
- Dividend disputes
- Tag-along/drag-along rights
- **NOT Oppression & Mismanagement** under Sections 241-242 Companies Act (NCLT jurisdiction)

**Insolvency-Related Disputes** ⚠️:
- **Existence of debt** ✅ Arbitrable (*Vidya Drolia v. Durga Trading* 2021)
- **Quantum of debt** ✅ Arbitrable
- **Insolvency status** (winding up order, corporate death) ❌ Non-arbitrable (NCLT/Tribunal)

**Fraud Allegations** ✅:
- **Civil consequences of fraud** in contract dispute (*Avitel Post Studioz v. HSBC PI Holdings* 2021 SC)
- Serious, complex fraud with criminal implications ⚠️ (case-by-case, *A. Ayyasamy* doctrine)

**Competition Law** ✅:
- **Civil consequences** of anti-competitive agreements (*Vimal Kishor Shah v. Jayesh Dinesh Shah* 2016)
- Competition Commission of India (CCI) jurisdiction concurrent (NOT ousted)

**Consumer Disputes** ⚠️:
- **Bilateral arbitration clause** (negotiated) ✅ (*Emaar MGF Land v. Aftab Singh* 2019 SC)
- **Adhesion contract** (standard form, no negotiation) ❌ May be unfair trade practice (Consumer Protection Act 2019 bars unilateral arbitration clauses)

**Real Estate (RERA Disputes)** ⚠️:
- **Pre-RERA contracts** ✅ Arbitrable
- **Post-RERA** ⚠️ (concurrent jurisdiction - RERA or arbitration, allottee's choice)
- *M/s Emaar MGF Land* (2019): RERA not bar arbitration, but allottee may choose forum

**Employment Disputes** ✅:
- **Termination/wrongful dismissal** ✅ (private sector)
- **Bonus/incentive disputes** ✅
- **Government employees** ❌ (service law, administrative law - tribunal/court jurisdiction)

**MSME Payment Disputes** ⚠️:
- MSMED Act Section 18 arbitration mandatory (but A&C Act applies as modified)
- Deemed arbitration, enforceable under A&C Act

### 4.3 Non-Arbitrable Disputes

**Criminal Offenses** ❌:
- Prosecution, punishment, criminal liability
- BUT civil damages/compensation from same facts ✅ (*Aftab Singh v. Emaar MGF* 2017)

**Matrimonial Disputes** ❌ (*A. Ayyasamy v. A. Paramasivam* 2016):
- Divorce, judicial separation
- Custody, guardianship
- Matrimonial status (rights in rem)
- **BUT financial settlement/property division** ✅ (rights in personam)

**Testamentary/Succession** ❌:
- Validity of will (probate jurisdiction)
- Succession rights under personal law
- **BUT family settlement/partition** ✅ (if consensual)

**Insolvency Status** ❌:
- Winding up orders (corporate death)
- Bankruptcy adjudication (individual)
- Administered by NCLT/DRT (exclusive jurisdiction)

**Eviction under Rent Control Laws** ❌ (*Himangni Enterprises v. Kamaljeet Singh Ahluwalia* 2017):
- Statutory tenancy under rent control → Rent Controller jurisdiction
- **BUT contractual tenancy** (license agreements, lease) ✅

**Trust Disputes (Public/Charitable Trusts)** ❌:
- Validity of trust, trustee removal (public interest)
- **Private trust** disputes between beneficiaries ✅

**Validity of Subordinate Legislation** ❌:
- Constitutionality, vires of statutory rules
- Courts' exclusive domain

### 4.4 Vidya Drolia Test (2021) - Four-Fold Analysis

**Supreme Court in *Vidya Drolia v. Durga Trading Corporation* (2021) established structured test**:

**When Court Examines Arbitrability (Section 11 or Section 8)**:
Limited prima facie review at reference stage, detailed review under Section 34 post-award

**Four-Fold Test**:

**1. Nature of Dispute**:
- Rights in personam ✅ OR rights in rem ❌?
- Contractual ✅ OR status-based ❌?

**2. Statute Creates Special Right/Liability**:
- Is dispute governed by special statute (RERA, Consumer Act, Companies Act)?
- Does statute oust civil court jurisdiction?
- Is there exclusive statutory forum (NCLT, NCDRC, RERA Authority)?
- **If yes**, arbitration may be impliedly barred ❌
- **If concurrent jurisdiction**, arbitration may survive ✅

**3. Public Policy**:
- Would arbitration of dispute violate fundamental policy of Indian law?
- Does subject matter require judicial oversight (e.g., parens patriae for minors)?

**4. Remedies Available**:
- Can arbitral tribunal grant remedy sought? (e.g., criminal punishment ❌, monetary damages ✅)
- Does remedy require sovereign/regulatory power?

**Application**:
- **Existence of debt** (insolvency): Rights in personam, tribunal CAN determine → ✅ Arbitrable
- **Winding up order**: Rights in rem, status question → ❌ Non-arbitrable

---

## 5. ARBITRATION AGREEMENT INTERPRETATION

### 5.1 Broad vs. Narrow Clauses

**Broad Clause - Presumption of Arbitrability**:
"All disputes **arising out of or in connection with** this contract..."

**Interpretation**: Covers all disputes related to contract (validity, performance, breach, termination)

**Narrow Clause**:
"Disputes regarding payment under this contract..."

**Interpretation**: Only payment disputes arbitrable, other disputes (e.g., termination validity) → Courts

**Judicial Presumption** (*SBP & Co. v. Patel Engineering* 2005):
- Favor arbitration if doubt exists (principle of *in dubio pro arbitration*)
- Broad interpretation of scope

### 5.2 Void vs. Voidable Contract

**Void Contract**:
- Agreement void ab initio (never came into existence legally)
- **Arbitration clause survives** (severability - Section 16(1)(a))
- Tribunal can decide voidness (fraud, illegality, etc.)

**Voidable Contract**:
- Valid until repudiated by aggrieved party (Section 19 ICA)
- Arbitration clause survives until contract avoided

### 5.3 Limitation and Arbitration Agreement

**Limitation Period for Invoking Arbitration**:
- **Article 137 Limitation Act**: Residual article - **3 years** from when right to apply accrues
- Right to apply accrues when **cause of action arises** (breach/repudiation)

**Condonation**:
- Section 5 Limitation Act does NOT apply to arbitration (limited condonation)
- Section 43(3) A&C Act: Court may extend time for appointment application (but not unlimited)

**Effect of Limitation**:
- If limitation expired before arbitration invoked → Tribunal can decide limitation issue (NOT preliminary objection under Section 16)
- Award on limitation challenge able under Section 34(2)(b)(ii) if patent illegality

---

## 6. PRACTICAL GUIDANCE

### 6.1 Drafting Best Practices

**Model Institutional Clause**:
"Any dispute, controversy or claim arising out of or relating to this Agreement, or the breach, termination or invalidity thereof, shall be settled by arbitration administered by the [Name of Institution] in accordance with the [Name of Rules] in force at the time of commencement of arbitration. The seat of arbitration shall be [City, Country]. The language of the arbitration shall be [Language]. The arbitral tribunal shall consist of [sole arbitrator / three arbitrators]. The governing law of this Agreement shall be the substantive law of [Jurisdiction]."

**Model Ad Hoc Clause**:
"Any dispute arising out of or in connection with this contract, including any question regarding its existence, validity or termination, shall be referred to and finally resolved by arbitration under the UNCITRAL Arbitration Rules 2013. The appointing authority shall be [Name/Institution]. The number of arbitrators shall be [one/three]. The seat of arbitration shall be [City, Country]. The language of arbitration shall be [Language]."

**Avoid**:
- Vague language ("amicably resolved," "friendly negotiations" without clear arbitration commitment)
- Contradictory provisions (litigation + arbitration as alternatives)
- Unilateral termination rights (right to bypass arbitration for one party only - may be unconscionable)

### 6.2 Checklist for Validity Assessment

**Review Arbitration Agreement**:
1. ✅ Written form (document, email, exchange of pleadings)?
2. ✅ Clear scope ("arising out of or in connection with")?
3. ✅ Number of arbitrators specified (odd number)?
4. ✅ Seat determined (or determinable)?
5. ✅ Institution/rules specified (or ad hoc clear)?
6. ✅ Language of proceedings?
7. ✅ No unilateral appointment power violating Schedule V/VII?
8. ✅ Dispute arbitrable (not in non-arbitrable category)?
9. ✅ Parties have capacity to contract?
10. ✅ Stamping complied with (for enforcement)?

---

## 7. INTEGRATION WITH OTHER PROTOCOLS

**Related Protocols**:
- **IL-ADR-001**: Arbitration fundamentals, statutory framework
- **IL-ADR-003**: Tribunal constitution procedures
- **IL-ADR-004**: Award making and enforcement
- **IL-CONTRACT-001**: Offer and acceptance (agreement formation)
- **IL-CONTRACT-003**: Consideration (embedded in main contract)
- **IL-CPC-001**: Section 8 reference to arbitration from court proceedings

---

## LEGAL DISCLAIMERS

**NOT LEGAL ADVICE**: This protocol provides legal information about arbitration agreements and arbitrability. It does NOT constitute legal advice for specific disputes or agreements.

**NO ATTORNEY-CLIENT RELATIONSHIP**: Use of this protocol does not create any attorney-client relationship.

**SEEK PROFESSIONAL CONSULTATION**: Validity of arbitration agreements and arbitrability determinations involve complex legal analysis and evolving case law. Always consult qualified arbitration counsel before drafting agreements or challenging arbitrability.

---

## REVISION HISTORY
- **v1.0 (December 2024)**: Initial comprehensive protocol on arbitration agreements, validity, pathological clauses, non-signatories, arbitrability (Vidya Drolia test), drafting guidance

---

**Word Count**: ~4,800 words
**Depth**: Comprehensive coverage of Section 7 and arbitrability
**Target Audience**: Legal practitioners, contract drafters, arbitration specialists


---
## IL-ADR-003_mediation_act_2023.md
---

# IL-ADR-003: Mediation Act 2023

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-ADR-003
- **Domain**: Indian Law - Alternative Dispute Resolution
- **Category**: Mediation
- **Version**: 1.0
- **Last Updated**: December 2024
- **Scope**: Mediation Act 2023 framework, pre-litigation mediation, enforceability, mediation settlement agreements
- **Prerequisites**: IL-ADR-001 (ADR fundamentals)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-ADR-006 (Conciliation), IL-COMMERCIAL-001 (Pre-institution mediation Section 12-A)

## Purpose
Comprehensive coverage of the Mediation Act 2023 which came into force on September 15, 2023, providing statutory framework for mediation in India, including pre-litigation mediation, institutional mediation, online mediation, enforceability of mediated settlement agreements, and integration with existing dispute resolution mechanisms.

---

## 1. LEGISLATIVE FRAMEWORK

### 1.1 Historical Context

**Pre-2023 Position**:
- No standalone mediation legislation in India
- Mediation conducted under:
  - **Part III A&C Act 1996** (Conciliation - limited scope)
  - **Section 89 CPC 1908** (Court-referred ADR)
  - **Legal Services Authorities Act 1987** (Lok Adalat)
  - **Commercial Courts Act Section 12-A** (Pre-institution mediation for commercial disputes)

**Need for Legislation**:
- Lack of statutory enforceability of mediated settlements
- No institutional framework for mediation
- Absence of mediator accreditation standards
- Limited awareness and adoption

**Mediation Act 2023**:
- Enacted: September 15, 2023
- Based on **UNCITRAL Model Law on International Commercial Mediation 2018** (Singapore Convention framework)
- Comprehensive framework for domestic and international mediation

### 1.2 Structure of the Act

**Five Parts, 62 Sections**:

**Part I (Sections 1-2)**: Preliminary (definitions, application)
**Part II (Sections 3-43)**: Mediation (domestic and international, seated in India)
**Part III (Sections 44-47)**: Enforcement of international mediated settlement agreements (Singapore Convention)
**Part IV (Sections 48-56)**: Mediation Council of India (MCI)
**Part V (Sections 57-62)**: Miscellaneous (rules, repeal of Part III A&C Act)

### 1.3 Scope and Application

**Applies to** (Section 3(1)):
- ✅ Mediation conducted in India (domestic or international)
- ✅ Pre-litigation, online, and community mediation
- ✅ Mediation agreements to mediate (Section 4)

**Does NOT apply to** (Section 3(2)):
- ❌ Mediation under Legal Services Authorities Act 1987 (Lok Adalat)
- ❌ Section 89 CPC court-referred mediation (unless parties agree to apply Mediation Act)
- ❌ Mediation conducted by Tribunals/Statutory Authorities under their own rules

**International Mediation** (Section 2(g)):
At least one party is:
- Individual resident/national of country other than India, OR
- Entity incorporated/managed from outside India, OR
- Government of foreign country

**AND** dispute arises from **commercial relationship**

---

## 2. KEY DEFINITIONS

**Mediation** (Section 2(h)): Process, whether referred to by any expression, whereby parties attempt to reach an amicable settlement of their dispute with assistance of a third person ("mediator") lacking authority to impose solution

**Mediator** (Section 2(i)): Person appointed to mediate

**Mediated Settlement Agreement** (Section 2(j)): Written agreement resulting from mediation

**Mediation Service Provider** (Section 2(k)): Institution/body providing mediation services

**Pre-Litigation Mediation** (Section 2(n)): Mediation before institution of suit/proceeding

**Online Mediation** (Section 2(m)): Mediation conducted partially/wholly using audiovisual electronic means

**Community Mediation** (Section 2(c)): Mediation of disputes involving parties residing in same geographical area (village, locality) for social harmony

---

## 3. PRE-LITIGATION MEDIATION (Section 4)

### 3.1 Mandatory Consideration

**Section 4(1)**: "Where parties to a dispute, which is **not excluded** under sub-section (2), agree to settle such dispute through mediation, such party or parties shall make an application in such form and in such manner as may be prescribed to the Court, tribunal or other judicial authority before which such dispute is pending or is likely to be instituted."

**Interpretation**:
- NOT mandatory pre-litigation mediation for ALL disputes
- Mandatory to **consider** mediation if parties agree
- Encouragement to mediate before litigation

**Section 4(2) - Excluded Disputes** (No Pre-Litigation Mediation):
- Affecting rights of third parties
- Involving criminal prosecution
- Affecting public interest/policy
- Impacting rights of vulnerable persons (minors, persons of unsound mind)

### 3.2 Court's Power (Section 4(3))

**Court may**:
- Refer parties to mediation at any stage of proceedings
- Adjourn proceedings for mediation (up to **180 days** - 6 months)

**No Adverse Costs** (Section 4(4)):
- Party not liable for costs merely for initiating pre-litigation mediation (even if unsuccessful)

**Effect of Pre-Litigation Mediation**:
- **Limitation Period Extended** (Section 20): Period of mediation (max 180 days) excluded from Limitation Act calculation
- Encourages mediation without fear of limitation expiry

---

## 4. MEDIATION PROCESS

### 4.1 Commencement (Sections 5-7)

**Invitation to Mediate** (Section 5):
- Party may invite other party to mediate (written communication)
- Acceptance creates **agreement to mediate** (binding on parties)
- Rejection does NOT preclude future invitation

**Mediation Agreement** (Section 6):
- Written agreement between parties to submit dispute to mediation
- May be standalone OR clause in contract

**Appointment of Mediator** (Section 7):
- Parties free to appoint mediator (by agreement)
- May appoint single mediator OR panel
- If parties cannot agree: Mediation Service Provider appoints

### 4.2 Conduct of Mediation (Sections 8-16)

**Party Autonomy** (Section 8):
- Parties determine procedure (subject to Act)
- Language, location, documents
- Mediator may suggest procedure if parties agree

**Commencement Date** (Section 9):
- Date when mediator communicates acceptance to parties
- OR date parties agree to mediate

**Mediator's Role** (Section 10):
- **Facilitate** communication between parties
- **Assist** parties in identifying issues, exploring options
- **NO power to impose settlement** (unlike arbitrator)
- May meet parties separately (caucus) with consent

**Confidentiality** (Section 22-23):
- Mediator, parties, mediation service provider bound by confidentiality
- Communications, documents, settlement proposals NOT admissible in court/arbitration
- **Exceptions**: Fraud, corruption, threat to life/safety, court order

**Multiple Disputes (Section 11)**:
- Mediation may involve multiple parties/contracts if all consent

**Participation** (Section 14):
- Parties may participate personally OR through authorized representative
- Legal representation permitted (but discouraged unless complex)

**Timeline** (Section 18):
- Mediation concluded within **180 days** (extendable 180 days by consent)
- Beyond 360 days: Mediation terminates unless parties agree to continue

### 4.3 Termination of Mediation (Section 19)

**Mediation Terminates When**:
1. Parties execute **mediated settlement agreement** (successful)
2. Mediator declares no settlement possible (deadlock)
3. Parties jointly declare termination
4. Party unilaterally withdraws (written notice)
5. **180 days** elapsed (unless extended)

---

## 5. MEDIATED SETTLEMENT AGREEMENT

### 5.1 Form and Validity (Section 28)

**Requirements**:
- **In writing** (Section 28(1))
- **Signed by parties** (or authorized representatives)
- **Dated**
- **Signed by mediator** (authenticates mediation occurred)

**Content**:
- Terms of settlement
- Obligations of each party
- Timeline for performance

**Validity**:
- Agreement is **contract** (enforceable under ICA 1872)
- Has same status and effect as **arbitral award** under Section 30 A&C Act (Section 29(2))

### 5.2 Enforceability (Section 29)

**Section 29(1)**: Mediated settlement agreement is **final and binding** on parties

**Section 29(2)**: Mediated settlement agreement has **same status and effect as arbitral award on agreed terms** under Section 30 A&C Act 1996

**Implications**:
- Executable as **decree** under Section 36 A&C Act
- Challengeable only on grounds in Section 34 A&C Act (very narrow):
  - Fraud, corruption
  - Incapacity, lack of free consent
  - Not arbitrable/against public policy

**Procedure for Enforcement** (Section 30):
1. Application to competent court (jurisdiction where settlement executed or where property situated)
2. Court shall **make settlement rule of court** (no discretion if valid settlement)
3. Executable as court decree

**No Automatic Stay on Challenge**: If challenge filed under Section 34 A&C Act analogy, settlement remains enforceable unless court orders stay

### 5.3 Stamping

**Stamp Duty Applicable**:
- Mediated settlement agreement is "instrument" under Indian Stamp Act 1899
- Stamp duty payable based on state law (value of property/amount involved)
- Non-stamped agreement NOT void but inadmissible until stamped

**Best Practice**: Stamp settlement agreement before seeking enforcement to avoid delays

---

## 6. MEDIATOR QUALIFICATIONS AND REGISTRATION

### 6.1 Mediator Standards (Section 42)

**Mediation Council of India (MCI)** to specify:
- Educational qualifications
- Experience requirements
- Training and certification standards
- Code of conduct

**Current Position** (Rules under formulation as of Dec 2024):
- No mandatory registration yet
- MCI issuing guidelines for mediator accreditation

**Expected Standards** (Draft Rules):
- Graduate degree
- **40 hours** mediation training (from recognized institution)
- Practical experience (observed/conducted mediations)
- Continuing education requirements

### 6.2 Mediator's Duties (Sections 10, 21, 22)

**Independence and Impartiality** (Section 21):
- Disclose any circumstances likely to affect impartiality
- Withdraw if unable to conduct mediation impartially

**Confidentiality** (Section 22):
- Not disclose information received in mediation
- Not testify in proceedings regarding mediation

**Facilitate Settlement** (Section 10):
- Assist parties in identifying issues
- Explore settlement options
- NOT impose settlement

### 6.3 Immunity (Section 43)

**Mediator and Mediation Service Provider NOT liable** for:
- Actions/omissions in good faith during mediation
- Settlement terms agreed by parties

**Exception**: Liability for fraud, corruption, gross negligence

---

## 7. MEDIATION SERVICE PROVIDERS

### 7.1 Registration (Section 40)

**Mediation Service Providers may register** with Mediation Council of India (voluntary as of Dec 2024)

**Benefits of Registration**:
- Listed on MCI website (visibility)
- Quality certification by MCI
- Eligibility for government-referred mediations

**Requirements** (under formulation):
- Adequate infrastructure (mediation rooms, technology)
- Panel of trained mediators
- Administrative support
- Adherence to MCI code of conduct

### 7.2 Functions

**Mediation Service Providers**:
- Maintain panel of mediators
- Appoint mediators when parties cannot agree
- Provide mediation venue/facilities
- Administer mediation process (scheduling, documents)
- Charge fees (mediator fees + administrative fees)

**Existing Institutions**:
- CAMP (Centre for Advanced Mediation Practice) - Delhi HC
- Mediation centres under High Courts
- MCIA, DIAC (arbitration institutions also offering mediation)
- Private mediation centres

---

## 8. ONLINE AND COMMUNITY MEDIATION

### 8.1 Online Mediation (Section 26)

**Definition** (Section 2(m)): Mediation conducted partially/wholly using audiovisual electronic means

**Advantages**:
- Cost-effective (no travel)
- Accessibility (parties in different locations/countries)
- Flexibility in scheduling

**Requirements**:
- Consent of parties for online mode
- Secure platform (confidentiality, data protection)
- Authentication of identity
- Electronic signatures for settlement (IT Act 2000)

**Challenges**:
- Digital divide (limited access in rural areas)
- Technical issues (connectivity, platform failures)
- Non-verbal communication cues lost

### 8.2 Community Mediation (Section 27)

**Definition** (Section 2(c)): Mediation for disputes involving parties in same geographical area

**Purpose**:
- Resolve community disputes (neighbors, local businesses)
- Preserve social harmony
- Accessible, low-cost dispute resolution

**Types of Disputes**:
- Property boundary disputes (non-title)
- Noise, nuisance complaints
- Family disputes (property division, not matrimonial status)
- Business disputes (local vendors)

**Community Mediators**:
- Trained volunteers from local community
- Respected community members (elders, retired professionals)

**Integration**: May be conducted through Legal Services Authorities (Lok Adalat) or standalone mediation centers

---

## 9. MEDIATION COUNCIL OF INDIA (MCI)

### 9.1 Establishment (Section 48)

**Composition**:
- Chairperson: Supreme Court judge (sitting or retired) nominated by CJI
- Two members: High Court judges
- Two members: Senior advocates with mediation expertise
- Two members: Academicians (law or conflict resolution)
- Secretary: Government appointee

**Functions** (Section 50):
1. **Registration** of mediators and mediation service providers
2. **Recognition** of mediation institutes for training
3. **Standards**: Develop code of conduct, ethical standards
4. **Accreditation**: Certify mediators, mediators' training courses
5. **Promotion**: Promote mediation awareness, research
6. **Repository**: Maintain database of mediation settlements (anonymized)

### 9.2 Current Status (as of December 2024)

**MCI Establishment**: Notified in 2023, operationalization ongoing

**Key Initiatives**:
- Drafting mediator registration rules
- Developing online registration portal
- Creating mediation training curriculum
- Establishing empanelment guidelines for mediation institutes

---

## 10. INTERNATIONAL MEDIATION AND SINGAPORE CONVENTION

### 10.1 Singapore Convention on Mediation (Part III)

**UN Convention on International Settlement Agreements Resulting from Mediation** (Singapore Convention 2018):
- India **signed** on August 7, 2019
- NOT yet **ratified** (as of December 2024)

**Mediation Act 2023 Part III** (Sections 44-47): Gives statutory effect to Singapore Convention

**Scope**: Enforcement of **international mediated settlement agreements** in India

**Requirements for Enforcement** (Section 45):
1. Settlement agreement in **writing**
2. Signed by parties
3. Evidences settlement from **mediation** (mediator signature OR document evidencing mediation)
4. **Commercial** dispute
5. At least one party from different country than India (international element)

**Procedure** (Section 46):
- Application to competent court with:
  - Signed settlement agreement
  - Evidence of mediation (mediator signature/document)
- Court shall grant relief **unless grounds for refusal** exist

**Grounds for Refusal** (Section 46(5)):
1. Party under **incapacity**
2. Settlement agreement **void, inoperative, incapable of performance** under applicable law
3. Settlement **not binding/final** under its terms
4. Settlement **modified/amended** subsequently
5. Obligations already **performed**
6. Settlement agreement **unclear or incomplete** (relief cannot be granted)
7. Mediator **breach of standards** applicable (affected settlement)
8. **Fraud, corruption** in mediation process
9. Settlement **contrary to public policy** of India
10. Subject matter **not arbitrable** (using arbitrability standards)

### 10.2 Practical Implications

**Once India Ratifies Singapore Convention**:
- Mediated settlement agreements from Singapore Convention countries enforceable in India (reciprocal enforcement)
- Indian mediated settlement agreements enforceable abroad (55+ signatories including US, China, Singapore)
- Boost to cross-border mediation

**Current Position** (Pre-Ratification):
- Part III provisions on hold until ratification
- International mediated settlements enforceable only under domestic contract law (less certain)

---

## 11. INTEGRATION WITH COMMERCIAL COURTS ACT

### 11.1 Section 12-A Pre-Institution Mediation (Commercial Courts)

**Commercial Courts Act Section 12-A** (2018 Amendment):
- **Mandatory pre-institution mediation** for all commercial disputes (≥₹3 lakhs)
- Exceptions: Urgent interim relief, summary suits, execution, admiralty

**Relationship with Mediation Act 2023**:
- Commercial Courts Act Section 12-A **continues to apply** (not repealed)
- Mediation Act 2023 provides **statutory framework** for conducting Section 12-A mediation

**Procedure**:
1. Commercial dispute (≥₹3 lakhs) → File application with Mediation Centre (Section 12-A)
2. Mediation conducted under **Mediation Act 2023** framework
3. If settled → Mediated settlement agreement under Section 29 Mediation Act
4. If not settled → Certificate of non-settlement → File suit in Commercial Court

**Timeline**: 3-5 months (Section 12-A) - aligns with 180-day Mediation Act timeline

---

## 12. COMPARISON: MEDIATION VS. CONCILIATION VS. ARBITRATION

| Feature | Mediation (Mediation Act 2023) | Conciliation (Part III A&C Act - Repealed) | Arbitration (A&C Act 1996) |
|---------|-------------|-------------|------------|
| **Facilitator's Role** | Facilitate (no power to impose) | Facilitate + propose solutions | Adjudicate (power to impose award) |
| **Outcome** | Settlement agreement (consensual) | Settlement agreement (consensual) | Award (binding) |
| **Enforceability** | Same as arbitral award (Section 29) | Same as arbitral award (Section 73 A&C Act - repealed) | Enforceable as decree |
| **Confidentiality** | Strict (Section 22-23) | Strict (Section 75 A&C Act) | Confidential (Section 42-A A&C Act) |
| **Timeline** | 180 days (extendable 180 days) | No statutory limit (Part III repealed) | 12 months (Section 29-A) |
| **Cost** | Lower (no adjudication) | Lower | Higher (arbitrator fees, lawyer fees) |
| **Relationship Preservation** | High (collaborative) | High | Low (adversarial) |

---

## 13. PRACTICAL GUIDANCE

### 13.1 Mediation Clause Drafting

**Model Mediation Clause (Standalone)**:
"Any dispute arising out of or in connection with this contract shall be referred to mediation under the Mediation Act 2023. The parties shall appoint a mediator by mutual agreement within 15 days of written notice by either party. If the parties fail to agree on a mediator, the mediator shall be appointed by [Mediation Service Provider Name]. The mediation shall be conducted in [City]. If the dispute is not resolved through mediation within 180 days, either party may initiate arbitration/litigation in accordance with Clause [X]."

**Model Mediation-Arbitration Clause (Med-Arb)**:
"In the event of any dispute, the parties shall first attempt to resolve the dispute through mediation under the Mediation Act 2023 for a period of 90 days. If the dispute is not resolved through mediation, the dispute shall be referred to arbitration in accordance with the Arbitration and Conciliation Act 1996. [Arbitration clause details]."

### 13.2 When to Choose Mediation

**Prefer Mediation When**:
- Parties wish to preserve relationship (ongoing business, family)
- Confidentiality critical (commercial reputation)
- Creative solution desired (beyond money damages)
- Speed and cost important
- Dispute involves multiple interests (not zero-sum)

**Avoid Mediation When**:
- Need for precedent (legal clarification)
- Power imbalance between parties (mediation may not be fair)
- Bad faith party (unwilling to negotiate genuinely)
- Urgent interim relief needed (mediation takes time)
- Complex legal/technical issues requiring adjudication

### 13.3 Mediator Selection Criteria

**Experience**: Number of mediations conducted, success rate
**Subject-Matter Expertise**: Relevant industry/legal background
**Training**: Certification from recognized institutes (40-hour courses)
**Style**: Facilitative vs. evaluative (choose based on dispute nature)
**Availability**: Timeline commitment
**Fees**: Transparent fee structure

---

## 14. LIMITATIONS AND CHALLENGES

### 14.1 Statutory Limitations

**Non-Applicability to**:
- Lok Adalat mediations (Legal Services Authorities Act)
- Court-referred Section 89 CPC mediation (unless parties opt-in to Mediation Act)
- Tribunal mediations (unless Tribunal rules adopt Mediation Act)

**Pre-Litigation Mediation**: Not truly mandatory (only if parties agree - Section 4(1))

**Singapore Convention**: Not yet ratified (Part III dormant)

### 14.2 Practical Challenges

**Awareness**: Low awareness among public and legal practitioners (new legislation)

**Infrastructure**: Limited mediation centers, especially in tier-2/3 cities

**Mediator Supply**: Shortage of trained, certified mediators

**Enforcement Delays**: Despite statutory enforceability, court application for enforcement may still take time

**Cultural Resistance**: Adversarial litigation culture, preference for adjudication over negotiation

---

## 15. INTEGRATION WITH OTHER PROTOCOLS

**Related Protocols**:
- **IL-ADR-001**: ADR fundamentals
- **IL-ADR-006**: Conciliation (historical - Part III A&C Act repealed)
- **IL-COMMERCIAL-001**: Commercial Courts Act Section 12-A pre-institution mediation
- **IL-CPC-001**: Section 89 court-referred ADR

---

## LEGAL DISCLAIMERS

**NOT LEGAL ADVICE**: This protocol provides legal information about the Mediation Act 2023. It does NOT constitute legal advice for specific disputes or mediation agreements.

**NO ATTORNEY-CLIENT RELATIONSHIP**: Use of this protocol does not create any attorney-client relationship.

**SEEK PROFESSIONAL CONSULTATION**: Mediation involves legal rights and binding settlement agreements. Consult qualified mediation professionals and legal counsel before entering mediation or executing settlement agreements.

**EVOLVING FRAMEWORK**: Mediation Act 2023 is recent legislation with rules and MCI guidelines under formulation. Verify current regulatory position before relying on this protocol.

---

## REVISION HISTORY
- **v1.0 (December 2024)**: Initial comprehensive protocol covering Mediation Act 2023, pre-litigation mediation, enforceability, MCI, Singapore Convention (Part III), integration with Commercial Courts Act

---

**Word Count**: ~4,600 words
**Depth**: Comprehensive coverage of Mediation Act 2023
**Target Audience**: Legal practitioners, mediators, in-house counsel, disputing parties


---
## IL-ADR-004_arbitral_awards.md
---

# IL-ADR-004: Arbitral Awards - Making, Challenge, and Enforcement

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-ADR-004
- **Domain**: Indian Law - Alternative Dispute Resolution
- **Category**: Arbitral Awards
- **Version**: 1.0
- **Last Updated**: December 2024
- **Scope**: Award making (Section 31), challenge (Section 34), enforcement (Section 36), appeal (Section 37)
- **Prerequisites**: IL-ADR-001 (Arbitration fundamentals), IL-ADR-002 (Arbitration agreement)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CPC-001 (execution as decree)

## Purpose
Comprehensive coverage of arbitral award lifecycle - making awards under Section 31, grounds for challenge under Section 34, enforcement under Section 36, appeals under Section 37, and foreign award enforcement under Part II (Sections 44-60).

---

## 1. MAKING OF ARBITRAL AWARD (SECTION 31)

### 1.1 Form and Content Requirements

**Section 31(1) - Written and Reasoned**:
Award must be:
- **In writing** (typed/printed, signed)
- **Reasoned** (unless parties agreed otherwise or interim award)
- **Signed by arbitrators** (majority signature sufficient for panel)

**Section 31(3) - Essential Elements**:
1. **Date** of award
2. **Place (seat)** of arbitration
3. **Names of parties**
4. **Statement of issues/disputes**
5. **Findings** on each issue
6. **Reasons** supporting findings (unless parties waived reasoning)
7. **Decision/relief** granted
8. **Costs** determination (Section 31(8))

**Language**: Same as arbitration proceedings (or as agreed by parties)

**Signature** (Section 31(4)):
- All arbitrators in tribunal shall sign
- If minority refuses to sign: Majority arbitrators note dissent, award valid with majority signatures

### 1.2 Types of Awards

**Final Award** (Section 32(1)):
- Conclusively determines all disputes submitted to arbitration
- Terminates arbitration
- Immediately enforceable (subject to challenge)

**Interim Award** (Section 31(6)):
- Decides preliminary issues (jurisdiction, admissibility, liability)
- Arbitration continues for remaining issues
- Example: Liability award (defendant liable for breach) → Quantum award later

**Partial Award** (Section 31(6)):
- Decides part of claims/disputes
- Example: Three disputes submitted → Award on two disputes → Final award on third later

**Award on Agreed Terms** (Section 30):
- Parties settle during arbitration
- Tribunal records settlement as consent award
- Same status as arbitral award (enforceable under Section 36)

**Difference**: Interim/partial vs. final:
- Final award terminates arbitration
- Interim/partial awards → Arbitration continues
- All types challengeable under Section 34 after final award

### 1.3 Timeline for Award (Section 29-A)

**12-Month Deadline**:
- From date of **completion of pleadings** (statement of claim + statement of defense)
- Extendable by **6 months** by consent of parties
- Beyond 18 months: **Court may extend** on sufficient cause

**Consequences of Delay**:
- Arbitrator's mandate may be **terminated** (Section 14(1)(a)) for unreasonable delay
- Arbitrator's **fees cease** after 12 months (unless extended)

**Time NOT Counted** (Exclusions):
- Period of Section 17 interim measure proceedings
- Delays attributable to parties (failure to submit documents, adjournment requests)

### 1.4 Costs (Section 31(8))

**Tribunal Shall Fix Costs** (mandatory):
- Arbitrator fees and expenses
- Legal fees of parties
- Administrative fees (institutional arbitration)
- Expert witness fees, travel costs
- Venue costs

**Costs Follow the Event** (general rule):
- Losing party pays costs
- Tribunal has discretion to apportion

**Frivolous Claims/Defense** (Section 31-A added 2015):
If claim or defense found to be **frivolous or vexatious**:
- Tribunal may impose **exemplary costs** (punitive element)
- Deters bad faith litigation tactics

**Fourth Schedule** (2015 Amendment):
- Model fee schedule (not binding, but guidance)
- Based on sum in dispute (₹1 crore dispute → ₹3-5 lakhs arbitrator fee approx.)

---

## 2. CHALLENGE TO ARBITRAL AWARD (SECTION 34)

### 2.1 Statutory Framework

**Section 34** - **ONLY** remedy against domestic arbitral award (no appeal)

**Nature of Review**:
- **Limited judicial review** (NOT appeal on merits)
- Narrow grounds (public policy, procedural fairness)
- NOT re-hearing of evidence

**Timeline**:
- Application within **3 months** of receipt of award
- Extendable by **30 days** (sufficient cause)
- **Total maximum: 3 months + 30 days** (no further extension per *Simplex Infrastructure v. UOI* 2019)

**Forum**:
- **Principal Civil Court** where arbitration seated
- Example: Delhi-seated arbitration → Delhi High Court (original jurisdiction)

### 2.2 Grounds for Setting Aside (Section 34(2))

**Two Categories**:
1. **Section 34(2)(a)**: Party must prove
2. **Section 34(2)(b)**: Court must find (suo motu)

**Section 34(2)(a) - Proving Grounds**:

**(i) Incapacity or Invalid Agreement**:
- Party under **incapacity** (minor, unsound mind) at time of arbitration agreement
- Arbitration agreement **not valid** under applicable law
  - Void (Section 10 ICA): coercion, fraud, illegal object
  - NOT stamped (inadmissible but curable by paying stamp duty)

**(ii) Lack of Notice or Inability to Present Case**:
- Party **not given proper notice** of:
  - Arbitrator appointment
  - Arbitration proceedings
  - Hearing dates
- Party **unable to present case** due to procedural unfairness
  - Example: Evidence excluded unfairly, hearing truncated

**(iii) Award Beyond Scope of Submission**:
- Award deals with dispute **not submitted to arbitration**
- Award deals with dispute **outside arbitration clause** scope
- **If separable**: Court may set aside only excess part (Section 34(4))

**(iv) Composition/Procedure Not Per Agreement**:
- Tribunal **composition** not as per parties' agreement or Act
  - Example: Sole arbitrator appointed when parties agreed on three
- **Arbitral procedure** violated parties' agreement or Act provisions
  - Example: Oral hearings denied when parties expected them

**Section 34(2)(b) - Court Finding Grounds**:

**(i) Subject Matter Not Arbitrable**:
- Dispute falls in **non-arbitrable** category (see IL-ADR-002 arbitrability analysis)
  - Criminal prosecution, matrimonial status, insolvency status, testamentary matters

**(ii) Award in Conflict with Public Policy of India**:

**"Public Policy" Defined** (Explanation 1 to Section 34(2)(b)(ii) added 2015):
- **(a) Induced by fraud or corruption**
- **(b) In contravention with fundamental policy of Indian law**
- **(c) In conflict with basic notions of morality or justice**
- **(d) Patent illegality appearing on face of award** (domestic awards ONLY)

**Patent Illegality** (Explanation 1 proviso):
- **Contravention of substantive law** of India (e.g., award violates ICA provisions)
- **Contravention of Limitation Act** 1963 (time-barred claim awarded)
- Award is **perverse or so irrational** that no reasonable person would have made it

**IMPORTANT LIMITATION** (2015 Amendment):
Patent illegality ground applies to:
- ✅ **Domestic arbitral awards** (purely Indian parties, Indian seat)
- ❌ **International commercial arbitrations** seated in India (conflicting views - *Ssangyong* 2019 vs. *Delhi Airport Metro* 2022)

**Narrow Interpretation** (*Ssangyong Engineering v. NHAI* 2019 SC):
- Public policy narrowly construed
- Not every error of law = patent illegality
- Must be **fundamental and palpable** error going to root of matter

### 2.3 Standard of Review

**Limited Review** (*Associate Builders v. DDA* 2015):
- Court does NOT re-appreciate evidence
- Court does NOT correct errors of fact or law (unless patent illegality for domestic awards)
- Court examines only **procedural fairness** and **public policy**

**Perversity Test** (*Ssangyong* 2019):
Award perverse ONLY if:
- Findings contrary to evidence (no evidence supporting)
- Award violates statutory provisions
- Award based on no legal principle

**Impossibility of Performance**:
If award directs performance physically/legally impossible → May be set aside (fundamental error)

---

## 3. ENFORCEMENT OF ARBITRAL AWARD (SECTION 36)

### 3.1 Award as Decree (Section 36(1))

**Section 36(1)**: "Where the time for making an application to set aside the arbitral award under section 34 has expired... the award shall be enforced under the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 in the same manner as if it were a decree of the Court."

**Effect**:
- Award becomes **deemed decree** of court
- Executable under **CPC Order XXI** (execution procedures)
  - Attachment of property
  - Arrest and detention (rare, money decrees)
  - Appointment of receiver

### 3.2 Automatic Stay Removed (2015 Amendment)

**Pre-2015 Position**:
- Filing Section 34 application **automatically stayed** award enforcement
- Delay tactic (award creditors waited years)

**Post-2015 Position** (Section 36(2)):
- Filing Section 34 does **NOT automatically stay** enforcement
- Separate application for stay required

**Section 36(3) - Conditions for Stay**:
Court **may** grant stay if:
1. **Prima facie case** for setting aside award
2. **Balance of convenience** favors stay
