3. **Irreparable injury** to applicant if stay not granted

**Court may impose conditions**:
- Deposit of **part/whole** award amount
- Furnish security/bank guarantee

**2021 Amendment - Exception** (Section 36(3) proviso):
If court is satisfied that deposit would cause **undue hardship**, may grant unconditional stay for:
- Micro/small enterprises (MSMEs)
- Startups
- "Reasons to be recorded"

**Practical Effect**:
- Award creditors can execute immediately after 3-month Section 34 period (unless stay obtained)
- Award debtors must show strong prima facie case and provide security to get stay

---

## 4. APPEAL (SECTION 37)

### 4.1 Appealable Orders

**Section 37(1)** - Appeal lies from:

**(a) Section 11(6) order** (refusing to appoint arbitrator)

**(b) Section 11(7) order** (deciding on arbitrator's jurisdiction under Section 16 at appointment stage)

**(c) Section 34 order**:
- Granting/refusing to set aside arbitral award
- **Most common appeal**

**(d) Section 36 order** (refusing to enforce award or granting/refusing stay)

**No Other Appeal**:
- No appeal from interim orders of tribunal (Section 17)
- No appeal from tribunal's preliminary jurisdiction ruling (Section 16) - wait until final award

### 4.2 Appellate Forum

**Domestic Arbitration**:
- Section 34 order by District Court → Appeal to **High Court**
- Section 34 order by High Court (if HC has original jurisdiction) → Appeal to **Division Bench of same HC**

**International Commercial Arbitration**:
- Section 34 order → Appeal to **High Court**

**Timeline**:
- Appeal within **90 days** (some HCs have 60 days under local rules)
- Condonation possible under Limitation Act Section 5

### 4.3 No Second Appeal (Section 50)

**Section 50**: "Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, no second appeal shall lie from an order passed in appeal under this section, but nothing in this section shall affect or take away any right to appeal to the Supreme Court."

**Effect**:
- Appeal from District Court → High Court → **NO second appeal** to Supreme Court under CPC Section 100
- **Only SLP** (Special Leave Petition) under Article 136 Constitution to Supreme Court (discretionary)

**Finality**: Arbitral awards have greater finality than civil court decrees (which allow second appeals on substantial questions of law)

---

## 5. FOREIGN AWARDS ENFORCEMENT (PART II)

### 5.1 New York Convention Awards (Sections 44-52)

**Convention on Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral Awards 1958** (New York Convention):
- India party since 1960
- 172 contracting states (as of 2024)

**"Foreign Award"** (Section 44):
Award made in territory of:
- New York Convention country, OR
- Country notified by India as reciprocating territory

**Enforcement Procedure** (Section 47):
- Application to competent court (where award debtor has assets)
- Produce:
  - **Original award** or certified copy
  - **Original arbitration agreement** or certified copy
  - Certified translation (if not in English)

**Conditions for Enforcement** (Section 48):
Award enforced **UNLESS** award debtor proves:

**(a) Party-Proving Grounds**:
1. **Incapacity** or **arbitration agreement invalid** under applicable law
2. **Lack of notice** or unable to present case
3. **Award beyond scope** of submission
4. **Composition/procedure not per agreement** or law of arbitral country
5. **Award not binding** or set aside/suspended by competent authority of award country

**(b) Court-Finding Grounds**:
1. Subject matter **not arbitrable** under Indian law
2. Enforcement **contrary to public policy** of India

**NO Patent Illegality** for Foreign Awards:
- Public policy limited to:
  - Fundamental policy of Indian law
  - Interests of India
  - Justice/morality
- **Renusagar Power* (1994) and *Shri Lal Mahal* (2014)

**Procedure**: Foreign award treated as decree of Indian court (enforceable under CPC)

### 5.2 Geneva Convention Awards (Sections 53-60)

**Geneva Convention 1927** (Protocol and Convention on Arbitration Clauses):
- Pre-dates New York Convention
- Limited applicability now (most countries adopted NYC)

**Fewer Countries**: ~40 countries (many also NYC parties, NYC prevails)

**Enforcement**: Similar procedure, slightly different grounds

**Practical Note**: New York Convention awards overwhelmingly more common in practice

---

## 6. SPECIFIC SCENARIOS

### 6.1 Consent Awards (Section 30)

**Parties Settle During Arbitration**:
- Request tribunal to record settlement as consent award
- Tribunal shall terminate proceedings and record settlement as award

**Effect**:
- Same status as regular arbitral award
- Enforceable under Section 36
- Challengeable under Section 34 (limited grounds - fraud, incapacity, non-arbitrability)

**Advantage Over Settlement Agreement**:
- Award immediately executable as decree
- Settlement agreement requires separate suit for enforcement (unless filed under Mediation Act 2023 Section 29)

### 6.2 Rectification/Interpretation/Additional Award (Section 33)

**Section 33(1) - Correction/Interpretation**:
Within **30 days** of award receipt, party may request tribunal to:
- **Correct** computation errors, clerical/typographical errors
- **Interpret** specific point/part of award (clarification)

**Section 33(3) - Additional Award**:
- If claims presented but **not decided** in award → Party may request additional award
- Within **60 days** of award receipt

**Tribunal Action**:
- Decide within **30 days** (extendable 30 days)
- Correction/interpretation/additional award has same status as original award

**Example**:
- Award grants ₹50 lakhs but computation error (should be ₹55 lakhs based on findings) → Correction
- Award silent on interest from award date → Request additional award on interest

### 6.3 Termination Without Award (Section 32(2))

**Arbitration Terminates Without Award**:
1. Parties agree to terminate
2. Claimant withdraws claim (unless respondent has legitimate interest in final settlement)
3. Tribunal finds continuation unnecessary/impossible

**Effect**: Arbitration ends, no award issued

**Costs**: Tribunal may still determine costs (Section 32(3))

---

## 7. CRITICAL CASE LAW

**Associate Builders v. DDA (2015) 3 SCC 49**:
- Limited scope of Section 34 review
- Not re-appreciation of evidence
- Public policy narrowly construed

**Ssangyong Engineering v. NHAI (2019) 15 SCC 131**:
- Patent illegality NOT ground for international commercial arbitrations seated in India
- Public policy: fundamental policy, justice, morality only
- Perversity test

**Project Director, NHAI v. M. Hakeem (2021) 9 SCC 1**:
- Section 11 orders (appointment) NOT appealable to Supreme Court (no substantial question of law)
- Reduces delays in arbitrator appointments

**N.N. Global Mercantile v. Indo Unique Flame (2021) 4 SCC 379**:
- Unstamped arbitration agreement valid but inadmissible
- Can be stamped later for enforcement

**Simplex Infrastructure v. Union of India (2019) 2 SCC 455**:
- 3 months + 30 days maximum for Section 34 application
- No further extension

**Vijay Karia v. Prysmian Cavi (2020) 11 SCC 1**:
- Emergency arbitrator orders enforceable under Section 17(2)

---

## 8. PRACTICAL GUIDANCE

### 8.1 Award Debtor Strategy (Resisting Enforcement)

**Immediate Actions After Award Receipt**:
1. **Analyze award** for Section 34 grounds (consult arbitration counsel)
2. **File Section 34** within 3 months (or 3 months + 30 days with extension application)
3. **Simultaneously apply for stay** (Section 36(3)) with:
   - Prima facie case demonstration
   - Offer security/deposit (if financially viable)

**Strategic Considerations**:
- **Frivolous challenges deter**: If award on strong legal/factual basis, challenge unlikely to succeed and incurs costs
- **Deposit requirement**: Be prepared to deposit substantial portion (courts often require 50-75% deposit for stay)
- **Time cost**: Section 34 + appeal takes 2-5 years; during this time, interest accrues on award amount

### 8.2 Award Creditor Strategy (Enforcing Award)

**Post-Award Actions**:
1. Wait for **3-month Section 34 period** to expire (if no challenge filed)
2. If Section 34 filed:
   - **Oppose stay application** vigorously (demonstrate no prima facie case, no irreparable injury)
   - Seek **security/deposit order** (if stay granted)
   - **Execute award** if stay not granted (initiate execution under CPC Order XXI)

**Execution Steps**:
1. File execution petition in court where award seated
2. Attach debtor's assets (bank accounts, immovable property)
3. If debtor absconding/diverting assets → Seek orders under Section 9 (preservation measures)

### 8.3 Drafting Award - Arbitrator's Perspective

**Best Practices for Arbitrators**:
1. **Detailed Reasoning**: Reduces Section 34 challenges (no "non-reasoned award" ground)
2. **Address All Issues**: Ensure no claim left undecided (avoid Section 33 additional award applications)
3. **Jurisdiction Findings**: Explicitly state tribunal's jurisdiction determination (Section 16 compliance)
4. **Costs Breakdown**: Itemize costs (arbitrator fees, party costs) clearly
5. **Compliance with Section 31**: Date, place, signatures, language
6. **Timely Award**: Meet 12-month deadline (avoid mandate termination risk)

---

## 9. INTEGRATION WITH OTHER PROTOCOLS

**Related Protocols**:
- **IL-ADR-001**: Arbitration fundamentals, statutory framework
- **IL-ADR-002**: Arbitration agreement and arbitrability
- **IL-CPC-001**: Execution of award as decree (CPC Order XXI procedures)
- **IL-LIMITATION-004**: Limitation periods for Section 34, Section 37 appeals

---

## LEGAL DISCLAIMERS

**NOT LEGAL ADVICE**: This protocol provides legal information about arbitral awards, challenges, and enforcement. It does NOT constitute legal advice for specific awards or enforcement proceedings.

**NO ATTORNEY-CLIENT RELATIONSHIP**: Use of this protocol does not create any attorney-client relationship.

**SEEK PROFESSIONAL CONSULTATION**: Challenging or enforcing arbitral awards involves significant legal rights and complex procedural requirements. Always consult qualified arbitration counsel for award review, Section 34 applications, and enforcement strategy.

---

## REVISION HISTORY
- **v1.0 (December 2024)**: Initial comprehensive protocol on arbitral award making (Section 31), challenge (Section 34), enforcement (Section 36), appeals (Section 37), foreign awards (Part II)

---

**Word Count**: ~4,400 words
**Depth**: Comprehensive coverage of award lifecycle
**Target Audience**: Arbitrators, legal practitioners, in-house counsel, award creditors/debtors


---
## IL-CONSUMER-001_consumer_rights_2019.md
---

# IL-CONSUMER-001: Consumer Protection Act 2019 - Rights and Framework

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-CONSUMER-001
- **Domain**: Indian Law - Consumer Protection
- **Category**: Consumer Rights
- **Version**: 1.0
- **Last Updated**: December 2024
- **Scope**: Consumer Protection Act 2019, consumer rights, definitions, CCPA, e-commerce protections
- **Prerequisites**: None (foundational)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CONSUMER-002 (Redressal Mechanism), IL-CONTRACT-001 (Consumer contracts)

## Purpose
Comprehensive coverage of Consumer Protection Act 2019 which replaced 1986 Act, including consumer rights, definitions, Central Consumer Protection Authority (CCPA), product liability, e-commerce regulations, unfair trade practices, and regulatory framework.

---

## 1. LEGISLATIVE FRAMEWORK

### 1.1 Evolution

**Consumer Protection Act 1986**: Pioneering consumer rights legislation (repealed)

**Consumer Protection Act 2019** (effective July 20, 2020):
- Modernized framework for digital economy
- Established Central Consumer Protection Authority (CCPA)
- Introduced product liability provisions
- E-commerce protections
- Enhanced penalties
- Streamlined dispute redressal

**Constitutional Basis**: Entry 7 List III (Concurrent List) - Contracts including sale of goods, partnerships

### 1.2 Key Definitions (Section 2)

**"Consumer"** (Section 2(7)):
Person who:
1. **Buys goods** for consideration (paid or deferred/installments), OR
2. **Hires/avails services** for consideration

**AND** uses goods/services for **self-consumption** (NOT for:
- ❌ Commercial resale
- ❌ Commercial purpose/earning livelihood)

**Includes**:
- Actual buyer/user
- Beneficiary (gift recipient if goods bought for gift)
- Online purchasers

**Excludes**:
- Buyers for resale (traders, retailers)
- Buyers for commercial manufacturing (raw materials)

**"Goods"** (Section 2(19)): Every kind of movable property (exclud ing actionable claims, money, stocks/shares)

**"Service"** (Section 2(42)): Any service made available to potential users (excluding free services, personal services under contract of employment)

**"Defect"** (Section 2(10)): Fault, imperfection, shortcoming in quality, quantity, potency, purity, standard

**"Deficiency"** (Section 2(11)): Inadequacy in quality, nature, manner of service performance

**"Unfair Trade Practice"** (Section 2(47)): Trade practice that adopts unfair method/deceptive practice (false representation, misleading advertisements)

---

## 2. CONSUMER RIGHTS (SECTION 2(9))

### 2.1 Six Fundamental Rights

**1. Right to Safety** (Section 2(9)(a)):
Protection against goods/services hazardous to life/property

**2. Right to Information** (Section 2(9)(b)):
Information about quality, quantity, potency, purity, standard, price (for informed choice)

**3. Right to Choose** (Section 2(9)(c)):
Access to variety of goods/services at competitive prices (no forced purchase)

**4. Right to be Heard** (Section 2(9)(d)):
Representation in consumer forums/bodies

**5. Right to Seek Redressal** (Section 2(9)(e)):
Redress against unfair trade practices/exploitation (through consumer forums)

**6. Right to Consumer Education** (Section 2(9)(f)):
Awareness of rights and remedies

---

## 3. CENTRAL CONSUMER PROTECTION AUTHORITY (CCPA)

### 3.1 Establishment and Powers (Sections 10-32)

**Section 10**: Central Government shall establish CCPA

**Composition**:
- Chief Commissioner (head)
- Additional, Joint, Deputy, Assistant Commissioners

**Functions** (Section 18):
1. **Inquiry** into violations of consumer rights (suo motu or on complaint)
2. **Recall products** (if unsafe)
3. **Refund orders** (if defective goods)
4. **Penalty** (if unfair trade practices, false advertisements)
5. **Product safety standards** enforcement
6. **File complaints** with consumer commissions on behalf of consumers

**Powers**:
- Issue **safety notices** (warn public)
- Order **discontinuance of unfair trade practices**
- Impose **penalties** (up to ₹10 lakhs for false advertisements)
- **Recall/refund/replacement** orders

**False/Misleading Advertisements** (Section 21):
- Manufacturer/endorser penalty: Up to ₹**10 lakhs** (first offense), ₹**50 lakhs** (subsequent)
- **Ban** on endorsing product for up to 1 year (repeat offenses 3 years)

**Endorser Liability**: Celebrity endorsers liable for false/misleading advertisements (unless due diligence proved)

---

## 4. UNFAIR TRADE PRACTICES (SECTION 2(47))

### 4.1 Definition and Types

**Includes**:
1. **False representation** about goods/services (quality, grade, composition, standard)
2. **False representation** about sponsorship, approval, affiliation
3. **False/misleading facts** disparaging competitors' goods/services
4. **Bait advertising** (advertise goods at low price without intent to sell)
5. **Offering gifts/prizes** without intent to provide
6. **Withholding information** about goods/services (quality, fitness)

**Example Scenarios**:
- "Pure honey" containing added sugar ❌
- "Approved by XYZ authority" (false claim) ❌
- "50% off" without actual discount (misleading price) ❌
- Celebrity endorsing product making false health claims ❌

### 4.2 Remedies

**Consumer can**:
1. File complaint with **Consumer Commission** (District/State/National)
2. Complain to **CCPA** (for suo motu action)

**Reliefs**:
- Removal of defect/deficiency
- Replacement of goods
- Refund of price paid
- Compensation for loss/injury
- Discontinuance of unfair practice
- Penalty/punishment

---

## 5. PRODUCT LIABILITY (SECTIONS 82-87)

### 5.1 Strict Liability Framework

**New Provision** (NOT in 1986 Act):
Manufacturer, service provider, seller **strictly liable** for harm caused by defective product/deficiency in service

**"Product Liability"** (Section 2(34)): Liability of manufacturer, service provider, seller for damages for harm caused by defective product/deficiency

**Liable Parties**:
1. **Product manufacturer**
2. **Product service provider** (installer, assembler, repairer)
3. **Product seller** (retailer, distributor)

**Harm** (Section 2(21)): Personal injury, death, damage to property (excluding defective product itself)

**Example**:
- Defective pressure cooker explodes → Causes injury → Manufacturer liable for personal injury + property damage
- Defective car → Accident → Manufacturer liable

### 5.2 Grounds for Product Liability (Section 84)

**Manufacturer/Service Provider/Seller liable if**:
1. Product contains **manufacturing defect**
2. Product **defective in design**
3. Product deviates from **express warranty/representations**
4. Product does NOT contain **adequate instructions** for safe use
5. Product does NOT conform to **mandatory safety standards**

**No-Fault Liability**: Consumer NOT required to prove negligence (strict liability)

### 5.3 Defenses (Section 85)

**Product manufacturer/seller NOT liable if proves**:
1. Did NOT place product in **stream of commerce**
2. Defect did NOT exist when placed in stream of commerce
3. **Compliance with mandatory government standards** (does not negate other defenses)
4. **Express written** warning of danger (and consumer voluntarily accepted risk)
5. **State of scientific/technical knowledge** at time of supply did not permit discovery of defect

---

## 6. E-COMMERCE PROTECTIONS (SECTIONS 88-94)

### 6.1 E-Commerce Entity Obligations

**"E-Commerce"** (Section 2(16)): Buying/selling goods/services through digital/electronic network

**"E-Commerce Entity"** (Section 2(17)): Company owning, operating, managing digital/electronic facility/platform for e-commerce

**Mandatory Requirements** (Section 88):
1. **Legal name**, principal office address, website
2. **General terms and conditions** (prominently displayed)
3. **Grievance redressal mechanism** (details of grievance officer)
4. **Return/refund/exchange** policies
5. **Payment methods** (security details)
6. **Delivery/shipment** timelines
7. **Seller information** (for marketplace models - name, address, contact)

**Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules 2020**:
- **Country of origin** marking (products)
- **Total price** display (including all charges)
- **No false reviews** (fake ratings/testimonials prohibited)
- **Warranties/guarantees** honoured
- **Grievance officer** appointment (respond within 48 hours)

### 6.2 Prohibited Practices

**Direct Selling** (Section 2(13)): Multi-level marketing, network marketing

**Pyramid Schemes** (illegal under Prize Chits and Money Circulation Schemes (Banning) Act 1978):
- Promise of quick returns through recruiting others
- Unsustainable business model
- CCPA can act against pyramid schemes disguised as direct selling

**Flash Sales**: Permissible if genuine (limited inventory), but NOT if artificial scarcity created to manipulate pricing

---

## 7. EXEMPTIONS AND LIMITATIONS

### 7.1 Services NOT Covered

**Free Services**: Services provided **free of charge** (no consideration)
- Example: Free education, free healthcare (government hospitals)

**Employment Contracts**: Personal services under employer-employee relationship

**BUT**:
- Government services for which **fee paid** (passport services, vehicle registration) → Covered ✅
- Educational institutions charging fees → Covered ✅ (*Maharshi Dayanand University v. Surjeet Kaur* 2010 SC)

### 7.2 Commercial Purpose Exclusion

**Buyer for commercial purpose** NOT consumer

**Test** (*Laxmi Engineering Works v. P.S.G. Industrial Institute* 1995 SC):
- If goods/services bought for **self-consumption** (personal/household use) → Consumer ✅
- If bought for **earning livelihood** by means of self-employment → Consumer ✅ (farmer buying tractor, professional buying computer)
- If bought for **commercial resale/manufacturing** → NOT consumer ❌

**Examples**:
- Doctor buying stethoscope for medical practice → Consumer ✅ (earning livelihood through self-employment)
- Shopkeeper buying goods for resale → NOT consumer ❌
- Farmer buying tractor for agriculture → Consumer ✅

---

## 8. COMPARISON: 2019 ACT VS. 1986 ACT

| Feature | 1986 Act | 2019 Act |
|---------|----------|----------|
| **CCPA** | No central authority | Central Consumer Protection Authority (CCPA) established |
| **Product Liability** | No specific provisions | Strict liability framework (Sections 82-87) |
| **E-Commerce** | No provisions | Comprehensive e-commerce protections (Sections 88-94) |
| **Pecuniary Jurisdiction** | Fixed (₹20 lakhs District, ₹1 crore State) | Increased (₹1 crore District, ₹10 crore State, beyond ₹10 crore National) |
| **Mediation** | No provision | Mediation as first step encouraged (Section 37) |
| **Penalties** | Lower | Enhanced (up to ₹50 lakhs for misleading ads) |
| **Endorser Liability** | No provision | Celebrities/endorsers liable for false ads |
| **Appeals** | To higher commission then National Commission | To State Commission, National Commission, then Supreme Court |

---

## 9. PRACTICAL GUIDANCE

### 9.1 Filing Consumer Complaint - Checklist

**Determine Jurisdiction**:
- Value ≤ ₹1 crore → **District Commission**
- Value > ₹1 crore but ≤ ₹10 crore → **State Commission**
- Value > ₹10 crore → **National Commission**

**Documents Required**:
- Complaint in prescribed form
- **Purchase receipt/invoice** (proof of transaction)
- **Warranty/guarantee card** (if applicable)
- **Correspondence** with seller/manufacturer (complaint emails, letters)
- Evidence of defect/deficiency (photos, expert reports)

**Complaint must state**:
- Consumer's name, address, contact
- Opposite party's (seller/manufacturer) details
- Facts of complaint (what, when, where)
- Relief sought (replacement, refund, compensation)
- Court fee (nominal - ₹100-500 depending on value)

### 9.2 Avoiding Unfair Trade Practices - Business Compliance

**For Businesses**:
1. **Accurate advertising**: No false/misleading claims
2. **Country of origin**: Mandatory marking (e-commerce)
3. **Total price transparency**: All charges disclosed upfront
4. **Warranty/guarantee**: Honour commitments
5. **Grievance redressal**: Functional grievance officer (48-hour response)
6. **Product safety**: Comply with BIS/statutory standards
7. **Endorsements**: Ensure celebrity endorsers have conducted due diligence
8. **Return/refund**: Clear policies, honor commitments

**CCPA Enforcement**: Non-compliance → Investigation → Penalty → Recall/refund orders

---

## 10. INTEGRATION WITH OTHER PROTOCOLS

**Related Protocols**:
- **IL-CONSUMER-002**: Consumer dispute redressal mechanisms (District, State, National Commissions)
- **IL-CONTRACT-001**: Consumer contracts (offer/acceptance)
- **IL-CPC-001**: Appeal from consumer commissions (Supreme Court)

---

## LEGAL DISCLAIMERS

**NOT LEGAL ADVICE**: This protocol provides legal information about consumer protection rights and remedies. It does NOT constitute legal advice for specific consumer disputes or compliance matters.

**NO ATTORNEY-CLIENT RELATIONSHIP**: Use of this protocol does not create any attorney-client relationship.

**SEEK PROFESSIONAL CONSULTATION**: Consumer complaints, product liability claims, and CCPA enforcement involve specific factual determinations. Consult qualified consumer law attorneys for guidance on specific disputes or business compliance.

---

## REVISION HISTORY
- **v1.0 (December 2024)**: Initial comprehensive protocol on Consumer Protection Act 2019, consumer rights, CCPA, product liability, e-commerce protections, unfair trade practices

---

**Word Count**: ~2,800 words
**Depth**: Comprehensive coverage of CP Act 2019
**Target Audience**: Consumers, businesses, e-commerce platforms, consumer advocates, legal practitioners

## IL-IP-001_patents_fundamentals.md
---

# IL-IP-001: Patents Act 1970 - Patentability and Registration

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-IP-001
- **Domain**: Indian Law - Intellectual Property
- **Category**: Patents
- **Version**: 1.0
- **Last Updated**: December 2024
- **Scope**: Patents Act 1970, patentability criteria, exclusions, registration procedure, patent rights
- **Prerequisites**: None (foundational)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-IP-002 (Patent Infringement), IL-CONTRACT-007 (licensing agreements)

## Purpose
Comprehensive coverage of Indian patent law under the Patents Act 1970 (as amended 2005, 2016, 2020), including patentability criteria (Sections 2(1)(j), 3, 4), registration procedure, patent rights, duration, and recent developments including compulsory licensing and pharmaceutical patents post-TRIPS compliance.

---

## 1. LEGISLATIVE FRAMEWORK

### 1.1 Constitutional and International Basis

**Constitution**: Patents fall under **Entry 49 List I (Union List)** - Union has exclusive legislative power

**TRIPS Agreement 1995** (Trade-Related Aspects of Intellectual Property Rights):
- India WTO member since 1995
- Obligation to comply with TRIPS minimum standards
- **2005 Amendment**: Introduced product patents for pharmaceuticals/chemicals (previously only process patents)

**Paris Convention 1883**: Priority rights, national treatment

### 1.2 Patents Act 1970 - Major Amendments

**2005 Amendment** (TRIPS Compliance):
- Section 3(d): Added to prevent evergreening (minor modifications)
- Product patents for pharmaceuticals/chemicals introduced
- Extended patent term to **20 years** from filing date

**2016 Amendment**:
- Expedited examination (Section 11-B)
- Startup fee benefits
- Working statement simplified (Form 27)

**2020 Amendment** (Patent Rules):
- Video conferencing for hearings
- Reduced timelines for opposition/responses

---

## 2. PATENTABILITY CRITERIA (SECTION 2(1)(j))

### 2.1 Definition of "Invention"

**Section 2(1)(j)**: "Invention" means a new product or process involving an **inventive step** and capable of **industrial application**.

**Three Requirements (Cumulative)**:
1. **Novelty** (New)
2. **Inventive Step** (Non-obvious)
3. **Industrial Application** (Useful)

### 2.2 Novelty (Sections 2(1)(l), 13)

**Section 2(1)(l) - "New Invention"**:
Invention NOT anticipated by **prior publication** or **prior public use** in India or elsewhere.

**Prior Art** (Section 13):
- Any knowledge, publication, use **anywhere in the world**
- Before **priority date** or filing date of patent application

**Global Novelty Standard**:
- Publication in any country (not just India) destroys novelty
- Example: Patent granted in USA for invention X → Same invention NOT novel in India

**Grace Period**: **None** (unlike USA 12-month grace period)
- Disclosure by inventor before filing → Destroys novelty
- **Best Practice**: File patent application BEFORE any public disclosure

**Exceptions to Novelty Objection**:
- Disclosure at **recognized international exhibition** (Section 30) - if filed within 12 months
- Disclosure before **learned society** OR published in transactions (Section 30) - if filed within 12 months

### 2.3 Inventive Step / Non-Obviousness (Section 2(1)(ja))

**Section 2(1)(ja)**: Feature that involves **technical advance** compared to existing knowledge or having **economic significance** or both, making invention **not obvious** to person skilled in art.

**Test**:
1. **Technical Advance**: Improvement over prior art (better efficiency, new function, superior performance)
2. **Economic Significance**: Commercial value, cost reduction
3. **Non-Obvious**: Person having **ordinary skill in art** would NOT have arrived at invention as obvious next step

**"Person Skilled in Art"**: Hypothetical person with average knowledge/skill in relevant technical field

**Example**:
- Prior Art: Drug X treats disease Y
- Invention: Combining Drug X + Drug Z treats Y more effectively
- Question: Would skilled person obviously try this combination?
  - If YES → NOT inventive ❌
  - If NO (unexpected synergistic effect) → Inventive ✅

**Evergreening Prevention** (Section 3(d) - Pharmaceutical Focus):
"Mere discovery of new form of known substance NOT patentable UNLESS it results in **enhancement of known efficacy**."

**Application**: Prevents patenting of:
- New salt/ester/polymorph of known drug (unless enhanced efficacy proved)
- New dosage form (sustained release) of known drug (unless enhanced efficacy)

**Landmark Case - *Novartis v. Union of India* (2013 SC)**:
- Novartis sought patent for beta-crystalline form of imatinib mesylate (Gleevec)
- Supreme Court: Section 3(d) violated (no enhanced **therapeutic** efficacy over known imatinib)
- Improved bioavailability alone ≠ enhanced efficacy (must show better clinical outcome)

### 2.4 Industrial Application (Section 2(1)(ac))

**Section 2(1)(ac)**: Invention capable of being **made or used in industry**.

**Broad Interpretation**: "Industry" includes agriculture, commerce, services (not just manufacturing)

**Requirement**:
- Invention must be **useful** (have practical utility)
- NOT purely theoretical/abstract

**Example**:
- Chemical compound with no known use → NOT capable of industrial application ❌
- Chemical compound as pharmaceutical ingredient → Industrial application ✅

---

## 3. NON-PATENTABLE SUBJECT MATTER (SECTIONS 3, 4)

### 3.1 Section 3 - Excluded Inventions

**Section 3(a) - Frivolous/Contrary to Law**:
Invention frivolous or contrary to natural laws

**Section 3(b) - Harmful to Public/Immoral**:
Invention causing serious prejudice to human/animal/plant life, health, or environment

**Section 3(c) - Scientific Principles/Abstract Theories**:
- Mathematical methods, algorithms
- Business methods, computer programs **per se**
- **Note**: Computer programs **with technical application** MAY be patentable (*Ferid Allani v. Union of India* 2019)

**Section 3(d) - New Form of Known Substance** (Evergreening):
Mere discovery of new form of known substance UNLESS enhanced efficacy

**Section 3(e) - Admixtures**:
Mere admixture without synergistic effect (combining ingredients with no new property)

**Section 3(f) - Known Process/Machine/Apparatus**:
Mere rearrangement/duplication

**Section 3(g) - Agricultural/Horticultural Methods**:
Methods of agriculture/horticulture

**Section 3(h) - Medical Treatment Methods**:
- Method of treatment of humans/animals
- **BUT**: Pharmaceutical products (drugs) ARE patentable (only method of treatment excluded)

**Section 3(i) - Plants/Animals**:
Plants, animals in whole or part (seeds, varieties, species)
- **Exceptions**: Microorganisms, non-biological/microbiological processes

**Section 3(j) - Traditional Knowledge**:
Invention being traditional knowledge (oral or otherwise)

**Section 3(k) - Computer Programs Per Se**:
Computer programs per se (algorithms, software as such)
- **But**: Software with **technical contribution/industrial application** patentable (inventions "involving" computer programs)

**Section 3(m) - Atomic Energy**:
Inventions related to atomic energy (Section 20 Atomic Energy Act jurisdiction)

**Section 3(n) - Biological Process**:
Traditional/biological process for propagation/multiplication of plants

**Section 3(p) - Aggregation/Presentation of Information**:
Mere scheme, rule, method of performing mental act, playing game

### 3.2 Section 4 - Plant Varieties and Atomic Energy

**Section 4**: Plant varieties NOT patentable (governed by Protection of Plant Varieties and Farmers' Rights Act 2001)

---

## 4. PATENT REGISTRATION PROCEDURE

### 4.1 Patent Application (Sections 7-11)

**Who Can Apply** (Section 6):
- True and first inventor (or legal representative)
- Assignee of invention
- Legal representative of deceased inventor

**Forms**:
- **Form 1**: Application for Patent
- **Form 2**: Provisional/Complete Specification
- **Form 3**: Statement and Undertaking (Section 8 - foreign applications)
- **Form 5**: Declaration of Inventorship

**Provisional vs. Complete Specification**:
- **Provisional** (Section 9(1)): Filed to secure priority date, broad description (no claims)
  - Must file complete within **12 months** or application deemed withdrawn
- **Complete** (Section 9(3)): Detailed description + claims defining invention scope

**Priority Date** (Section 11):
- **Indian application**: Filing date (provisional OR complete)
- **Convention application**: Foreign filing date (if filed in India within 12 months under Paris Convention)

### 4.2 Examination (Sections 11-B, 12-14)

**Publication** (Section 11-A):
- Application published **18 months** after priority date (in patent journal)
- Early publication possible on request (Form 9)

**Request for Examination** (Section 11-B):
- **Mandatory** within **48 months** of priority date (or application deemed withdrawn)
- Examination fee payable

**Examination Report** (Section 14):
- Patent Office issues First Examination Report (FER) identifying objections
- Applicant must respond within **6 months** (or 12 months from filing)

**Common Objections**:
- Lack of novelty, inventive step, industrial application
- Non-patentable subject matter (Section 3)
- Insufficient disclosure (Section 10)
- Claims not clear/supported by description

**Response Strategy**:
- **Amendments** to claims/description (narrow scope, clarify, add technical details)
- **Arguments** distinguishing prior art, demonstrating inventive step
- **Evidence** (affidavits, technical reports, comparative data)

### 4.3 Grant or Refusal (Sections 15, 25)

**Grant** (Section 43):
If objections satisfied → Patent granted
- Patent Certificate issued (Form 7)
- Patent details published in journal

**Refusal** (Section 15):
If objections not satisfied → Application refused

**Pre-Grant Opposition** (Section 25(1)):
- **Any person** may file opposition **after publication** (18 months) but before grant
- Grounds: Non-patentability (Section 3), lack of novelty/inventive step, insufficiency, non-disclosure of foreign applications

**Hearing** (Section 14):
Applicant and opponent given oral hearing opportunity

---

## 5. PATENT RIGHTS AND DURATION

### 5.1 Exclusive Rights (Section 48)

**Patent grants exclusive right** to:
1. **Make** the patented product
2. **Use** the patented product
3. **Offer for sale** the patented product
4. **Sell/import** the patented product
5. **License** others (grant permission for above acts)

**For Process Patents**:
- Right to use process
- Right to prevent use of process
- Right to prevent use/sale of product **directly obtained by patented process**

### 5.2 Duration (Section 53)

**20 years** from **filing date** (priority date for convention applications)

**NOT extendable** (unlike trademarks which are renewable indefinitely)

**Calculation Example**:
- Filing date: January 1, 2020
- Patent expires: December 31, 2039 (20 years from filing)

### 5.3 Annual Renewal Fees (Section 53)

**Payment Required**: Annually from **3rd year** onwards

**Consequences of Non-Payment**:
- **6-month grace period** with additional fee
- If not paid within grace period → Patent **lapses**
- Restoration possible within 18 months (Section 60 - if failure unintentional)

---

## 6. WORKING OF PATENTS (SECTION 84-92)

### 6.1 Section 146 Working Statement

**Obligation** (Section 146):
Patentee must file annual statement of **working of patent** in India (Form 27)

**Due Date**: Within 6 months of end of each calendar year (by June 30 each year)

**Purpose**: Monitor whether patent worked in India (compulsory licensing ground)

**Content**:
- Quantity of patented product manufactured in India
- Imports (if any)
- Licenses granted

### 6.2 Compulsory Licensing (Sections 84-92)

**Grounds** (Section 84(1)):
After **3 years** from grant, compulsory license may be granted if:
1. Reasonable requirements of public **not satisfied** (product not available/affordable), OR
2. Patented invention **not worked in India** (no manufacture), OR
3. Patented product **not available at reasonably affordable price**

**Procedure**:
- Application to Patent Controller
- Patentee given hearing opportunity
- Controller may grant license with conditions (royalty rate, territory, duration)

**Landmark Case - *Natco v. Bayer* (2013)**:
- First compulsory license granted post-2005 Amendment
- Bayer's kidney/liver cancer drug Nexavar (sorafenib tosylate)
- Grounds: Not available at reasonably affordable price (₹2.8 lakhs/month), not worked in India
- Compulsory license granted to Natco at **6% royalty**

**Government Use** (Section 100):
- Government may use patented invention for its own purposes
- Royalty paid to patentee (negotiated or determined by Government)

---

## 7. POST-GRANT OPPOSITION (SECTION 25(2))

**Any Person** may file opposition **after grant** within **12 months** of grant

**Grounds**: Same as pre-grant opposition (Section 25(1))
- Non-patentability
- Lack of novelty, inventive step
- Wrongful obtainment
- Insufficiency/non-disclosure

**Hearing**: Both parties heard

**Revocation Possible**: Patent may be revoked if grounds proved

---

## 8. PHARMACEUTICAL PATENTS - SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS

### 8.1 Section 3(d) Application

**Purpose**: Prevent evergreening (extending patent life through minor modifications)

**Test**: New form must show **enhanced known efficacy**
- For drugs: Enhanced **therapeutic** efficacy (better clinical outcome)
- Improved bioavailability alone ≠ enhanced efficacy (*Novartis* 2013)

**Examples**:
- New salt of known drug → NOT patentable (unless enhanced efficacy) ❌
- New polymorph (crystalline form) → NOT patentable (unless enhanced efficacy) ❌
- New combination of known drugs → Patentable if synergistic effect (enhanced efficacy) ✅

### 8.2 TRIPS Flexibilities

**Bolar Provision** (Section 107-A):
- Exceptions to patent infringement for **regulatory approval purposes**
- Generic manufacturers can conduct studies/trials before patent expiry (for marketing authorization)

**Parallel Imports** (Section 107-A(b)):
- Import of patented product from abroad (where lawfully sold) NOT infringement
- Exhaustion of rights doctrine

---

## 9. PRACTICAL GUIDANCE

### 9.1 Patent Search Strategy

**Before Filing**:
1. **Prior Art Search**: Databases (Indian Patent Office, USPTO, EPO, Google Patents)
2. **Freedom to Operate (FTO) Search**: Ensure commercialization doesn't infringe others' patents
3. **Patentability Opinion**: From patent agent/attorney

**Databases**:
- Indian Patent Office (ipindiaservices.gov.in)
- USPTO (patents.google.com)
- Espacenet (European Patent Office)

### 9.2 Drafting Claims

**Independent Claims**: Broad scope (cover invention essence)
**Dependent Claims**: Narrow scope (specific embodiments, fall-back positions)

**Best Practices**:
- Draft **multiple independent claims** (different aspects of invention)
- Use functional language where possible (broader protection)
- Include fall-back positions (if broad claims rejected)

### 9.3 Timeline Management

| Milestone | Deadline | Consequence |
|-----------|----------|-------------|
| Complete specification after provisional | 12 months | Application deemed withdrawn |
| Request for examination | 48 months from priority date | Application deemed withdrawn |
| Response to FER | 6 months (or 12 months from filing) | Application deemed abandoned |
| Pre-grant opposition | After publication, before grant | Patent may not be granted |
| Post-grant opposition | 12 months from grant | Patent may be revoked |
| Annual renewal fees | From 3rd year, 6-month grace period | Patent lapses |

---

## 10. INTEGRATION WITH OTHER PROTOCOLS

**Related Protocols**:
- **IL-IP-002**: Patent Infringement and Remedies
- **IL-CONTRACT-007**: Licensing Agreements (patent licensing)
- **IL-ADR-002**: Arbitrability of IP disputes

---

## LEGAL DISCLAIMERS

**NOT LEGAL ADVICE**: This protocol provides legal information about Indian patent law. It does NOT constitute legal advice or patentability opinions for specific inventions.

**NO ATTORNEY-CLIENT RELATIONSHIP**: Use of this protocol does not create any attorney-client or patent agent-client relationship.

**SEEK PROFESSIONAL CONSULTATION**: Patent prosecution, patentability assessments, and freedom-to-operate opinions require expertise of registered patent agents/attorneys. Always consult qualified patent professionals before filing applications or commercializing inventions.

---

## REVISION HISTORY
- **v1.0 (December 2024)**: Initial comprehensive protocol on patentability criteria, registration procedure, pharmaceutical patents, compulsory licensing, Section 3(d) evergreening prevention

---

**Word Count**: ~3,800 words
**Depth**: Comprehensive coverage of Patents Act 1970
**Target Audience**: Inventors, R&D teams, patent agents, in-house IP counsel


---
## IL-IP-002_trademarks_registration.md
---

# IL-IP-002: Trademarks Act 1999 - Registration and Protection

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-IP-002
- **Domain**: Indian Law - Intellectual Property
- **Category**: Trademarks
- **Version**: 1.0
- **Last Updated**: December 2024
- **Scope**: Trademarks Act 1999, trademark registration, distinctiveness, infringement, passing off
- **Prerequisites**: None (foundational)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-IP-001 (Patents), IL-CONTRACT-007 (Licensing)

## Purpose
Comprehensive coverage of Indian trademark law under Trademarks Act 1999 (as amended 2010, 2017, 2021), including trademark registrability, registration procedure, rights, infringement, passing off remedy, and well-known marks protection.

---

## 1. LEGISLATIVE FRAMEWORK

### 1.1 Definition of Trademark (Section 2(1)(zb))

**"Trademark"** means a mark capable of:
1. Being represented **graphically** (visually perceptible)
2. Capable of **distinguishing** goods/services of one person from others

**Includes**:
- Shape of goods
- Packaging
- Combination of colors

**"Mark"** (Section 2(1)(m)): Device, brand, heading, label, ticket, name, signature, word, letter, numeral, shape of goods, packaging, combination of colors, or any combination thereof

### 1.2 Types of Marks

**Word Marks**: Text-based (brand names, slogans)
- Example: "TATA", "COCA-COLA"

**Device/Logo Marks**: Graphic symbols, logos
- Example: Nike swoosh, Apple bitten apple

**Combined Marks**: Word + device
- Example: "McDonald's" text + golden arches

**Shape Marks**: 3D shapes (product shape, packaging)
- Example: Coca-Cola bottle shape

**Sound Marks**: Audio (jingles, tunes)
- Registrable if graphically represented (notation/sonogram)
- Example: Yahoo yodel, Intel chime

**Smell/Scent Marks**: Fragrances (limited acceptance in India)

**Certification Marks** (Section 2(1)(e)): Certify origin, quality, accuracy
- Example: ISI mark, Woolmark

**Collective Marks** (Section 2(1)(g)): Used by members of association
- Example: CPA (Chartered Accountants association mark)

**Well-Known Marks** (Section 2(1)(zg)): High reputation, extensive use
- Protection beyond registered classes

---

## 2. REGISTRABILITY CRITERIA

### 2.1 Absolute Grounds for Refusal (Section 9)

**Section 9(1) - Inherently Non-Distinctive Marks**:

**(a) Not Capable of Distinguishing**:
Mark lacks distinctiveness (cannot identify source)

**(b) Descriptive Marks**:
Marks designating kind, quality, quantity, intended purpose, value, geographical origin, time of production

**Examples**:
- "COLD" for refrigerators ❌ (descriptive of quality)
- "BEST" for any product ❌ (laudatory/non-distinctive)
- "MANGALORE" for mangoes from Mangalore ❌ (geographical origin)

**(c) Generic/Common Marks**:
Marks consisting exclusively of marks/indications that have become **customary in current language/trade**

**Example**: "Escalator" (originally TM, now generic)

**(d) Shapes**:
- Shape resulting from **nature of goods** (e.g., football shape for football)
- Shape necessary to obtain **technical result** (functional shape)
- Shape giving **substantial value** to goods (ornamental shape)

**Proviso - Acquired Distinctiveness**:
Marks under Section 9(1)(b), (c) **MAY be registered** if acquired **distinctiveness through use** (secondary meaning)

**Test for Acquired Distinctiveness**:
- Extensive use (duration, volume)
- Advertising expenditure
- Market surveys showing public association
- Registration in other countries

**Example**: "TATA" (surname, descriptive) acquired distinctiveness through 150+ years use

### 2.2 Relative Grounds for Refusal (Section 11)

**Section 11(1) - Identical/Deceptively Similar**:
Mark shall NOT be registered if:
- **Identical** with earlier trademark (same class), OR
- **Deceptively similar** (likely to confuse/deceive)

**Deceptive Similarity Test** (*Amritdhara Pharmacy v. Satya Deo Gupta* 1963 SC):
Marks must be compared as **wholes** (not dissected)
- Visual similarity (appearance)
- Phonetic similarity (sound when spoken)
- Conceptual similarity (idea suggested)

**Average Consumer Test**:
- Person of **average intelligence and imperfect recollection**
- First impression (not side-by-side comparison)

**Examples**:
- COLGATE vs. COALGATE → Deceptively similar ✅ (phonetically similar)
- PARLE vs. PARLE-G → NOT deceptively similar ❌ (different visual, established co-existence)

**Section 11(2) - Similarity in Related Goods**:
Mark may be refused if similar to earlier mark in **related goods/services** (not necessarily same class)

**Section 11(6) - Identical/Similar to Well-Known Mark**:
Mark identical/similar to **well-known mark** (even different class) → Refusal
- Protection **beyond registered classes**
- Prevents dilution of well-known marks

**Examples of Well-Known Marks** (recognized by Indian courts):
- GOOGLE, MICROSOFT, COCA-COLA, BMW, ROLEX

### 2.3 Other Grounds for Refusal

**Section 9(2) - Prohibited Marks**:
- **(a)** Contains scandalous/obscene matter
- **(b)** Prohibited under **Emblems and Names (Prevention of Improper Use) Act 1950** (government emblems, UN flag, Red Cross)

**Section 9(3) - Geographical Indications**:
Marks consisting of geographical indications (protected under GI Act 1999)

**Example**: "DARJEELING" for tea (protected GI)

---

## 3. NICE CLASSIFICATION (SECTION 9-A)

### 3.1 Classes of Goods and Services

**45 Classes Total**:
- **Classes 1-34**: Goods
- **Classes 35-45**: Services

**Common Classes**:
- Class 5: Pharmaceutical products
- Class 9: Electronics, software
- Class 25: Clothing
- Class 35: Advertising, business management services
- Class 42: Scientific/technological services, IT services

**One Application Per Class**:
Separate applications for each class (separate fees)

**Cross-Class Protection**:
Registration in Class A does NOT automatically prevent registration of similar mark in Class B (unless well-known mark)

---

## 4. TRADEMARK REGISTRATION PROCEDURE

### 4.1 Application (Section 18)

**Form TM-A**: Application for Trademark Registration

**Information Required**:
- Applicant details (name, address, nationality)
- Trademark representation (image/text)
- **Class** of goods/services
- **Date of first use** (if claiming prior use)
- Priority claim (if Convention application)

**Fees**: Based on class and applicant type
- Individuals/startups: Lower fees
- Others: Higher fees

### 4.2 Examination (Section 18(3))

**Trademark Registry** examines application for:
- **Absolute grounds** (Section 9) - Distinctiveness, descriptiveness
- **Relative grounds** (Section 11) - Similarity to earlier marks
- **Formal requirements** - Proper classification, representation

**Examination Report**:
If objections → Issued to applicant

**Response** (Section 18(4)):
Within **1 month** (extendable)
- Arguments addressing objections
- Evidence of acquired distinctiveness (if Section 9(1) objection)
- Amendments to specification of goods/services

**Hearing**: If objections persist, oral hearing granted

### 4.3 Publication and Opposition (Sections 20-21)

**Advertisement** (Section 20):
If accepted → Published in **Trademark Journal** (weekly)

**Opposition** (Section 21):
**Any person** may file opposition within **4 months** of publication (extendable 1 month)

**Grounds for Opposition**:
- Non-registrability (Sections 9, 11)
- Prior use by opponent
- Passing off likelihood

**Counter-Statement**: Applicant files counter-statement (within 2 months)

**Evidence**: Both parties file evidence

**Hearing**: Registrar hears both sides

**Decision**:
- Opposition upheld → Application **refused**
- Opposition dismissed → Application proceeds to **registration**

### 4.4 Registration (Section 23)

**Certificate of Registration** issued

**Rights Effective**: From **date of application** (priority given from filing date)

**Validity**: **10 years** from date of registration

**Renewal**: Indefinitely renewable every 10 years (unlike patents with fixed 20-year term)

---

## 5. TRADEMARK RIGHTS (SECTION 28)

### 5.1 Exclusive Rights

**Registered Proprietor** has **exclusive right**:
- Use trademark in relation to registered goods/services
- **Prevent unauthorized use** by others (infringement action)
- **License** trademark to others (Section 48)
- **Assign/sell** trademark (Section 37)

**Infringement** (Section 29):
Unauthorized use of **identical/deceptively similar** mark by third party

### 5.2 Permitted Uses (No Infringement)

**Section 30 - Defenses to Infringement**:

**(1) Registered Proprietor's Use**:
Use of registered trademark by proprietor or licensee

**(2)(a) Own Name/Address**:
Use of person's **own name/address** (bona fide)
- Example: Mahendra Singh Dhoni uses "MS Dhoni" (his name) → Not infringement of MS Dhoni trademark

**(2)(b) Descriptive Use**:
Use indicating **kind, quality, quantity, intended purpose** (not as trademark)
- Example: "Extra Strong Coffee" (descriptive, not trademark use)

**(2)(c) Comparative Advertising**:
Use for **honest** comparison (not disparaging)

**Section 33 - Prior Use Defense**:
User who used mark **continuously from before registration** may continue (limited to extent of prior use)

---

## 6. INFRINGEMENT (SECTION 29)

### 6.1 Types of Infringement

**Section 29(1) - Identical Mark, Identical Goods**:
Use of **identical mark** for **identical goods/services** → Infringement (strict liability)

**Section 29(2) - Similarity Causing Confusion**:
Use of **similar mark** for **similar goods/services** if likely to cause **confusion** (including association)

**Test**:
- Similarity of marks (visual, phonetic, conceptual)
- Similarity of goods/services
- Likelihood of confusion among average consumers

**Section 29(4) - Well-Known Marks**:
Use of identical/similar mark (even different goods) to take **unfair advantage** or cause **detriment** to well-known mark

**Section 29(9) - Spoken Use**:
Oral reference to trademark (phonetic infringement)

### 6.2 Remedies (Sections 134-135)

**Civil Remedies** (Section 134):
- **Injunction** (interim and permanent) - Prevent further infringement
- **Damages** - Compensatory damages OR account of profits (disgorgement)
- **Delivery up** - Surrender infringing goods for destruction

**Criminal Remedies** (Section 103):
- Imprisonment up to **3 years**
- Fine up to ₹**2 lakhs**
- Both (for serious/repeat offenses)

**Border Measures** (Sections 139-141):
- Customs authorities may **seize** infringing goods at border (upon notice from trademark owner)

---

## 7. PASSING OFF (COMMON LAW REMEDY)

### 7.1 Basis and Requirements

**NOT Statutory** (common law tort remedy)

**Available to**:
- Unregistered trademark users
- Registered trademark owners (concurrent remedy with infringement)

**Test** (*Reckitt & Colman v. Borden Inc.* "Jif Lemon" case):
Three requirements:
1. **Goodwill/Reputation** in the mark
2. **Misrepresentation** by defendant (likely to deceive)
3. **Damage** (actual or likely) to plaintiff's goodwill

**Goodwill**: Acquired through use (not registration)
- Extensive use in India
- Public association with plaintiff

**Misrepresentation**:
- Use of similar mark/trade dress/packaging
- Creating impression of connection with plaintiff

**Damage**:
- Loss of sales, confusion, dilution of distinctiveness

### 7.2 Passing Off vs. Infringement

| Feature | Passing Off | Trademark Infringement |
|---------|-------------|------------------------|
| **Basis** | Common law (tort) | Statute (Trademarks Act) |
| **Registration Required** | NO | YES (for infringement suit) |
| **Proof** | Goodwill + Misrepresentation + Damage | Use of registered mark without authorization |
| **Burden** | Plaintiff must prove all three | Lighter burden (registration = prima facie validity) |
| **Scope** | Unlimited (any goods/services if confusion) | Limited to registered classes (except well-known marks) |

---

## 8. WELL-KNOWN MARKS (SECTION 11(6)-(9))

### 8.1 Protection

**Enhanced Protection**:
- Protection **beyond registered classes**
- NO need to prove use/registration in all classes

**Example**: GOOGLE (registered for IT services) protected against GOOGLE shoes, GOOGLE cars (not IT services)

**Rationale**: Prevent **dilution**, **tarnishment**, **unfair advantage** of well-known marks' reputation

### 8.2 Determining Well-Known Status (Section 11(6))

**Factors** (non-exhaustive):
- Knowledge/recognition among **relevant public**
- Duration, extent, geographical area of **use**
- Duration, extent of **promotion/advertising**
- Records of **successful enforcement** (litigation success)
- **Registrations** in multiple jurisdictions

**Procedure**:
- Proprietor may claim well-known status in application/opposition
- Registrar/court determines based on evidence

**Central List**: Trademark Registry maintains list of recognized well-known marks (updated periodically)

---

## 9. RENEWAL AND RESTORATION (SECTIONS 25, 60)

### 9.1 Renewal (Section 25)

**Validity**: **10 years** from registration date

**Renewal**: Indefinitely renewable every 10 years

**Renewal Application**:
- File within **6 months** before expiry (early renewal)
- **6-month grace period** after expiry (with additional fee)

**Failure to Renew**: Trademark removed from register

**Effects of Removal**:
- Rights cease
- Mark available for third-party registration (after 1 year - Section 25(3))

### 9.2 Restoration (Section 60)

**If removed for non-renewal**:
- Restoration application within **1 year** of removal
- Discretionary relief (if Registrar satisfied non-renewal unintentional/reasonable)

---

## 10. ASSIGNMENT AND LICENSING

### 10.1 Assignment (Section 37)

**Trademark assignable** (registered or unregistered)

**With or Without Goodwill**:
- **With goodwill**: All rights transferred (new owner exclusive user)
- **Without goodwill**: Rare (prior owner may continue use)

**Recordation** (Section 45):
- Assignment must be **recorded** with Trademark Registry (within 6 months)
- Unrecorded assignment → Assignee cannot sue for infringement

**Formalities**:
- Written deed
- Stamp duty payable (state-specific)

### 10.2 Licensing (Section 48)

**Registered User** (now called "Permitted User"):
Licensee using trademark under license from proprietor

**Recording**:
- License recorded with Registry
- Protects licensee's rights (can sue infringers if registered)

**Quality Control**:
- Proprietor must exercise **quality control** over licensee's use (prevent abandonment)
- Naked licensing (no control) → Trademark may be cancelled (Section 47)

---

## 11. PRACTICAL GUIDANCE

### 11.1 Trademark Selection Strategy

**Strong Marks** (Highly Distinctive):
- **Fanciful**: Invented words (KODAK, XEROX)
- **Arbitrary**: Common words unrelated to product (APPLE for computers)

**Weaker Marks** (May face objections):
- **Suggestive**: Hint at product nature (COPPERTONE for suntan lotion)
- **Descriptive**: Describe product (COLD for refrigerators) - Need acquired distinctiveness
- **Generic**: Common name (cannot be trademark)

**Best Practice**: Choose fanciful/arbitrary marks for easier registration and stronger protection

### 11.2 Clearance Search

**Before Adoption**:
- **Trademark search**: Indian Trademark Registry database, pending applications
- **Domain name search**: Availability of .in, .com domains
- **Business name search**: ROC company name database
- **Common law search**: Google, industry databases (unregistered marks)

**Clearance Opinion**: From trademark attorney assessing registration/infringement risks

### 11.3 Use Requirements

**Actual Use**:
- Trademark must be **used** (not merely registered warehoused)
- **Non-use for 5 years** → Vulnerable to removal for non-use (Section 47)

**Bona Fide Use**:
- Commercial use in ordinary course of trade
- Token use NOT sufficient

**Maintaining Rights**:
- Continuous use
- Quality control (if licensed)
- Timely renewal
- Enforcement against infringers (prevent genericization)

---

## 12. DOMAIN NAMES AND TRADEMARKS

### 12.1 Cybersquatting

**Definition**: Registering domain name identical/similar to another's trademark to profit

**Remedies**:
1. **Trademark infringement** (if domain use amounts to trademark use)
2. **Passing off** (if goodwill + misrepresentation + damage)
3. **UDRP** (Uniform Domain Name Dispute Resolution Policy) - ICANN mechanism
   - For .com, .net, .org (international TLDs)
4. **INDRP** (Indian Domain Name Dispute Resolution Policy)
   - For .in domains (administered by NIXI)

**Test** (*Yahoo Inc. v. Akash Arora* 1999 Delhi HC - first Indian cybersquatting case):
- Plaintiff has trademark rights
- Defendant's domain identical/deceptively similar
- Defendant registered in bad faith (to profit/prevent trademark owner)

---

## 13. LIMITATIONS AND EXCEPTIONS

**Concurrent Use** (Section 12):
If two parties using identical/similar marks in different geographical areas **bona fide** before registration → Both may register (with territorial limitations)

**Honest Concurrent Use**: Long, honest, simultaneous use by multiple parties

**Defensive Trademark** (Section 2(1)(ea)):
Well-known trademark registered for non-used classes (to prevent third-party use)

---

## 14. INTEGRATION WITH OTHER PROTOCOLS

**Related Protocols**:
- **IL-IP-001**: Patents (patent-trademark overlap for product shapes)
- **IL-CONTRACT-007**: Licensing agreements (trademark licensing)
- **IL-ADR-002**: Arbitrability of IP disputes

---

## LEGAL DISCLAIMERS

**NOT LEGAL ADVICE**: This protocol provides legal information about Indian trademark law. It does NOT constitute legal advice or trademark clearance opinions.

**NO ATTORNEY-CLIENT RELATIONSHIP**: Use of this protocol does not create any attorney-client or trademark agent-client relationship.

**SEEK PROFESSIONAL CONSULTATION**: Trademark selection, clearance searches, and registration require expertise of registered trademark agents/attorneys. Always consult qualified trademark professionals before adopting marks or filing applications.

---

## REVISION HISTORY
- **v1.0 (December 2024)**: Initial comprehensive protocol on trademark registrability, registration procedure, infringement, passing off, well-known marks, domain names

---

**Word Count**: ~3,700 words
**Depth**: Comprehensive coverage of Trademarks Act 1999
**Target Audience**: Brand managers, marketing teams, trademark agents, in-house IP counsel
