## IL-LIMITATION-001_fundamentals.md
---

# IL-LIMITATION-001: Limitation Act 1963 - Fundamentals and Key Periods

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-LIMITATION-001
- **Domain**: Indian Law - Limitation
- **Category**: Limitation Periods
- **Version**: 1.0
- **Last Updated**: December 2024
- **Scope**: Limitation Act 1963, fundamental principles, key articles, extension/exclusion, condonation
- **Prerequisites**: None (foundational)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CPC-001 (appeals), IL-CONTRACT-007 (breach remedies), IL-SRA-001 (specific performance)

## Purpose
Comprehensive coverage of Limitation Act 1963 providing statutory time limits for filing suits, appeals, and applications. Covers fundamental principles (Sections 2-25), key Schedule Articles, exclusion/extension provisions, and application to contracts, property, torts, and procedural matters.

---

## 1. LEGISLATIVE FRAMEWORK

### 1.1 Purpose and Scope

**Limitation Act 1963** (effective January 1, 1964):
- Replaced Limitation Act 1908
- Prescribes **time limits** for filing:
  - **Suits** (civil claims in courts)
  - **Appeals** (from decrees/orders)
  - **Applications** (to courts, tribunals, authorities)

**Purpose** (*Consolidated Engineering Enterprises v. Irrigation Department* 2008 SC):
1. **Legal certainty**: Disputes resolved within reasonable time
2. **Prevent stale claims**: Evidence deteriorates, witnesses disappear over time
3. **Presumption of satisfaction**: Delay creates inference of payment/waiver
4. **Protect defendants**: From indefinite threat of litigation

**Constitutional Basis**: Entry 13 List III (Concurrent List) - Limitation

### 1.2 Application (Section 1)

**Extends to**: Whole of India (except Jammu & Kashmir - until 2019)

**Applies to**:
- Suits in civil courts
- Appeals to civil courts
- Applications to civil courts
- Appeals/applications to tribunals (if Limitation Act not excluded by specific statute)

**Does NOT apply to**:
- Criminal proceedings (Criminal Procedure Code has own limitation)
- Revenue proceedings (unless Limitation Act expressly made applicable)
- Specific statutes with own limitation provisions (Consumer Protection Act, Arbitration Act in some respects)

---

## 2. FUNDAMENTAL PRINCIPLES

### 2.1 When Limitation Begins (Section 3)

**Section 3 - Bar of Limitation**:
"Subject to the provisions contained in sections 4 to 24 (inclusive), every suit instituted, appeal preferred, and application made after the **prescribed period** shall be dismissed, although limitation has not been set up as a defence."

**Effect**:
- Limitation is **substantive law** (not mere procedural technicality)
- Court must dismiss suit/appeal/application if time-barred (even if defendant does not raise objection)
- **Mandatory** dismissal (no discretion except under Section 5 condonation)

### 2.2 Computation of Period (Sections 4-12)

**Section 4 - Exclusion of Time in Legal Proceedings**:
If person prosecuting suit/appeal/application is prosecuting **another civil proceeding bona fide** (relating to same matter in court of first instance or appeal):
- Period during which such proceeding continues **excluded**

**Example**:
- Suit filed in wrong court (lack of jurisdiction) → Dismissed after 1 year
- Time for re-filing (if fresh suit possible): Limitation period **excludes** 1 year spent in wrong court

**Section 5 - Extension of Prescribed Period (Condonation)**:
"Any appeal or any application, other than an application under any of the provisions of Order XXI of the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908, may be admitted after the prescribed period if the appellant or the applicant satisfies the court that he had **sufficient cause** for not preferring the appeal or making the application within such period."

**Key Points**:
- Applies to **appeals and applications** (NOT suits - no condonation for suits except under special statutes)
- **Sufficient cause**: Liberal interpretation (*N. Balakrishnan v. M. Krishnamurthy* 1998 SC - justice-oriented approach)
- Maximum extension: **NO statutory limit** (unlike CPC appeals with fixed extended period)

**Sufficient Cause Examples**:
- ✅ Bonafide ignorance of order (no knowledge)
- ✅ Serious illness of appellant/lawyer
- ✅ Natural calamity (floods, earthquakes - COVID-19 impact)
- ✅ Postal delay in receiving order
- ❌ Negligence, laches (delay without explanation)
- ❌ Impecuniosity alone (poverty NOT sufficient cause per se)

**Section 12 - Exclusions in Computing Period**:
The following periods **excluded**:
- Day from which period begins (count from next day)
- Time during which court closed (public holiday falling on last day extends deadline to next working day)

**Example - Computation**:
- Limitation period: 90 days
- Decree passed: January 1, 2024
- Start counting: January 2, 2024 (exclude January 1)
- Last day: April 1, 2024
- If April 1 is Sunday (court closed) → Deadline extends to April 2, 2024

**Section 14 - Exclusion of Time in Certain Cases (Prior Litigation)**:
"In computing the period of limitation for any suit, the time during which the plaintiff has been **prosecuting with due diligence another civil proceeding**, whether in a court of first instance or in a court of appeal or revision, against the defendant shall be excluded."

**Requirements**:
1. **Same cause of action**
2. **Same parties** (or their representatives)
3. **Bona fide** prosecution
4. **Due diligence** (not abandoned)

**Example**:
- Suit filed in District Court X (lacks territorial jurisdiction) → Dismissed after 18 months
- Plaintiff files fresh suit in District Court Y (proper jurisdiction)
- Limitation for fresh suit: Original limitation period (3 years) **minus time already elapsed** (minus 18 months if prior suit was bona fide and with due diligence) = 1.5 years available

### 2.3 When Cause of Action Arises (Section 2(b))

**"Cause of Action"** (not defined in Act, judicial interpretation):
- Bundle of essential facts which plaintiff must prove to succeed
- Arises when **right is infringed** (not when damage occurs, unless damage is element of right)

**For Contracts** (Section 19):
- **Breach of contract**: From date of breach
- **Continuing breach**: Fresh cause of action each day (but usually limitation runs from first breach)

**For Torts**:
- **Negligence**: From date of accident/injury
- **Defamation**: From date of publication

**For Property**:
- **Possession**: From date of dispossession
- **Specific performance**: From date fixed for performance OR when performance refused

---

## 3. KEY SCHEDULE ARTICLES

### 3.1 Contract-Related Articles

**Article 54 - Specific Performance (3 years)**:
"For specific performance of a contract" - **3 years** from date fixed for performance, or if no date fixed, when plaintiff has notice of refusal

**Article 55 - Compensation for Breach of Contract (3 years)**:
"For compensation for the breach of any contract, express or implied" - **3 years** from date of breach

**Article 58 - Suit on Contract (3 years)**:
"For relief on the ground of mistake" - **3 years** from date when mistake becomes known to plaintiff

**Article 113 - Suit on Contract (3 years - Residual)**:
"Any suit for which no period of limitation is provided elsewhere in this schedule" - **3 years** from date when right to sue accrues

**Example**:
- Contract for sale of property dated January 1, 2024
- Performance date: March 1, 2024
- Seller refuses to perform on March 1, 2024
- Limitation for specific performance: **3 years from March 1, 2024** (until March 1, 2027)

### 3.2 Property-Related Articles

**Article 65 - Possession of Immovable Property (12 years)**:
"To recover possession of immovable property" - **12 years** from date of dispossession

**Article 64 - Declaration of Title (12 years)**:
"For a declaration and consequential relief" - **12 years**

**Example**:
- Person dispossessed from land on January 1, 2020
- Limitation to file suit for recovery of possession: **12 years** (until January 1, 2032)

### 3.3 Tort-Related Articles

**Article 109 - Wrongful Act Causing Injury (3 years)**:
"For compensation for any wrongful act done to the person or movable property" - **3 years** from date of injury

**Article 95 - Damages for Defamation (1 year)**:
"For compensation for libel" - **1 year** from publication

### 3.4 Procedural Articles

**Article 116 - Appeal from Decree (90 days)**:
"Appeal from a decree or order" - **90 days** from date of decree (for most civil appeals)

**Article 117 - Appeal from Order (30/60 days)**:
"Appeal from an order under the Code of Civil Procedure" - **30 days** (some orders 60 days)

**Article 137 - Residual (3 years)**:
"Any application for which no period of limitation is provided elsewhere in this Schedule" - **3 years** from date when right to apply accrues

---

## 4. EXTENSION AND EXCLUSION

### 4.1 Acknowledgment (Section 18)

**Section 18 - Acknowledgment Extends Limitation**:
If before limitation expires, person liable **acknowledges liability** in writing signed by him:
- **Fresh limitation period** starts from date of acknowledgment

**Requirements**:
1. **Written** acknowledgment
2. **Signed** by person liable (or authorized agent)
3. Made **before limitation expires**
4. **Admits liability** (clearly recognizes debt/obligation)

**Example**:
- Debt due: January 1, 2020 (limitation expires January 1, 2023 - 3 years)
- Debtor writes letter on December 1, 2022: "I acknowledge owing you ₹5 lakhs. Will pay soon."
- Fresh limitation: **3 years from December 1, 2022** (until December 1, 2025)

**Acknowledgment Needs NO Fresh Consideration**:
- Mere acknowledgment extends limitation (unlike fresh contract which requires consideration)

### 4.2 Part Payment (Section 19)

**Section 19 - Part Payment Extends Limitation**:
If before limitation expires, person liable makes **part payment** of debt/legacy:
- **Fresh limitation** starts from date of payment

**Requirements**:
1. **Actual payment** (not mere promise)
2. Made **before limitation expires**
3. Payment of **part** (not full debt, which extinguishes claim)

**Example**:
- Loan of ₹10 lakhs due January 1, 2020 (limitation expires January 1, 2023)
- Debtor pays ₹1 lakh on December 1, 2022
- Fresh limitation: **3 years from December 1, 2022**

### 4.3 Fraud/Mistake (Section 17)

**Section 17 - Exclusion in Case of Fraud or Mistake**:
If suit is for relief from consequences of **fraud** or **mistake**:
- Limitation does NOT begin until plaintiff **discovers** fraud/mistake (or could with reasonable diligence have discovered)

**Example**:
- Contract induced by fraud (January 1, 2020)
- Plaintiff discovers fraud on January 1, 2023
- Limitation for rescission: **3 years from January 1, 2023** (discovery date, not contract date)

### 4.4 Disability (Sections 6-8)

**Section 6 - Legal Disability**:
If person entitled to institute suit is:
- **Minor** (under 18 years)
- **Insane**, OR
- **Idiot**

**Effect**: Limitation begins when disability ceases (person attains majority/recovers sanity)

**Maximum Period** (Section 7): Even with disability, suit must be filed within **maximum 30 years** from accrual of right

**Example**:
- Cause of action arises when plaintiff is minor (10 years old)
- Normal limitation: 3 years
- Limitation begins when plaintiff turns 18 (attains majority)
- Plaintiff has **3 years from turning 18** to file suit (until age 21)

---

## 5. COVID-19 IMPACT (SUPREME COURT ORDERS 2020-2021)

### 5.1 Extension by Supreme Court

**Suo Motu Writ Petition (Civil) No. 3/2020**:
- Supreme Court **extended all limitation periods** from March 15, 2020 to March 14, 2021 (initially)
- Further extended until October 2, 2021

**Effect**:
- Period from **March 15, 2020 to October 2, 2021** (531 days) **excluded** from limitation calculation
- Applies to suits, appeals, applications, arbitration, condonation, etc.

**Example**:
- Decree passed: January 1, 2020
- Appeal limitation: 90 days (until April 1, 2020)
- COVID extension: 531 days excluded
- Effective deadline: **April 1, 2020 + 531 days** = September 14, 2021

**Post-COVID**: No further blanket extensions (as of December 2024)

---

## 6. ARBITRATION AND LIMITATION

### 6.1 Arbitration Act Exceptions

**Section 11 Application (Appointment of Arbitrator)**:
- NO limitation in Limitation Act 1963 (Section 43(3) Arbitration Act provides own rules)

**Section 34 Application (Set Aside Award)**:
- **3 months** from receipt of award (extendable 30 days) - Arbitration Act provision, NOT Limitation Act

**Section 9 Application (Interim Measures)**:
- Article 137 Limitation Act may apply (**3 years**) - debatable

**Commencement of Arbitration**:
- Filing request for arbitration (or appointing arbitrator) does NOT stop limitation for court suit (unless agreement excludes court jurisdiction)

---

## 7. EXCEPTIONS AND SPECIAL CASES

### 7.1 Suits NOT Barred by Limitation

**Section 25 - Suits for Possession**:
- No limitation for suit by sovereign to recover property (government property)

**Trust Property** (Article 65 Exception):
- No limitation against trustee for trust property while trust continues

**Permanent Injunction** (No specific article):
- Courts have held injunctions follow Article 58 or 113 (3 years)

### 7.2 Statutes Excluding Limitation Act

**Consumer Protection Act 2019**: 2-year limitation (Sections 69, 79) for complaints to consumer commissions (NOT Limitation Act 1963)

**Specific Relief Act Section 16(c)**: Readiness and willingness continuous requirement (regardless of limitation)

---

## 8. PRACTICAL GUIDANCE

### 8.1 Calculating Limitation - Checklist

**Step 1: Identify Nature of Claim**:
- Contract → Article 54, 55, 58, 113 (3 years)
- Property (possession) → Article 65 (12 years)
- Tort → Article 109 (3 years)
- Appeal → Article 116, 117 (90/30 days)

**Step 2: Determine When Cause of Action Arose**:
- Breach of contract → Date of breach
- Dispossession → Date of dispossession
- Fraud/mistake → Date of discovery

**Step 3: Apply Exclusions**:
- Section 14 (prior bona fide litigation) → Exclude time
- Section 17 (fraud/mistake) → From discovery date
- COVID-19 extension → Exclude March 15, 2020 - October 2, 2021 (531 days)

**Step 4: Check Extensions**:
- Section 18 (acknowledgment) → Fresh limitation from acknowledgment date
- Section 19 (part payment) → Fresh limitation from payment date

**Step 5: Compute Final Date**:
- Exclude first day (Section 12)
- Extend if last day is holiday

### 8.2 Avoiding Time-Bar

**For Plaintiffs/Appellants**:
1. **Diarize deadlines** (appeals: 90 days, specific performance: 3 years from performance date)
2. **File early** (do not wait until last day - unforeseen delays may occur)
3. **Acknowledgment/part payment**: Secure before limitation expires (extends period)
4. **Condonation application**: File with appeal/application if delayed (with detailed affidavit explaining sufficient cause)

**For Defendants**:
1. **Raise limitation** as preliminary objection
2. **Calculate from correct starting point** (cause of action date)
3. **Resist frivolous condonation applications** (no sufficient cause shown)

---

## 9. INTEGRATION WITH OTHER PROTOCOLS

**Related Protocols**:
- **IL-CONTRACT-007**: Breach of contract remedies (3-year limitation under Articles 54, 55)
- **IL-SRA-001**: Specific performance (Article 54 - 3 years)
- **IL-CPC-001**: Appeals (Article 116 - 90 days)
- **IL-ADR-004**: Arbitration applications (Section 34 - 3 months, own provision)
- **IL-PROPERTY-001**: Sale of property (Article 54 specific performance)

---

## LEGAL DISCLAIMERS

**NOT LEGAL ADVICE**: This protocol provides legal information about limitation periods under Indian law. It does NOT constitute legal advice for specific claims or limitation calculations.

**NO ATTORNEY-CLIENT RELATIONSHIP**: Use of this protocol does not create any attorney-client relationship.

**SEEK PROFESSIONAL CONSULTATION**: Limitation calculations involve complex factual and legal determinations. Errors in limitation can result in dismissal of otherwise valid claims. Always consult qualified legal practitioners for limitation analysis and filing strategies.

**CRITICAL TIME-SENSITIVE**: Limitation is **mandatory bar** under Section 3. Late filing results in dismissal. When in doubt about limitation expiry, consult counsel IMMEDIATELY.

---

## REVISION HISTORY
- **v1.0 (December 2024)**: Initial comprehensive protocol on Limitation Act 1963 fundamentals, key articles (54, 55, 65, 109, 116), extension/exclusion provisions, COVID-19 impact, practical guidance

---

**Word Count**: ~4,000 words
**Depth**: Comprehensive coverage of Limitation Act 1963
**Target Audience**: Legal practitioners, litigants, in-house counsel, law students


---
## IL-PROPERTY-001_sale_immovable_property.md
---

# IL-PROPERTY-001: Transfer of Property Act - Sale of Immovable Property

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-PROPERTY-001
- **Domain**: Indian Law - Property Law
- **Category**: Sale of Immovable Property
- **Version**: 1.0
- **Last Updated**: December 2024
- **Scope**: Transfer of Property Act 1882 Sections 54-55, sale contracts, registration requirements, specific performance
- **Prerequisites**: IL-CONTRACT-001 (Contract essentials)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-SRA-001 (Specific performance), IL-PROPERTY-002 (Mortgage), IL-CONTRACT-007 (Breach remedies)

## Purpose
Comprehensive coverage of sale of immovable property under Transfer of Property Act (TPA) 1882, including definition, essentials, contract vs. conveyance, registration requirements under Registration Act 1908, rights and liabilities, and remedies for breach including specific performance post-2018 SRA Amendment.

---

## 1. LEGISLATIVE FRAMEWORK

### 1.1 Transfer of Property Act 1882

**Scope**: Governs transfer of property in India (except where local law applies)

**Key Provisions**:
- **Sections 5-53**: General principles of transfer
- **Sections 54-55**: Sale
- **Sections 58-104**: Mortgage
- **Sections 105-117**: Leases
- **Sections 118-129**: Exchange, Gift, Actionable Claims

**Interplay with Registration Act 1908**:
- TPA defines **what** transfers require registration
- Registration Act prescribes **how** registration occurs

### 1.2 Constitutional Basis

**Entry 6 List III (Concurrent List)**: Transfer of property other than agricultural land

**Entry 18 List II (State List)**: Land (rights, ownership, alienation)

**Effect**: State laws may vary (agricultural land, stamp duty)

---

## 2. DEFINITION OF SALE (SECTION 54)

### 2.1 Section 54 - "Sale"

**"Sale"** is a transfer of ownership in exchange for **price paid or promised** or **part-paid and part-promised**.

**Essential Elements**:
1. **Transfer of ownership** (not mere possession)
2. **Immovable property** (land, buildings, hereditary allowances, rights in land)
3. **Consideration** = **price** (money, not kind)
4. **Present transfer** (immediate transfer of ownership, not agreement to transfer in future)

### 2.2 Sale vs. Agreement to Sell

**Sale** (Section 54):
- **Ownership transfers immediately**
- Creates **transfer of property**
- If immovable ≥₹100 → **Registered document** required (Section 54 TPA + Section 17 Registration Act)

**Agreement to Sell** (Contract):
- **Promise to transfer ownership in future** (on fulfillment of conditions or at future date)
- Creates **contractual rights** (not property transfer)
- **NOT required to be registered** (unless party seeks specific performance under Section 17(1-A) Registration Act)

**Key Distinction**:
- Sale → "I transfer ownership to you" → Registered deed
- Agreement to Sell → "I agree to transfer ownership upon payment/at later date" → Contract (may be unregistered)

**Example**:
- Buyer and seller execute **agreement to sell** (January 1, 2024): "Seller agrees to sell property for ₹50 lakhs, sale to complete on March 1, 2024"
  - January 1: Agreement to sell (contract), NO ownership transfer
  - March 1: **Sale deed** executed and registered → Ownership transfers

---

## 3. REGISTRATION REQUIREMENTS

### 3.1 Section 54 TPA - Registration Mandate

**Section 54**: Sale of immovable property of value ₹**100 or upwards** can be made **ONLY by registered instrument**.

**Exception**: Lease for term not exceeding 1 year OR rent not exceeding ₹100/year (oral/unregistered valid)

**Effect of Non-Registration**:
- Unregistered sale deed (value ≥₹100) → **VOID** (Section 49 Registration Act)
- Does NOT transfer ownership
- Creates NO legal title

**Example**:
- Seller executes sale deed (unregistered) for ₹50 lakhs property
- Buyer cannot claim ownership
- At best, creates **agreement to sell** (if otherwise valid contract) → Buyer may seek specific performance (to compel registration)

### 3.2 Registration Act 1908 - Section 17

**Section 17(1)(b)**: Non-testamentary instruments which purport or operate to create, declare, assign, limit or extinguish any right, title or interest of value ₹100 or more in immovable property **MUST be registered**.

**Section 17(1-A)**: Documents required to be registered under Section 17(1) can only be received as evidence of contract in suit for specific performance if **registered**.

**Implication**:
- Unregistered agreement to sell (value ≥₹100) → NOT admissible as evidence unless registered
- Buyer seeking specific performance must first get agreement registered

**Practical Effect** (*Janak Raj v. Gurdial Singh* 2001 SC):
- Courts require registration of agreement to sell (≥₹100) for specific performance suits
- Prevents use of unregistered agreements as proof of contract

### 3.3 Registration Procedure

**Parties**:
- **Executant** (seller) must appear before Sub-Registrar
- **Claimant** (buyer) should appear (or authorized representative)

**Documents**:
- Sale deed (minimum 2 copies + 1 for registration office)
- Identity proof (Aadhar, PAN)
- Property documents (title deeds, encumbrance certificate)
- Stamp duty payment receipt
- Registration fee

**Stamp Duty**:
- State-specific (varies 4-10% of property value)
- Payable before registration
- Non-stamped document inadmissible (Section 35 Stamp Act 1899)

**Attestation**:
- **2 witnesses** required (independent, not parties to transaction)

**Effect of Registration** (Section 47 Registration Act):
- Registered document operates from **date of execution** (not registration date)
- BUT legal title recognized only after registration

---

## 4. TRANSFER OF OWNERSHIP

### 4.1 When Title Passes

**General Rule (Section 54)**: Ownership transfers on **registration** of sale deed

**NOT**:
- ❌ On execution of sale deed (if unregistered)
- ❌ On payment of consideration (if sale deed not registered)
- ❌ On delivery of possession (if sale deed not registered)

**Exception - Doctrine of Part Performance** (Section 53-A TPA):
- Transferee who has taken **possession** under contract and performed part of obligation (paid part price) → Protected against seller
- Seller cannot claim property back (estoppel)
- BUT does NOT transfer title (only protects possession)

### 4.2 Rights and Liabilities (Section 55)

**Seller's Duties** (Section 55(1)):
1. **Disclose material defects** in property/title known to seller
2. **Produce title documents** for inspection
3. **Execute deed** and get registered
4. **Deliver possession** on payment (unless agreed otherwise)
5. **Pay government revenue** (property tax) up to sale date

**Seller's Rights** (Section 55(1)):
1. **Keep title documents** (unless agreed to deliver)
2. **Retain property** until full payment (unless credit agreed)

**Buyer's Duties** (Section 55(4)):
1. **Disclose defects in title** discovered after contract (known to buyer, not seller)
2. **Pay/tender price** at time/place agreed
3. **Bear registration/stamp duty costs** (unless agreed otherwise)

**Buyer's Rights** (Section 55(4)):
1. **Receive title documents** (if agreed)
2. **Possession** on payment
3. **Benefits** from property (rents, crops) from registration date

**Liability for Hidden Defects** (*Kanaiyalal v. Dineshchandra* 1961 SC):
- Seller liable for defects in title **known** to seller but not disclosed
- NOT liable for defects **equally unknown** to both parties (caveat emptor - buyer beware)

---

## 5. SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE REMEDY

### 5.1 Post-2018 SRA Amendment - Mandatory Specific Performance

**Pre-2018**: Specific performance **discretionary** (court may refuse even if contract valid)

**Post-2018** (Section 10 SRA as amended):
Specific performance **SHALL be enforced** (mandatory) UNLESS exceptions (Sections 11, 14, 16, 20)

**For Sale of Immovable Property**:
- Valid agreement to sell → Buyer entitled to specific performance (compel seller to execute/register sale deed) **as of right**
- Burden on seller to prove exceptions

**Exceptions** (Section 11 SRA):
- Contract vague, uncertain
- Performance impossible
- Contract depends on personal qualifications (not applicable to property sale)
- Damages adequate remedy (rare for property)

**Readiness and Willingness** (Section 16(c) SRA):
- Buyer must be **ready and willing** to perform (pay balance price)
- Continuous requirement (from contract → filing → trial)

**See IL-SRA-001 for detailed specific performance analysis**

### 5.2 Alternative Remedies

**If specific performance not granted**:
1. **Damages** (Section 73 ICA): Compensation for loss
2. **Rescission** (Section 27 SRA): Cancel contract, restore parties to original position
3. **Return of earnest money/advance** with interest

---

## 6. TIME IS OF THE ESSENCE (SECTION 55)

### 6.1 When Time is Essence

**Section 55(3)**: Time is **NOT of essence** in contract for sale of immovable property (unless parties expressly agree otherwise)

**Effect**:
- Delay in performance does NOT automatically terminate contract
- Party can still claim specific performance (even if delayed)

**Exception - Express Clause**:
"Time is of the essence" clause in contract → Strict deadline
- Failure to perform by deadline → Breach
- Other party may terminate and claim damages

**Example**:
- Agreement to sell: "Sale to complete by March 31, 2024. **Time is of essence**."
- If seller fails to complete sale by March 31 → Buyer may:
  - Terminate contract AND claim damages (loss of bargain), OR
  - Still seek specific performance (if willing to waive strict time)

### 6.2 Delay and Condonation

**Reasonable Time**:
If time not specified → Performance within **reasonable time**

**Factors for Reasonable Time**:
- Nature of property (complexity of title verification)
- Market conditions
- Financing requirements

**Notice to Complete** (*Saradamani v. Radhakanta* 1928 PC):
- Party may serve notice to complete within specified time (making time of essence)
- If other party fails → Contract terminable

---

## 7. DOCTRINE OF PART PERFORMANCE (SECTION 53-A)

### 7.1 Protection of Transferee

**Section 53-A**: Where transferee has, in part performance of contract:
1. Taken **possession** of property, OR
2. Performed **part of obligations** (paid part price, made improvements)

**Effect**:
- Transferor **cannot enforce against transferee** any right inconsistent with contract
- Transferee protected in **possession** (seller cannot evict)

**Requirements**:
1. **Written contract** (signed by transferor or authorized agent)
2. Transferee **performed part** (possession + payment/improvements)
3. Performance in **good faith**

**Limitation**:
- Does NOT transfer title (only protects possession)
- Transferee must still seek specific performance to obtain title

**Example**:
- Agreement to sell signed, unregistered
- Buyer paid 50% price, took possession, made renovations
- Seller attempts to sell to third party → Buyer protected under Section 53-A (seller cannot evict or sell to another)
- Buyer should file specific performance suit to compel registration

---

## 8. FIXTURES AND IMPROVEMENTS

### 8.1 Fixtures (Section 3 TPA)

**"Attached to Earth"**: Property attached to earth or permanently fastened to anything attached to earth

**Examples of Fixtures**:
- Buildings, trees, shrubs
- Machinery embedded in earth (factory machinery on foundation)
- Doors, windows (if permanently affixed)

**Transfer of Fixtures**:
- Sale of land includes fixtures (unless excluded)
- Buyer entitled to fixtures existing at time of sale

### 8.2 Improvements by Buyer in Possession

**Before Registration**:
- Buyer in possession under part performance (Section 53-A)
- Makes improvements (construction, repairs)

**Effect**:
- Buyer entitled to reimbursement (if seller defaults and specific performance not decreed)
- Improvements belong to landowner (seller) but buyer has lien for value

---

## 9. PRACTICAL GUIDANCE

### 9.1 Due Diligence Checklist

**Before Entering Agreement to Sell**:
1. **Title Verification**:
   - Chain of title documents (past 30 years minimum)
   - Encumbrance Certificate (last 13-30 years)
   - Tax receipts (property tax paid up to date)
2. **Physical Inspection**:
   - Site visit, boundary verification
   - Possession status (vacant or occupied)
3. **Statutory Approvals**:
   - Building plan approval (if constructed)
   - Occupancy Certificate (OC)
   - Commencement Certificate (CC)
4. **Encumbrances**:
   - No mortgages/liens (or to be discharged at sale)
   - No pending litigation
5. **Seller's Authority**:
   - Power of attorney (if seller represented)
   - Seller's identity verification

### 9.2 Agreement to Sell - Essential Clauses

**Must Include**:
1. **Parties**: Full names, addresses, identification
2. **Property description**: Survey number, boundaries, area
3. **Consideration**: Total price, payment schedule
4. **Timeline**: Date for sale completion
5. **Possession**: When delivered (on sale or earlier)
6. **Title warranty**: Seller warrants clear, marketable title
7. **Defects disclosure**: Known defects in property/title
8. **Specific performance clause**: Parties may seek specific performance
9. **Time is essence**: If strict deadline required
10. **Registration**: Parties agree to execute and register sale deed

### 9.3 Common Pitfalls

**Unregistered Sale Deeds** (≥₹100):
- Void, no title transfer
- Cannot be used as evidence (Section 49 Registration Act)

**Unregistered Agreements to Sell** (≥₹100):
- Valid as contract
- BUT inadmissible for specific performance (Section 17(1-A) Registration Act)
- Must be registered to use as evidence

**Inadequate Due Diligence**:
- Hidden encumbrances (mortgages, liens)
- Forged documents, impersonation
- Litigation pending

**Possession Without Registration**:
- Part performance protection (Section 53-A) but NO title
- Must file specific performance to secure title
